Build 0.7.23 · Save format 29 · 1 October 2026

CityBuilderSim

A macroeconomic city simulator: an age pyramid that decides the workforce, a labour market that prices it and in which every worker takes the best-paid post they qualify for, builders who share their crews by what each site can use and are paid at today's wages, with the player's hand on their queue to reorder it, rush it on overtime, cancel an order or pull a building down, fifteen private sectors on one template trading real goods on real markets — two of them selling to the world, one of them to the mills, one hauling the others' freight and one building the cars the households drive — and raising their money with shares and bonds that trade on order books, a bank that is a business, pricing a loan from what it costs, keeping its own capital and passing to the city when it fails, a central bank whose balance sheet is the only place money is made, whose dial is the price of it and whose holdings can reach every bond the city sells, a city founded with a name and a money of its own and made to borrow for what it builds, households that each keep their own books including the people the books used to leave out, illness that kills, crime that has reasons, households that eat thirteen separate foods and choose between them by income, own cars and borrow for them, and spend what is left over a counter, a road that carries three kinds of traffic and can be relieved but never deleted, a trade band that is three quarters freight, a currency with a balance of payments behind it, a vault that spends to defend it and a real rate that moves it, and a history the player can drag, zoom and read their own decisions on. This is what it currently has and does.

01

The month

Everything in the game happens in this order, and most of the hard bugs in its history have been a line that made it into one phase and not the other. The order is genuinely a sequence, so it is numbered — and since 18 September it is also generated: docs/month-order.md lists every top-level statement of Game.nextMonth() and SimulationEngine.simulateMonth() with its line, which is what this list is struck against.

  1. 1
    What falls due is rolled, before the calendar turns
    In the gap between two presses, where a player's own issue lands, so that it settles to its buyers and crosses the audit window exactly as a hand-made issue does. In a December the city's fund takes its dial's share of the year's surplus first (fundYearEnd()), so that no surplus is counted twice; then the rollover reads the principal that falls due next month, nets it against whatever of last year's surplus is still unused, and issues the rest, sized to the cash it has to raise (rollMaturities(), 0.7.13), and strikes how much of the central bank's own maturing paper it will take again at issue. Then the month turns, and as it turns the buildings the player ordered demolished between the presses close (closeDemolished(), 0.7.22), before anything in the month reads the city: their posts, homes and places are gone from its first count. No money moves there; it moved when the order was placed.
  2. 2
    The central bank settles with the treasury, and the promises are paid
    Before anything is priced, because the advances and the remittance are money made and destroyed and have to fall inside the audit's window. With the autopilot on — and a new game founds on it since 0.7.13 — the Taylor rule takes the dial, aimed at the player's target. Then the treasury pays the interest on last month's advances, takes last month's profit back as the remittance, repays its central bank out of any cash above zero, pays down arrears, and is advanced whatever it is still short (settleTreasury()). Then the central bank takes its own maturing par again, as an add-on to what the city sold between the presses (rollCentralBankAtIssue(), 0.7.15), and the holdings dial trades the city's term paper toward its target (openMarketOperation()), so this month's quotes carry both. Then the students' grant and EI are paid on the people the month opens with, in the month they are credited, and the bank pays its profit tax.
  3. 3
    The world turns and the land office re-prices
    World prices advance, the exchange rate is taken, the cost of living goes into the labour market, and the land office strikes its ground in US dollars and what businesses pay for it in the city's own money. What the city pays outside and what businesses pay inside are both inputs to everything below.
  4. 4
    Business credit is priced, interest billed, property tax charged
    What its money costs the bank goes to the city's floor first, and a failed bank lends nothing. Then the bank's capital rule is read once and handed to every desk that lends this month, the businesses' and the families', its bonds and its book's concentration re-read before it; every business's spread is set on the bank's prime at this month's dial, the landlords' insured mortgages at the insured rate beside it; and the businesses' bond coupons are struck on the same face the interest bills are, with property tax charged beside them. Before the statements run, so the figures each sector banks are net of it.
  5. 5
    Every building pays to stand, and every shipper pays its freight
    1% of build cost a year, charged to every owner in cash and materials and construction points — placed as a real order with the builders, not a line item. Since 0.7.19 the cash is the building's cash cost with its labour at today's builders' wages, and the bill carries the builders' sales tax, which a business that makes taxable supplies claims back and the landlords and the bank bear; the city's comes back to it, since the city collects it. A forty-year stock at 1% a year is about 40% of the city's whole construction output. Then the railway's invoice on the month's trade, for the same reason: an expense on fourteen sets of books and revenue on the fifteenth.
  6. 6
    Fifteen income statements are struck
    Retail, real estate, industry, construction, heavy industry, mining, materials, business services, manufacturing, agriculture, food processing, rail, automotive, luxury retail, restaurants — each at the utilisation the month was traded at, not at today's, and each from the same template. The VAT is settled from the same figures between the two halves, and since 0.7.19 the sales tax a business claims back on the buildings it bought reaches its till as cash back on them.
  7. 7
    National accounts, then the household books
    Household spending is consumption, read from the other end, so the people cannot be shown paying a figure the shops were not shown receiving. Consumption is three counters now — the grocers', the boutiques' and the kitchens' — plus the rent. The households' month strikes the bank's account fees, and since 0.7.19 its customers with them, which the branch rule in step 9 reads.
  8. 8
    The households buy cars, then spend the rest over a counter
    With the month's money fresh and before the investor can see it: the second-hand market clears first, because nobody pays forty for a car they can have for twelve, then the showroom; then the boutiques, and the kitchens right after, because they are the same kind of purchase out of the same surplus. The cars, the pieces and the meals count as demand for next month's strike.
  9. 9
    The private sector takes its turn
    Loans advance; a bond that falls due is paid from its issuer's till to its holders; matured principal comes back, and a till left short calls its dollars home, sells the other sectors' bonds it holds and goes to the shortfall desk, which lends or sells a bond for it, and what it still cannot pay defaults, a slice the size of the part it cannot pay. Plant is retired and sold to the builders for its material, the bank's branches that this month's account fees no longer cover at last month's cost close (0.7.19), the month's defaults are written off against the bank, the insured part paid by the treasury, and each sector forecasts its own signal six months out, orders only what the city could staff (0.7.18), and funds what it plans in a fixed order: sell shares, spend the till, call money home from abroad, and only then borrow — a bond where one is no dearer than the loan, the bank for the rest, and a landlord's homes on an insured mortgage.
  10. 10
    Construction advances; roads recount; the builders strike their crews, the posts are counted and the labour market reprices
    The sites take the month's points by the crew each building can use (0.7.17), and once the player has a hand on the queue the city's share goes to its own sites in the player's order and a rushed site works its month on overtime (0.7.22; see the builders, section 9). The builders buy the material that work drew on and recognise revenue on the same figure the sites advanced by; each owner then pays what the month's draw cost past what its quote allowed for it, or is refunded (0.7.19), and the hand's month is settled after it — the overtime's premium paid and paid out as wages, a cancelled order's refund, a finished demolition's material and ground (0.7.22). A shop that opens this morning trades this month and puts its trucks on the road this month — roads are recounted before anything reads them. Then the builders strike the share of their posts the work ahead needs and lay the rest off (0.7.17), and only then are the posts recounted and wages walk toward what scarcity says per band, and per licence, against this month's posts and before anybody ages.
  11. 11
    Demographics
    Age; give birth; die of three separate causes — the band's own rate, illness, and violence; migrate, never more than the room the placement has left at the doors standing today (0.7.17); rebuild the households against last month's; commit and record this month's crime; settle health, education, the police and the transit fare; advance the ring of the sick.
  12. 12
    The economy and the services sync
    Population, wages, jobs, the fill rate and the utilisation ratios reach every sector — the road ratio per sector, because only a tram can make two businesses on the same street face different jams, and the health ratio the demographics step has just set.
  13. 13
    Cash moves, every market clears, ore is lifted, the paper is paid, the government's books are re-struck
    The utility books what its customers were charged; every market clears and every maker produces in one pass; the mines ask the ground. The paper sold between the presses settles to its holders, the households taking their share first, and the month's coupons and principal are paid holder by holder. The books are re-struck at the bottom of the month, against the same period's spending and after the coupon is known, because the start-of-month call mixed two months' tax fields.
  14. 14
    The bank settles, the owners are paid, the shares and the bonds trade
    The bank pays for whatever paper the households did not take and earns a month of the discount on what it holds; then lending and its fees, repayments, the interest by who paid it and its bonds' coupons, what the month asks of its allowance, its staff and its branches — the charter exempt from the operating cost since 0.7.19 — the carry trade, the window and the interest on its reserves at the central bank, and, if it has failed and the city's setting is automatic, its resolution for its shares that month; then the cost of funds is struck once and final, the central bank strikes its month's profit, the savers are paid, then dividends, then the shares trade, then the city's preferred falls due at its anniversaries, then the bonds trade, then whatever is still idle goes abroad, the households' by the same rule as the sectors', and a household whose paper no longer out-yields its deposits sells a little of it back. What lands after the bank's close can still fail it, so it is looked at again, and a bank under its minimum asks the city for preferred.
  15. 15
    The currency moves, and the money is counted
    MoneyAudit strikes pooled after − pooled before = inflows − outflows across every pool in the city and every flow across its border. A cent of residual is a finding. The hot money moves just before the strike, because it moves a pool; then the balance of payments, the currency and the price index, each reading a month that has finished settling. The currency reads the real rate against the world's, and on a month it is pushed weaker on a deficit the central bank sells the vault's dollars against the push — a capital transaction booked after the audit, because no pool moves on it. Nothing after the audit may move a pool. Every twelfth month autosaves, also before any skip and on quit.

What moved since the last edition. Seven statements went in, at set places, and the order of the rest held. Two in 0.7.17: the builders strike the crews their work needs before the labour market counts the posts (strikeBuildersCrews()), so the posts it fills are the posts they offer, and migration is handed the doors standing that month (setDoors()), because arrivals are capped at the room the placement has left. Two in 0.7.19: each owner settles the escalation on the material its month's work drew right after the work is recognised (settleSiteContracts()), and a business's tax back on its buildings reaches its till after the statements. Three in 0.7.22: as the calendar turns the buildings the player ordered demolished close (closeDemolished()), the overtime the rushed sites worked is read beside the site output before the work is recognised, and after the escalation the player's hand on the queue is settled (settleConstructionControl()). Inside statements that were already there, 0.7.19 has the households' month strike the bank's customers beside its fees, the private sector's turn close the branches those fees no longer cover before it plans, and the bank pay the branches standing with the charter exempt from their operating cost. 0.7.16, 0.7.18, 0.7.20, 0.7.21 and 0.7.23 moved no statement: the generated list is the same with its line numbers stripped, and the decision log of 0.7.23 is written where each policy is applied, not in the month. The edition before this one had put the rollover and the fund's December share above the old first step (0.7.13, 0.7.14), the central bank's maturing par after the treasury settles (0.7.15), the bank's capital rule and its prices at the top of the month and its provision and its second look at the bottom (0.7.7, 0.7.8, 0.7.11, 0.7.12), and the resolution and the preferred after the shares trade (0.7.14).

Standing rule

A flow cannot be reconstructed from the state a month ended in. That is why the save carries income statements, the VAT ledger, the inventory baseline in units, wage history, loss streaks, plots consumed, the unburied backlog, last month's household shapes, the ring of the long sick, six months of prisoners, the railway's quote and what it carried, every sector's fleet and the memory of the commute, the treasury's journal for the month that ended and the one in progress, what the vault sold defending the currency, the bank's running costs and funding blend as it last priced them and the profit it booked after its close, a year of its month statements and of its balance sheets, its allowance and its worst year, every borrower's last quarter, the ledger of the surplus the rollover has netted, the four inputs the debt market's rate was struck from, the crews the builders kept on and the fill they were struck on, each order's contract on its site with the material its quote allowed for, the player's hand on the queue — the city's order of its own sites, the rushes and their months on overtime, the cancels waiting, the stopped shells, the demolitions and the buy-outs, with the overtime wages the next statement charges — and the player's own decisions — none of them derivable by looking at the city.

02

People

The age pyramid is the population. It used to be a display beside a formula; now nothing else decides how many people live here — and since the long sick and the crime batch, nothing dies of only one thing.

Six ages, three ways to die

The band's own annual mortality. Monthly rates are compounded, not divided by twelve.
BandAgesAnnual mortalityNote
Babies0–50.10%almost all of it in the first year
Children6–120.0075%the safest band there is
Teens13–170.02%accidents
Adults18–690.225%averaged over a fifty-two year span — and the entire workforce
Seniors70–842.90%population-weighted from a life table
Elders85–11912.77%plus ageing out at 120

The seniors were one band of fifty years until save format 27, at a flat 4.5%. Senior care asks for a place for everybody over seventy, and in the real world about one in nine of them is in one — 2% of seventy-four-year-olds against 29.6% of the over-85s — and one band cannot hold a fifteenfold gradient, nor a mortality curve that runs from 1.4% a year at seventy to over 30% at ninety-five. The two figures are population-weighted from Canada's 2019 life table, and the split is very nearly aggregate-preserving: total deaths among the over-seventies went from 6.5% a year to about 6.7%, and a seventy-year-old has 14.9 years left against the 15.4 the single band gave. The over-85s keep two household shapes of their own, because Elder living alone is the cell in trouble first when senior care is short.

Children, teens and adults were halved when illness started killing people, so that the new cause of death would be a redistribution rather than a surcharge. A month's deaths are now struck from three rates against the same opening balance — the band's own rate (scaled by care, and by who has no home), illness from the ring of the long sick, and violence from the month's homicides — capped together at 5% a month, with the cap scaling each part alike so the shares survive it. Every one of them is recorded by band, so City History can say what the city has died of since it was founded.

Bands drain exponentially rather than on a fixed schedule, so a baby boom smears into a bulge rather than travelling through the city as a wave. Nobody is tracked individually; the bucket only has a mean.

Migration

The city moves toward a target rather than snapping to one. Housing and jobs say how attractive the city is, not how many people are in it.

target = (0.75 × jobs × residentsPerJob + 0.25 × housingCapacity) × seniorCarePull × rentPull × crimePull residentsPerJob = (1 + 0.125) / adultShare // 12.5% labour slack arrivals = min(room, max(0, target − population) × 0.15 × crowding) // room: what the placement has left, since 0.7.17 departures = population × decliningPressure × 0.05

Gross flows, not net, so the screen can say why the number moved. Crowding interpolates to zero between comfortable households and the point where every flatshare and doubled-up home has already formed — a full city still takes arrivals, just fewer. Since 0.7.17 it reads the households that have a door of their own, so a doubled-up household counts as crowding, Jerus's rule: his year-149 city had doors enough by the count, read 0.63, and had 143,443 households living in somebody else's home because the doors were the wrong size. And a month's arrivals are capped at the room the placement has left (FamilyModel.roomLeft()): the free doors by size and what the doubling valve still has, in households of the arrivals' own mix. It replaced a wall that stopped every arrival while a single household was left with nowhere, and then let a month of 25,000 to 32,000 in at once; in his city, run on for 120 months, arrivals came in 115 months where they had come in 18 (0.7.17). Nobody leaves unless a pay tier's real pay has been declining twelve months straight or has stopped entirely. Four more pushes: a band whose wage is pinned at the minimum and still has more workers than posts sheds 2% of the surplus a month, bankrupt households leave, rent above the target burden deters arrivals, and crime deters arrivals — calibrated off Cullen & Levitt (1999), a tenth of the city's draw gone at twice Canada's crime rate, almost all of it as people not coming rather than people leaving. Arrivals carry a skill mix bought by the wage: at the going rate every arrival has a high-school diploma and nothing more, and every band above it, and since 0.7.18 the band below it, comes only for a premium over the going rate, out of a world that has few to spare. At every band's ceiling 7.5% of a month's arrivals have no diploma, the 2021 Census share of immigrants who landed in 2016–2021 aged 25–54 with no certificate (Ontario's figure, as the Financial Accountability Office of Ontario tabulated it; WageBand.NONE's ceiling, 0.1192). Jerus chose "Some unskilled migrants": "Allow arrivals without a diploma when the unskilled wage is high." In his year-149 city, run on for 120 months, 13,168 came (0.7.18).

Households, pay, and what they remember

Fifteen household shapes, from SENIOR_ALONE and the over-85s' ELDER_ALONE through COUPLE_TWO_CHILDREN to SHARED_ADULTS. Families are built from the adults who work; everyone else has their own books. The mix follows the age pyramid and the job tiers; when homes run short, singles form flatshares first and families double up last.

The households remember. The builder keeps last month's households as far as the people still exist for them — 1% of them re-form each month by design — shrinking each shape by the worst shortage among the bands it needs, rebuilding only the remainder, and refitting tiers to the jobs by iterative proportional fitting. Before it, a city redrew every family from scratch every month and wallets moved with the redraw. Eight seeds: orphans at the end 490–633 → 11–19, hunger 4% → 0%, unemployment 14.8% → 12.2%. Everything a cell holds moves with the people when the shapes re-form — savings, debt, shares, the dollars abroad, the student loan, and since 16 September the cars — and the move is clamped so that a pool cannot hand out more than it holds, because the day it could, four retired cells owed minus 2.7e-29 dollars and the sign of that decided the exchange rate (see how it's checked).

Homes have sizes. A studio takes two, a low-rise flat four, a house six; a household has to fit, largest families into the smallest unit that fits, and a one- or two-person flat refuses a dependant outright. Rent is two markets — see prices. An empty home now costs its owner something to hold, and so does a full one: every building pays 1% of its cost a year to stand.

Six pay tiers, eleven job types — base wages on NS Job Bank medians
TierMonthly
Unskilled$3,460
Skilled$4,500
College$5,500
Professional$8,200
Senior professional$9,600
Elite$15,600
Seventy-eight ledgers

Eleven working shapes × six pay tiers, four retired rows, three kinds of out-of-work, students, orphans by age band, and the prisoners. Each keeps savings, debt, a lockout clock, shares in each of the sixteen listed companies, dollars held abroad, student debt, its cars, the month's investment income, the dinners it ate out, the share of its people who could pay for care, the city's own paper and, since 0.7.12, the businesses' bonds — thirty-two slots a cell in the save, each keyed by name so a new row never shifts an old one, and each new slot appended to the end so that an older save reads as a city that had none of it, which it did not. Since 0.7.12 each cell also keeps its bonds one by one, and trades its shares and bonds with the other cells as a participant of its own, saved by the cell's name.

Since 0.7.2 a cell whose census count falls under half a household (HouseholdBalance.EMPTY_CELL) is empty: it keeps its count, holds nothing and is paid nothing, and every stock it held folds to its own row first and then to the city — because a cell of 1e-15 households was still being paid, and one line divided a credit by it.

The household books, and a balance sheet

Every cell shows wages, the banded wage tax, pension contributions and pensions, the health premium, rent, tuition, interest, shopping, healthcare fees, the transit fare, the bank's account fee, and what is left — plus savings, debt, shares, the city's paper, the businesses' bonds and cars per household, and a waterfall: payslip, rent, fees, food; short, and they spend savings, then sell the city's paper back to the bank's desk, then bring their paper home from abroad, then sell their bonds, then offer their shares on the book at the price at which the dividend they give up yields what borrowing would cost them, then borrow from the bank — since 0.7.7 at a household loan's four costs plus a spread that climbs with what they already owe, never past 36%, and since 0.7.8 only as far as the bank's capital lets it lend — and only then eat less, which makes them ill. A row six months of income in debt that still cannot cover subsistence goes bankrupt: 4% a month discharged, locked out of credit for a year, a quarter of them leaving the city. A row that can be evicted and still cannot pay loses the share of its homes the shortfall covers.

Spending follows income, and since 17 September it follows wealth too. A household plans to spend 80% of what is left above subsistence at the grocer's, so shopping is a budget rather than a headcount; on top of that it spends 0.33% of its net worth a month — four percent a year, the middle of the measured propensity to consume out of wealth — and puts half of the income it does not spend on food over a luxury counter and a quarter of it on a table. Dividends and the coupon on its paper abroad are paid home and enter the plan, and so, since 0.7.1, are the coupons on the city's own paper, paid into savings as untaxed investment income, and since 0.7.12 those on the businesses' bonds. A household with no debt, no lockout and more than three months of disposable income in the bank puts 30% of the excess into shares, and sends the rest of its idle savings abroad on the same rule the sectors use. It also takes the city's paper at issue: at the settle the households go first, taking a fifth of an issue for every point it yields over the deposit rate (HOUSEHOLD_PAPER_APPETITE) and never more than half (MAX_HOUSEHOLD_PAPER_SHARE), out of savings past the cushion, and the bank buys the rest. Pensions are pay-as-you-go, and both the contribution rate and the replacement rate are Policy dials. Contributions follow workers and pensions follow pensioners, so an ageing city buys a structural deficit without changing a single policy.

And since 0.7.3 saving answers the real rate. Everything a household plans above a basket a head — the propensity's share, the wealth term, and the surplus the counter and the table spend out of — is multiplied by one factor a month struck on the real deposit rate, the bank's deposit rate less the year's inflation: ten points of real return cut it by a tenth, ten points of negative real return raise it by a tenth, and it is held between a half and one and a half. Subsistence never moves, and at a factor of one the plan is the old plan to the bit. On the default seed on 22 September savers ended the run earning 3.63% real and the households planned 0.964 of what they would spend above a basket at zero; over 4,005 months the factor ran from 0.835 to 1.131, median 0.993. On 28 September, with a deposit rate the bank now chooses (see the bank), savers ended the run earning 0.23% real and the households planned 0.998; over 4,002 months the factor ran from 0.921 to 1.060, median 1.000. On 1 October, at 0.7.23, they ended at 0.22% real and planned 0.998 again, and over 4,005 months the factor ran from 0.853 to 1.105, median 1.000. What it does to prices is almost nothing, and why is in the central bank.

Why the households were rich

Household net worth ran at 45 months of the city's GDP at month 480 — dead on the real world's sixty to eighty-four — and 5,072 by month 3,840. It did not sit at a wrong level; it diverged. Over one run the foreign coupons came to $2,649bn against $584bn of every wage the city ever paid, because the households kept about 79% of their wealth abroad at the world's 2% and the coupon was rolled where it was earned, so wealth compounded at 1.58% a year mechanically, 193× over 333 years. Three changes were ensembled one at a time: paying the coupon home took the ratio from 5,380 months to 3,412; letting investment income into the plan put it back to 5,465, because investment income is a flow and a bigger city pays more of it; the wealth term took it to 2,737 and gave the best city of the four. A stock that compounds can only be bounded by a drain that reads the stock. And with the coefficient pushed to nine times the empirical range the stock still rose, while delivery fell from 76% of plans to 57% — the households are not hoarding because they are misers; they are hoarding because there is nothing to buy. The cars, the boutiques and the kitchens are the answer to that, and net worth now oscillates (432 → 137 → 128 → 214 months of GDP) instead of climbing.

What a household owns, and borrows for

A car is a household stock, beside savings, debt, shares and the dollars abroad — it follows the people, it is saved per cell by name, a household that leaves takes it — and it is the first one that is not money, so a currency reform does not touch it. It is the first durable a household in this game has ever been able to own: the measurement that decided the shape found an unskilled couple banking $3,160 of a $4,880 month and sitting on $799,100, a hundred and sixty-four months of income, with $17.6bn of household savings in the city against zero household debt. That is not a rich city, it is a city with nothing to buy. So affordability barely binds in a mature city and binds hard in a young one, and the constraint is time: 2% of the households that have never owned one buy a car in a month, a car lasts 180 months and is replaced at once when it dies, one per household is saturation, and a cell buys whole cars — because a money ratio that comes out as a physical quantity has to cross at a grain coarser than the dust, or a reformed city wants an ulp more of a car and the two cities' trade balances part company. A city whose transit could carry everybody tops out at half a car per household, for ever; a metro built after the cars does not remove them, it stops them being replaced, and the fleet decays to the new ceiling over the life of a car.

And they borrow for it. Jerus: "make it so that citizens the car requirements are lowered and instead they finance with the bank." The rule is pay what you have and borrow the difference: a household hands over every dollar it has spare and a lender finds the rest, with a fifth of the price (CAR_DEPOSIT) as a minimum in cash and half the household's remaining credit room as the most a lender advances — that second number found by DenominationCheck, because every financed household was parked within a whisker of the bankruptcy line. With no credit line the rule reduces exactly to the old cash rule, so it can never refuse a purchase the old one allowed. Since 0.7.7 the bank's one per cent fee on new lending is added to the loan, because a household borrows when it is short of cash. The first draft — a fifth down, four fifths borrowed, compulsory — cut ownership at month 266 from 42% to 11%, because these households are cash-rich and income-poor and the loan moved the constraint onto the thing they have not. Seven of eight seeds are bit-identical over four thousand months; the young city diverges in month eight and has bought twice the cars by month ten. The loan is a bridge, not a note: the grocery waterfall repays it out of spare cash within a month or two. Household debt in this game had been exactly zero in every measured city. It is not any more.

The car goes last. Jerus: "if they are doing bad they cut back expenses, sell their cars or go for cheaper groceries." Two of the three were already in — the plan shrinks by itself, and a poorer cell already buys more grain and less meat — and the car was in nothing: a family sold its shares, brought its paper home, drew its last dollar of credit and went hungry with five months of take-home parked in the drive. There is a second-hand market at home now, and the fleet does not shrink when a household sells, it changes hands. The price is struck the way every market here strikes one: a floor at 15% of new (what a scrapper pays), a ceiling at 70%, a position between them from demand against supply — so a city cannot sell its way out of a general crash: everybody offers, nobody buys, the price is scrap, and the harness asserts it (8,000 offered, 0 sold, pinned at the floor). A household offers when the wage after the fixed bills no longer buys the food and half a year of that gap beats everything it has saved and can still borrow; a one-month test found households going bankrupt with the car still in the drive thirteen times for every one that sold it. Over 4,002 months of the default run when it shipped the trigger fired in two cell-months, both fractions of a household, and over 4,005 on 22 September in none, because the households were too rich to ever sell anything — which is the finding above, and the reason the counter and the kitchens exist. On 28 September the same seed offered 8,121 cars over 4,002 months and 362 of them found a buyer, at between 15% and 59% of a new car; on 1 October it offered 4,059 over 4,005 months and 149 found one, at between 15% and 32%.

03

Outside the families

Until this system the city's money was spread across every adult in a pay tier, working or not, so an unskilled single adult took home $1,262 in a month of 40% unemployment and nobody was ever poor. Four groups now keep their own books beside the families.

labour force = workforce − students − prisoners the pool = labour force − filled posts // an identity, not a second count
RowLives onWhat is true of them
On EI55% of the wage they lost, cappeda claim runs twelve months; the benefit is split among claimants out of a real premium
EI run outsavings, then creditthe only row that can be evicted; four in five of the pool end up here
Lost their homenothinghungry, and 3.7× as likely to be ill and to die
Studentssavings, a grant, and a loan, interest-free unless the player sets a rateout of the labour supply until they graduate; graduates repay over 114 months
Orphansnothing, and they pay no feesone row per age band; no grown-up, so no credit
Prisonersnothing; debts frozenout of the labour force, out of the housing match, out of every offender group

Employment insurance is a real programme. A 1.63% premium off insurable wages funds a 55% benefit capped at Canada's maximum insurable earnings scaled to the city's unskilled median — $1,903 a month for an unskilled claimant, $3,158 for anyone at or above the cap. Both rates are Policy dials. The ring is struck on the inflow of the newly unemployed, cohort by cohort: jobs lost this month join at the wage they lost, each cohort is thinned monthly by hires, deaths and departures, and the twelfth cohort falls off the end into "EI run out". Arrivals who never found work enter at the unskilled wage and get no claim. Since 0.7.17 the builders' idle crews join it too: a builder with less work than crews lays off the posts the work does not need, down to a core crew of a quarter of them, and those it lays off draw EI (the builders). Since 0.7.3 the benefit is paid in the month it is credited: struck again at the top of the month on the pool the month opens with and paid there (Game.payEiBenefits()), so the treasury, the ledger and the audit read one figure. For as long as EI had existed the out of work were credited last month's bill while the treasury paid this month's, and nothing saw it because the households are outside the audit's pools; OutsideCheck closes every Construction Depot in a small city and asserts that the closing month pays the EI of the pool it opened with and the month after pays the bill the closing struck, each credited the month it is paid; three of its lines fail on the old order. A save from 0.7.2 pays one month's EI twice on its first month, once.

Eviction is a level, not a rate. A row that can be evicted and is short loses households × min(1, shortfall / (rent + fees + interest)) of its homes; a quarter of those leave the city and the rest are on the street. Because a cell is an average, its households run out together — evictions arrive as a cliff. For most of the project's life nobody was ever evicted in the ensemble: an out-of-work adult ended a 333-year run holding millions, because the unskilled tier's savings are what they draw on first. It moves with the run. On the default seed on 18 September, with the wealth term and the counters in, 550 households were evicted over 4,002 months and 137 of them left; on 22 September, at 0.7.3, nobody was evicted over 4,005 months, nobody was left on EI at the end, and an out-of-work adult whose claim had run out ended the run with $303,200 put by; on 28 September, at 0.7.15, one household was evicted over 4,002 months and did not leave, 1,378 people were on EI at the end, and an out-of-work adult whose claim had run out ended with $80,600; and on 1 October, at 0.7.23, one was evicted over 4,005 months and did not leave, 4,746 people were on EI at the end, and an adult past EI ended with $235,700. Close the biggest employer in a young city and the path works exactly as designed: 549 evicted in the first year, 440 of them in two months, 384 on the street at the worst, 127 still there four years later (measured on 11 September; no harness reproduces it since).

Housing serves each group with its own valve: an out-of-work adult alone counts a full door, a flatsharer a fifth, a doubled-up seeker or someone on the street none — so the rent bill is split over the doors actually paid for. The transit fare is a third fee beside the health fee and the tuition, split across the rows the way the clinic's fees are: from the day transit was built until 16 September it was credited to the city and debited to nobody, and nobody saw it because no fixture had ever built a bus — see transport.

04

Health

Three classes. Health is the effect — a multiplier on output. Healthcare is the service — an employer with a payroll and a cemetery with a finite number of plots. Sickness is the ring of people who have not got better, and it is the one that kills.

Sickness is the fourth utilisation ratio

It sits beside energy, water and roads — and, since 17 September, beside the fifth ratio, the sector's own fleet of vans — multiplies with them, and is carried across a save the same way. The one difference is the whole point:

output = capacity × fill × bEnergy × bWater × bRoad × bHealth × bVans payroll = Σ wage[job type] × posts filled[job type] // no bHealth, no ratio at all; per job type since 0.7.17

Output falls, payroll does not. The employer carries it — a mill with fifty on the books pays fifty wages in a bad flu month and sells four weeks of output in three. The workforce, the pyramid and the wage bill never move. A sick worker still gets paid, still shops, still pays rent. Since 0.7.17 every employer pays each job type's wage on the posts of that type actually filled, and the households receive exactly that: it had been the whole schedule times the sector's average fill, which charged an empty post at the average of the posts beside it, and in Jerus's year-149 city the sectors paid $1,273M a month that no household received, the builders $269M of it. Jerus: "but sick still get paid right? if yes then good." They are: a sick worker holds a filled post.

General-care coverage sets the standing absence rate
CoverageOff sick
None18.0%
Half10.5%
Everybody3.0%

Five things add on top of that baseline and the total is capped at 45%: an outbreak, the unburied (up to 15 points), hunger (up to 8), the share of the city with no home at 3.7 times the baseline, and the injured — half a month off work for every violent crime.

Outbreaks land on top: roughly one every five years, peaking 8–25%, decaying 55% a month, with hospitals taking 60% off the peak but never preventing one. The roll is a pure function of the month number, so an epidemic survives a reload without the generator's state going in the save — you cannot save-scum out of one. Measured over 4,005 months on the default seed, 22 September: 52 outbreaks, the city ill for 289 months, worst month 45% — which is the cap; and the run ends at 15.1% with no outbreak in it at all, nobody unburied and two points of hunger on a baseline of 12.8% that general care covered 35% of. On 18 September the same seed ended at 36.8%, fifteen points of it the unburied; the batches since moved every seed, so the two readings are two cities, not a trend. On 28 September it had 52 outbreaks again, ill for 301 months, worst month 45%, and ended at 19.2%: no outbreak, nobody unburied, five points of hunger on a baseline of 14.1% that general care covered 26% of. On 1 October, at 0.7.23: 52 outbreaks, ill for 324 months, worst month 45%, ending at 20.7% — no outbreak, nobody unburied, 4.2 points of hunger on a baseline of 16.4% that general care covered 10% of.

The people who stay sick

Each band carries a thirteen-month ring of who fell ill when. Past two months sick, a share of them dies each month — 4% for babies and seniors, 8% for the over-85s, 2% for adults, 1% for children and teens. General care is what gets people out of the ring: it cures 50% of the sick a month untreated and 90% fully served. Babies and the retired fall ill twice as often as the city rate until their own care takes the excess away.

What general care stopped doing

It used to be a ×3 swing on the death rate of every teen and adult. That is gone: general care now acts only through the sick rate and through curing the sick. A fully served adult used to die at 0.15% a year; they now die at about 0.232%, base plus a sliver of illness. The halving of the base rate does not make that back at the good end — deliberately, and it is a live lever.

What care does to a life

A multiplier on the band's annual death rate, interpolated geometrically on coverage — unserved is multiplied by the swing, fully served is divided by it, half-served is exactly the base rate. Log space is the right space for a multiplier: halfway between "forty times worse" and "forty times better" is the same.

Annual mortality all-in: the band's rate scaled by care, plus steady-state illness — struck before the seniors were split at 85, and kept as it was struck; the two bands now start from 2.90% and 12.77%, with swings of 1.35× and 1.80×
BandCare typeSwingNo careHalfFull
BabiesChildcare40×7.96%0.77%0.017%
ChildrenChildcare40×0.83%0.120%0.004%
TeensGeneral—0.56%0.133%0.024%
AdultsGeneral—1.28%0.449%0.232%
SeniorsSenior1.35×9.96%5.14%3.35%

Teens and adults have no swing left; the whole of their column is illness. Senior care's swing is 1.35× on the 70-to-85 band and 1.80× on the over-85s, because that is where the care is worth more. Childcare also doubles the birth rate at full coverage — 15 per thousand a year to 30 — and senior care makes the city 30% more attractive to migrants, which is what turns an ageing pyramid into something to manage rather than endure.

Measured on the default seed on 22 September: illness is 32.7% of all deaths — 147,312 of 450,113 over 4,005 months, against 42.7% on 18 September; on 28 September it was 39.9%, 263,361 of 660,761 over 4,002; on 1 October 58.0%, 448,687 of 773,490 over 4,005. Two editions ago the same figure read 1.4%, 655 of 46,134, and that difference is not the model: until 17 September the hunger measure was reporting zero in a city that was starving, and the sick rate it feeds was being fed a zero (see what a household eats); and the playtest's default player leaves the city alone for fifty-four months at a stretch, so every figure it reports is a city in crisis from neglect. A player who tops up power, water, streets, clinics and stores once a year runs the same seeds at 7.8% sick against 23.1%. Babies take the worst of it: twice as often ill, 4% a month past two months, on top of childcare's ×40.

The two ways to bury somebody

A cemetery is a stock: plots are consumed permanently and the land never comes back. A crematorium is a rate: almost no land, a great deal of electricity, breaking even only when busy. Households whose monthly surplus would cover a plot over ten years choose burial; the rest choose cremation; overflow runs both ways. A city with neither leaves people where they fell, and the unburied make the living ill — up to 15 points on the sick rate, cleared the month a cemetery opens.

Somebody pays for all of it

Healthcare carries 2,128 jobs across its fourteen templates, more than any other category the city pays for — only the railway's three offer more. The treasury pays the staffed payroll plus maintenance, less what patients and funerals bring back — measured at about 20% cost recovery when the ring was added, 25% on the default seed on 22 September, $5.04M a month, with 246 buried and none cremated in the last month of the run, 30% on 28 September, $5.76M a month, 396 buried and none cremated, and 33% on 1 October, $4.28M a month, 400 buried and none cremated: a net-deficit business at the founding fees, as designed, and since 0.6.8 the player's to make something else. Fees are charged on people served, not beds built, so an empty ward is paid for and collects nothing — and since 0.6.8 served means treated.

Same 600-house city, 360 months, four programmes — measured before the base rates were halved and the sick ring added, and kept because the shape is what it is for
ProgrammePopulationOff sickBirths/moGDP/mo
Nothing1,23233.0%1.5$693k
General + death care1,4963.0%1.9$1,298k
… plus childcare2,8133.0%5.6$2,214k
Everything4,4613.0%7.9$3,305k

The city is founded with a doctor, a nursery, an almshouse, a churchyard and a constabulary — enough for about 1,200 people, sized off the pyramid's own equilibrium shares, and not a building among them. The churchyard's graves last four centuries for a village and six years for a city of eighty thousand.

The price at the door

Jerus: "healthcare should be an adjustable price, all the way to even make it a profitable business or the option to make it an obligatory insurance payment system." Two dials since 0.6.8. TaxPolicy.healthFeeScale multiplies the three care fees — general, childcare and senior; funerals are deliberately unscaled — from nothing to 15× the founding ones, and healthPremiumRate takes up to 10% of every wage, employee side, on the EI premium's own base, into the treasury with nothing balancing it. Between them the Policy tab's Promises → Health page names three corners: free at the point of use and paid from taxes; a business, fees past the city's own break-even, which the page strikes live (Healthcare.breakEvenScale(), the gross cost over what the fees would raise at 1×); or insurance, no fee and a premium. The ceiling went from 5 to 15 after the first measurement, because a played city breaks even at ×7 to ×13 — the founding fees were set against founding wages, and the labour market has lifted a clinic's payroll since. The default seed on 22 September breaks even at ×9.85, on 28 September at ×10.73, and on 1 October at ×18.84.

A household that cannot pay goes without care, not without food — Jerus's rule, and what makes the fee a decision. The share of a household's bill that fits in what its plan says it will have after rent, its other bills and a basket for everybody in it — counting income, savings, the paper abroad and the credit still open, the waterfall's own order — is the share of its people the clinics treat, struck against the bill at full service so that a household that skipped last month does not read an empty bill as affordable and swing between served and starving. The rest go untreated in the sick rate, the swings and the births. A household that can cover its bills is served exactly as before, multiplied by exactly 1.0 and asserted at zero tolerance, so at the founding fee the cliff catches only households with nothing, the out of work past EI and elders on a pension: 0 to 247 people a month across the eight seeds, and none on the default seed on 22 September, 28 September or 1 October. Fees at ×5 recovered about two thirds of the cost and priced out no more people than ×1, because the households the cliff catches are out at any positive fee and the working poor can still find $50 a head — a fee that prices a family out of childcare is a fee the dial cannot yet set. HealthCheck's sections 12–16 walk it: the scale against the constants, the break-even, one household both ways, a poor city three times, a rich one at zero tolerance, and the premium to the penny. Since 0.7.12 the poor city's hunger is read over its last year rather than its last month, which was Jerus's change, and read that way the line is red: on 28 September the city charging fifteen times the founding fees was 30.2% hungry over its last year against the free city's 21.8%, 8.4 points against a tolerance of five. The gap has grown since: 77 points on 0.7.17's tree and 39 on 0.7.18's, as 0.7.18's implementer ran them (20.9% against 98.1%, then 14.5% against 53.5%), and on 1 October, at 0.7.23, the free city was 11.1% hungry over its last year and the dear one 51.1%, 40.0 points. Which change of 0.7.17 opened it is not traced. Of the dear city's hungry, as 0.7.12 traced them, a quarter were priced out of care, a fifth were billed care their household row had already paid for, and nearly half had money for food after their fixed bills and still planned less, which is untraced. It ships red on Jerus's word, "Ship with it red, flagged", and has a batch of its own on the list.

05

Crime & police

Crime has reasons. Every adult at liberty is sorted once a month, cell by cell, into a graded list of them — because the cells are the only place the reasons meet: the pool knows who is out of work, the housing match knows who has no door and who is crowded, and the household books know who is going short.

The reasons, in order. Where a group overlaps another, the higher weight counts.
ReasonWeight
No home5
Out of work, EI run out4
Short of money3
Out of work, still on EI2
Crowded — doubled up or flatsharing2
No reason at all0.1
plus too few police, per adult2
pressure = Σ adults × weight + 2 × (1 − coverage) × adults crimes = K × pressure × (1 − 0.90 × coverage1.5) coverage = staffed officers / (360 per 100,000)

K is struck, not typed. It is solved at startup so that a city with Canada's police coverage and the game's own equilibrium adult share commits Canada's 5,585 crimes per 100,000 a year. Full coverage is twice Canada's 180 officers per 100,000 and takes 90% off — drastically, and never to nothing, which was the brief.

A quarter of crimes are violent. Each costs its victim half a month off work, which goes onto the sick rate as an injury term, and 0.115% of them kill — calibrated against Canada's 1.61 homicides per 100,000. The rest is theft, worth a quarter of an unskilled monthly wage each, half taken from households and half from the tills, and handed to the offenders in proportion to the weight their own cell carried. Theft leaves a sector's books through a named stolen line, so the money identity can see it.

Arrests scale with coverage: 9% of crimes at full coverage, pro rata below it. Everyone caught serves six months in a ring of monthly cohorts. A prisoner is out of the labour force, out of the housing match, out of every offender group, has their debts frozen and keeps their own ledger row. Caught and not held is a real state: with no cell free, the arrest is recorded and the person walks.

Four buildings, all paid for by the city like the hospitals and the schools
BuildingCapacityJobsAll-in
Police Station120 officers172$45M
Police Headquarters500 officers750$180M
Jail300 cells264$360M
Penitentiary600 cells590$600M

Measured over eight 333-year seeds: crime runs at 1.8–2.3 times Canada's rate in the years before the first station and settles at about a tenth of it once coverage is full, with the residue coming almost entirely from the long-term unemployed and the crowded. Between 2,100 and 2,900 people are caught over a run and every one of them walks, because the smallest prison in the catalogue is 300 cells for $360M and a city of 15,000 never needs or affords one. Seven to ten people are killed over three centuries; $12.8M to $18.6M is stolen. Those were cities of fifteen thousand. The default seed on 22 September ended at 130,978 people with three police stations, coverage at 46%, crime at 1.22× Canada's, 59,189 caught over the run and every one of them still walking, 334 killed and $891M stolen — the advisor built a station three times in four thousand months and a prison never, so the lockup question below had not gone away, it had grown. On 28 September the same seed ended at 184,736 people with a station and a headquarters, coverage at 62% and crime at 1.19× Canada's, and this time the advisor had built jails: four stood at the end with 344 people inside, and 35,279 of the 79,592 caught over the run were still not held; 593 were killed and $1.50bn stolen. On 1 October, at 0.7.23, it ended at 163,391 people with three stations and nothing else, coverage at 43% and crime at 1.71× Canada's; it built no jail, so all 69,581 caught over the run walked, and 423 were killed and $1.00bn stolen.

06

Work & output

Fifteen private sectors, each keeping its own books, its own cash reserve, its own debt, its own share register and, since 0.7.12, its own bonds — and, since 11 September, each of them one class extending a single template. Eight of the fifteen have been added since, five of them in the three days to 18 September, and the whole point of the template is that each cost an afternoon rather than a week.

Why there is a template

Jerus: "we should make a single template, and every single sector just extends it, and adds or overrides, right? cause in the future there will be over a thousand or near a thousand sectors." Before it, six sectors were five handlers in five shapes, named by hand in about a hundred places — eleven loops in the economy manager, a six-way switch in the books, thirteen enumerations in the interface. A seventh would have been a week of finding them. It is now one class, one constant, one line in the registry. Sixty files changed, fifteen new, eight deleted; save format 21 and a clean break.

Everything a sector has is declared once in the template: its identity (a saved key, a label, the build-menu group its buildings sit under), its buildings (every template in buildings.json names its owner, and the sector reads capacity, jobs, land, book value, power, water and road load off its own stacks), its production (what it makes, what it uses, how many months it stocks), its books (one statement, one shape), its credit, listing and money abroad, its planning, and its save. A sector class is now a declaration of about twenty lines. Mining is the shortest, and the shape a thousand of them should have.

SectorMakesUses
Retailgroceries, priced cost-plus at the shelfthirteen foods — local first and imports for the shortfall: bread and bakery goods from the ovens, dairy, eggs, meat, vegetables and fruit from the farms, the five processed goods from the plants; grains and fish only ever off a ship
Real estatehousing — one rent cheque per occupied homehouses and apartment blocks from construction
Industrybread and bakery goodscrops from local fields first, imports for the shortfall — it used to make food out of nothing

Miningiron orea deposit, which the player must buy as land
Constructionbuilding work, recognised as it is deliveredmaterials

Manufacturingfabricated steel and machinery — export only until 16 September, when the assembly plants became their first customer at homesteel, local or imported — 61% of a fabricator's revenue

Restaurantsmeals, one ninetieth of a person-month each, at three to eight times what the food costthe same thirteen foods off the same shelf at the same prices — it imports nothing

Materials is the seventh, and the first sector added under the template. The Construction Materials Plant used to feed the city's yard for free; it is a business now, filling a shed rather than planning to two months of demand, and planning on a thirty-six-month view because a building boom is not a month long. A city that builds hard makes materials dear, and a plant near the builders is the first thing in the game that gets cheaper for the buyer and richer for the maker at once.

The eleventh to the fifteenth

Food processing is the eleventh. Jerus: "now a new sector which makes the ready meals, processed meals, snacks, drinks, and cooking fats?" Those five are 16.3 kg of the reference basket's 50, a third of what a household eats by weight, and every gram of them arrived by ship. Three plants, each decided by a different number: the Meat Works buys meat, vegetables and grains and makes processed meats and ready meals, with the input bill at 40% of revenue; the Snack & Oils Plant turns crops into snacks and cooking fats at 19%; the Bottling Plant turns crops into drinks at 4%. It took four calibration passes and three of them were not calibration: the generic planner sizes a sector by one good and skipped every template that did not make it, so two of the three plants were not refused but invisible; a first plant was valued at the import ceiling its own arrival would end, so processed meat stood at $8.90 the month the works was financed and $7.70 the month it opened, and the sector now prices its own plant with its nameplate in the supply; and the sales tax was not in the estimate, which matters here because an import carries no input credit and a city with no herds imports every gram — month 72 of one run read $35k of revenue, $1k of operating income and $5k of tax.

Then the calibration itself, by revenue per worker, which is the ratio that decides whether a maker survives a city growing up: the first pass ran $6,000–$10,700 a head against the two bakeries' $13,500 and $17,800 and a foundry's $33,600, and a Bottling Plant was six people making a tonne a day; the three are $17,100, $16,800 and $18,000 a head now, payroll 21–22% of revenue at mid-band. And one rule no other sector needs: nobody builds a food plant on exports, because the world's band is narrower than the processing step — raw meat lands at $7.00 and processed meat ships at $5.60 — so a plant is financed only when the city already eats what it would make. What the sector is for, in one measurement: at month 98 of a real city the same Meat Works is worth $16,559 a month on meat at a farm's export floor and $1,678 on meat off a ship. The building did not change; the farms did. Eight seeds: median GDP up 11%, $375.8M to $417.9M a month, population unchanged, the sector ending every seed with $52M–$86M of assets and zero write-downs in seven of eight.

Rail is the twelfth and Automotive the thirteenth, and both are told in transport: the railway is the business that wants to be paid the freight every price in this game has always had inside it, and the assembly plant is the first thing in the city that buys a beam or a machine — ore, steel, fabrication, and now something a person drives away in.

Luxury retail is the fourteenth. Jerus: "lets add restuarants as well as luxury stores, these two will absorb some spending as well", and on the pricing, "supply v demand, thats the most important thing." A Boutique and a Department Store import what they sell — the world has no shortage of watches — and what a city can be short of is a counter, so the margin is what gets struck, on the market's own rule with a mark-up in place of a price: 1.25× the landed cost with nobody in the shop, 4.00× with a queue at the door, and the position between them from what is wanted against what the counters can serve. Until 0.7.19 what is wanted was the pieces the households would take at the floor price, which nobody is ever charged: in Jerus's year-149 city that queue held the margin at 2.646 while only the shoppers who would pay 2.646 bought, and the sector earned 2.5 times any other's profit. Since 0.7.19 it is the pieces they would buy at the margin the shop strikes — Jerus chose "The customers actually served": "The markup reads the shoppers who buy at the price actually charged, not a queue counted at the lowest price" — and since a dearer margin loses buyers, the margin is the one fixed point at which the buyers it leaves put it exactly there (LuxuryRetail.strikeMargin(), by bisection on the households' own demand); the boutiques' planner reads the same buyers. In his city it took the margin from 2.662 to 2.263 (0.7.19). Crowded shops widen the margin, a wide margin makes boutiques worth building, and the investor builds them. What it is for is the hoard: household net worth stops diverging and starts oscillating. It is deliberately an import, so a rich city's spending leaves rather than going round again. It is also a fragile business — on one seed it finishes with 22 write-downs and a 252-month borrowing ban — and the sizing of the two buildings against a city's luxury budget has not been tuned. The swing it showed every other month for a while was a price nobody paid, found by 0.7.12: the shop costed its stock at last month's clearing price, which in a month with no seller at home was half the import price, so its shelf turned round in 98% of months; it reads the import price now, and turns round in 2%.

Restaurants are the fifteenth, and the rule Jerus attached is what makes them a different sector rather than a second boutique: a meal out replaces groceries. So a kitchen imports nothing. A dinner is the same thirteen foods off the same shelf at the same prices, which makes the kitchens a second buyer in a market the city is already short of — where "let the basket get dearer, not bigger" actually bites — and a second door to the food there is, since Retail's binding constraint is coverage rather than stock. A meal is one ninetieth of a person-month, three a day for thirty days, and the hunger measure counts a dinner at what a person-month of food costs, not at what the kitchen charged, because three to eight times the food is wages, rent and somebody else's washing up and none of it is nourishment. The margin is 3.0× to 8.0×, wider than the trade's own 2.9–3.6× because in this city labour runs at four times food — three centuries of farms and food plants made food cheap while the wage schedule did not move — and at most a third of anybody's meals are eaten out. The harness caught three faults nothing else would have: the sector had no demand at all, because a share of a rich household's surplus is a share of zero once the wealth term is in the plan; the kitchens could not pay their staff, because a building pays at 100% and serves at the operating rate, which is about half; and a kitchen that stocked for what it served last month was self-limiting for ever. Eight seeds against 0.6.6: median hunger 36% to 22%, sickness 18.1% to 16.6%, the kitchens feeding 11–26% of each city and the shops selling about 5% fewer baskets, which is the crowding-out working. The sector finishes seven seeds of eight with zero write-downs at 2.00% credit, which is the profile of a business that cannot fail, and that is on the list.

Five ratios, and they multiply

RatioShort of it becauseWhat it does
Energygeneration below drawa step: output scales with the share delivered, and the bill scales with it too
Watersupply below drawsame shape; residents draw 0.3 units a head and nobody bills them
Roadsload past 90% of capacitynon-linear, floored at 35% — a gridlocked city still moves, slowly; per sector since the streams were split, because a tram takes one business's staff out of the jam and leaves the one next door's lorries in it
Healththe workforce is ill or hurtcuts output only; the payroll is paid in full
Vansthe sector has not taken delivery of the lorries its plant needsthe only one a business buys: a fleet is built up at an eighth a month, wears out over ten years, and a firm with none is slowed to 60%, not stopped — it hires haulage; since 0.7.12 it orders only the vans its cash and credit can pay for

On top of those sits the job fill rate, worked out per job type: the allocator spends the workforce across the posts the city offers, and a sector's output is discounted by how much of its establishment it could actually staff, and its payroll is each job type's wage on the posts of that type it filled (0.7.17). Since 0.7.18 workers take the best-paid job they qualify for (labour), so where a short city's bottom rung would out-pay the one above it the two fill as one market, and the empty posts move up the ladder rather than sitting on its bottom rung. The posts the city offers are every building's, bar the builders' crews their work does not need, which they lay off (the builders). The four that arrive from outside are the city's to fix; the fifth is the sector's own capital, and is told with the vehicles in transport.

Why it is a ratio basis and not a ratio

A month's income statement is written at the start of the month, off last month's utilisation. The simulation then moves it. Save after that and a reloaded game would price the month at ratios it was never traded at — which showed up the day roads arrived, as $0.51 of income appearing out of a reload. Every statement carries the utilisation it was struck against.

Maintenance is an operating cost, and it is demand

Every building in the city pays 1% of its build cost a year to stand — every sector, every city-maintained category, and the bank's branches — in cash, in materials and in construction points, placed as a real order with the builders rather than written off as a line. Since 0.7.19 the cash part is the building's cash cost with its labour at today's builders' wages, and the bill carries the builders' sales tax: a business that makes taxable supplies claims it back as an input, and the landlords, whose rent is an exempt supply, and the bank's branch bear it, because no rebate reaches a repair; the tax on the city's own repairs is remitted to the city's treasury and so comes home (VAT). On a forty-year stock that is about 40% of the city's whole construction output, permanently. Charging it made the city bigger: population 74,043 → 77,121, GDP $81.7M → $106.2M a month, unemployment 41.5% → 37.6% — because maintenance here is somebody's order book.

Construction recognises revenue as work is delivered: an order adds to unearned revenue and a backlog, each month's points earn their share, and the builders buy their material as they build. Booking it all up front made the sector look enormously profitable in a boom and catastrophic the year after. Until 0.7.17 its payroll had a 25% idle floor, every post kept filled and a quarter of the wages paid with nothing to build; since then it keeps a core crew of a quarter of its posts, paid in full, and lays the rest off onto EI, and the shedding spiral is still possible and still worth warning about (the builders). Since 0.7.22 an order's unearned remainder can go back — a cancelled order of the city's is refunded out of the order book — and the overtime the city pays on a rushed site is earned beside the work on a line of its own, Overtime on rushed sites, and paid out to the crews as wages.

07

Goods & markets

There used to be two bespoke markets, one for food and one for iron, each with its own formula and its own bugs. There is one rule now, and thirty-one goods that obey it or opt out — thirteen of which are the food on a grocer's shelf, three of which are vehicles, and two of which the world makes and will not buy back.

World prices are in the world's money and reach the city through the exchange rate; the third number is what moving one unit costs, three quarters of the wedge between the other two
GoodUnitImportExportFreightPriced

Restaurant mealsmeal———by the seller: the margin over the food, struck against the tables
supply = flow + stock / 6 // never the raw stockpile position = demand / (demand + supply) price = exportPrice + (importPrice − exportPrice) × position

The symmetric form is load-bearing. demand/supply clamped to 1 prices a perfectly balanced market at the buyer's worst price — one mine feeding one mill used to clear at $399.40 against a $400 ceiling. Here a matched pair splits the band. Supply is flow plus a slice of the stockpile, never the stockpile itself: pricing off inventory alone is circular — low price, withhold, bigger inventory reads as more supply, lower price — and measured that way food collapsed to the floor in every scenario including a genuine shortage.

Neither end of a band is a constant: both are read off the world's own prices every month, through the exchange rate, so a devaluation is import inflation mechanically — and an incentive to substitute. And since 16 September neither end is a single number either: each is the world's own price plus, or less, the cost of moving one unit, so the band a city faces is three quarters freight and one quarter the world's margin, and the freight is a price a railway can win back — see transport. The shops buy local food first and import the shortfall; the mills take local ore first and scrap for the rest. One clearing pass runs every month: flow goods are made, seller-priced goods sold, each traded good priced, offered, bid for and allocated pro rata on both sides, then imported or exported, then the stockable goods made. Every fill is a trade; nothing in these classes moves money. Since 0.7.12 a seller stocks only what its cash and the credit it can still get will pay for, after the bills it cannot avoid; a maker's inputs are the exception and are bought whole, because what a maker produces does not yet read what it bought, and holding its inputs back would make goods out of nothing. The households are not a sector and cannot bid in a strike: they take cars off the makers' shelf at the struck price and import the rest, through the same door the city uses for building material, and a month's purchases are demand for next month's strike. A good with a floor of zero — a van, a wagon set — strikes at exactly zero when nobody here wants one, and a saved zero is restored as zero, because for one build a guard against a missing figure dropped it and a reloaded city read a mid-band price in its place.

What a household eats

Until September 2026 one good called Food did all of this: a unit was a person fed for a month, everything they ate, and every question about a city's diet had that one answer. The shelf is itemised now, and what goes into the basket is decided by income rather than by a dial.

Engel's law is the first half: as a household gets richer the share of its money spent on food falls while the amount rises. Bennett's law is the second: the calories move off starchy staples onto meat, dairy, fish and produce. That is why grains carry a negative income elasticity — a staple is an inferior good, and a household eats less rice as it gets richer, not more.

Neither law is told what to do at the top of the ladder. A basket is scaled by the lesser of what a household can afford and what it can eat, so a poor household is money-limited and goes hungry while a rich one is calorie-limited and buys dearer food instead of more. The falling food share at the top is something the model produces rather than something it is given — which matters, because the first draft typed that share in as a constant and produced households whose calories went down as they got richer.

There is a third axis that is not income at all. Convenience food is bought for time: a working parent buys it at any income, and an out-of-work parent with the same children and the same money does not, because what they are short of is money and what they have is afternoons.

Income is measured in multiples of what bare subsistence costs rather than in dollars, so a currency reform cannot move it and the number means something you can say out loud: this household can afford four times the calories it needs to survive. The shops stock one shelf for the whole city, averaged over its households by headcount rather than by spending — a shelf stocked for money would carry the rich end's fish and fruit for a city of labourers.

The shelf is thirteen pantries

A shop can sell a basket only if it has all of it: what it can serve is the good that runs out first, which is Liebig's barrel on a shelf. That is also how a units bug was caught — shelf room was still sized in units of food when the goods became kilograms, and one person-month is exactly 50 kg of them, so every shop in the city delivered 20% of demand for ever. A city with roads and a city without scored identically until it was found; afterwards, 87% against 31%.

When the shelf was itemised all thirteen were imported. Eleven of them can be made at home now: the ovens bake bread and bakery goods out of crops and export what the city cannot eat; the farms make dairy and eggs, meat, vegetables and fruit; the plants make the five processed goods out of meat, vegetables, grains and crops. Grains and fish still only ever come off a ship. And since 18 September the shelf has a second buyer: the kitchens buy the same thirteen in the reference basket's proportions and sell them cooked, one ninetieth of a person-month a plate.

The hunger measure was reporting zero in a city that was starving

Until 17 September the figure read sold over the shops' own demand, and the shops' demand was already the least of coverage, want and what could be afforded — the shops' performance against a queue the shops had themselves decided they could serve. At month 3,840 of seed 0 the households planned 21,446,946 baskets, the shops could serve 177,760 people, and the line said 76%. A basket is one person-month, so the city wanted a hundred and twenty-two baskets a head and was allowed one. Honest: 58% of people short, 1.4% of the groceries asked for. Not a reporting change: the hungry feed the sick rate, which feeds the labour supply, and it had been fed a zero. Turning it on cost the default seed 11% of its population and 14% of its GDP — revealed, not caused — and showed a cascade nobody had seen, sickness 12.9% to 34.5%, of which hunger is eight points and the unburied fifteen. The basket count is floored to a whole basket, because dividing money by money and keeping the fraction broke the currency-reform twin 1.5e-06 at a time.

08

The private sector

Nobody tells businesses what to build. Each of the fifteen sectors measures a different signal, projects it six months out, and expands only if the new capacity would service its own debt.

the brake estimated monthly profit ≥ monthly interest × 1.25

A business test, not a credit limit: the lender is willing, the business should not be. Alongside it: one order at a time per sector, bar the landlords, who since 0.7.17 may hold twelve months of the builders' work on their sites (the builders); no order larger than would open inside twelve months at the wait it would actually have beside everything on site, where until 0.7.17 it was twelve months of the whole city's construction output, as though the order had the builders to itself; and a 5% headroom target so nothing expands into capacity it already has. Since 0.7.8 a plan is judged at the rate the loan would actually be written at, off the default curve at the leverage the loan would leave the sector at, and since 0.7.12 at the blend of a bond's coupon and the loan's rate where a bond carries part of it; 3,452 plans were declined on price over the default eight when the curve went in. A landlord's homes answer to the lender's own test instead (0.7.11): the building's net operating income must cover the mortgage's payment 1.20×.

And every planner that builds posts asks whether the city could staff it (0.7.18; Sector.staffing()). Jerus: "Construction, Retail, Restaurants, Luxury and the makers check whether they can staff a building before building it (the check four sectors already use), and the 20% it allows can't be jobs nobody can fill." A building passes when the city's spare workers could fill 80% of its posts (MIN_STAFFABLE_TO_ORDER), each band's spare counted as the best-paid-job fill would place it, and none of the rest is in a band nobody can fill. That last is Jerus's reading of his own words, "Truly unfillable only": a band with no spare workers, nobody above it who could step down into it, and no migrants who come for it. Every band has migrants who come for its wage since the same batch, so in play it never binds — 0 of 2,160 staffing questions in a default city — and stands for a band that ever has no way in, which InvestCheck's section 18 causes. An order of several is held to all of them, and the advisor's hold says "the city could staff X% of a Y; it wants 80%". The literal first reading, no posts at all in a band with nobody spare this month, failed every building with a college post in every city without a college, and the default cities ended at 13,808 people against 162,702. Real Estate's homes have no posts and are not asked, and the city's own buildings are the player's; the bank's branch is asked since 0.7.19. In Jerus's year-149 city, run on for 120 months, it held Retail and Restaurants back in 108 of them, Luxury in 116 and Food Processing and Mining in all 120 (0.7.18).

SectorWatches
Real estatejobs plus jobs under construction — never population, which is its own echo
Retaila twelve-month population trend, capped at housing that exists or is being built
Industryshop coverage against output; refuses outright if price is below cost
Constructionits own backlog over what the sites are left after the repairs, expanding past nine months of it, and only with a depot the city could staff (0.7.17)
Heavy industryspare ore — mines first, mills after
Miningunmined deposits, so geology gates it and the player controls the gate
Materialsthe sites' outstanding orders and the repair draw, on a three-year view
Business servicesseats it could actually staff — the world's appetite for the work is unbounded, so the brake is people, not demand
Manufacturingthe price of steel, which is the whole of it — 61% of a fabricator's revenue
Agriculturethe mills' crop bill against the price of the ground a field has to stand on — and refuses while anybody in the city has nowhere to live
Food processingwhat the city already eats of its five goods, less what it can already supply, valued at each good's export floor — never a forecast, and never the export market, because the band is narrower than the processing step; scores every template it owns, not the one that makes its planning good
Railthe freight nobody is carrying — it will not lay a line under 60% used, Materials' lesson relearned the afternoon a spur went up in a town of four hundred houses
Automotivethe parts — one new plant may want at most a quarter of everything the city fabricates, because "spare" was the wrong word and cost a fixture three hundred months
Luxury retailthe buyers at the margin it charges, against the counters — the queue counted at the floor price until 0.7.19
Restaurantsthe queue at the door: meals asked for against the tables there are, and a kitchen values itself, because nothing else can

The eighth, ninth and tenth, and why they are different

The first seven sectors all sold to the city. Business services and manufacturing sell to the world, which is what broke a two-hundred-year plateau: a city whose every customer lives in it can only grow as fast as it grows.
