Agriculture is the other direction — it sells to the mills, and it is the only sector whose real cost is the ground it stands on.

SectorInOutWhere the money goes
Business servicesalmost entirely people; 250 sq ft of lot a headsupport, back-office and engineering work, by the seat-month, at $5,500 / $8,600 / $14,600payroll 66.3% / 65.2% / 55.2%
Manufacturingsteel, local or importedfabricated steel and machinery, flow not stock; export only until the assembly plants arrivedsteel 61.0%, or wages 49.1% at the Machine Works
Agricultureland heavily, labour and power barelyCROPS, to the millslabour a seventh of a field's sale, power a five-hundredth

The engineering office is gated on a licence, not on money: it will not open without spare licensed engineers for half its gated posts (LICENCE_COVER_TO_OPEN = 0.5 — thirty-nine of them; thirty-eight is one short), and the only way to make one at home is an Institute of Technology. Steel got its first domestic customer and with it an import ceiling of 1.284 against the mills' export floor of .847, a 1.52× wedge that decides whether fabricating here pays at all: 1.55% of build cost a month on imported steel at the ceiling, 3.28% on steel at the mills' floor. The mills got an input cost — they used to make food out of nothing, on margins of 72–77% against every other plant's 28–31%.

The farmland dial

TaxPolicy.FARMLAND_RELIEF assesses farmland at use value rather than development value, on the land half only — barns are still taxed as buildings. It defaults to full relief and says in dollars what the relief costs. Six grain farms on $112m of ground: $151,496 a month with no relief, $11,295 with full relief. Without it a field cannot outbid a warehouse for the same acre, and by month 4,002 at $66 a square foot there are no fields left — ten greenhouses, and the two founding mixed farms.

How an expansion is paid for

In a fixed order, every month: sell shares if the register says the company should be raising equity, spend the till, call money home from abroad, and only then borrow. A company under a year old sells half of what it plans; a company with nine good months raises toward its equity target; a company with a bad year raises nothing and borrows nothing. Since 0.7.12 borrowing is two doors: the sector takes the largest bond whose all-in cost is no dearer than the bank's loan and borrows the rest from the bank, and loan and bond together never pass what the bank would lend. A landlord's homes are bought another way since 0.7.11: at least 15% of the cost from its own till and what it holds abroad, its owners asked for new shares for what those lack, and an insured mortgage for the rest (the bank).

Capacity retires, and distress retires it faster

Six consecutive losing months, genuinely spare capacity by a 25% margin, and nothing on site — then the biggest holding is thinned, never past what is actually in use, never more than a quarter in one month. The streak is carried in the save, because forgetting it made scrapping save-scummable.

Separately, a firm that cannot pay sheds plant: twenty-four months of losses and a negative balance after the credit desk has already refused it. Six months killed every founding plant in testing; two years is the runway a real business gets. Since 0.7.12 it may sell any plant that makes something the sector sells, because the rule could once sell only plant making a sector's main good and a Shared Services Centre lost money for 1,151 months. What is retired is sold to the builders as material since 0.7.8 — Jerus: "sold to construction company just in materials, and land to treasury" — so Construction pays for the template's material at the day's price as far as its cash goes and builds with it before it buys, and the plot goes back to the city as it always did. Occupied homes and the bank are never scrapped by their owners, though since 0.7.22 the city may buy a landlord's homes out and pull them down (the builders); the bank closes its own branches, by its own rule (the bank). A mine over a worked-out deposit now closes inside a year instead of borrowing to pay its crew for three — which makes the bust when the ore runs out sharper, and lets the bank survive it. And a distressed landlord can no longer demolish a city out from under it: the railway's bigger business cycle pushed Real Estate into distress deep enough that it sold 5,800 of a city's 13,272 homes in seven months and left 1,137 households with nowhere for a hundred and forty, because the retirement rule asked whether the segment had spare doors and a segment's slack is spread over four sizes. It is guarded, not fixed: nobody sells a door while the housing match says somebody has nowhere, and the proper fix — slack per size — is on the list.

Two banners exist because these fail silently

Builders being laid off — construction with no orders shrinks to fit, and everything you build runs through those crews. Nowhere to build — the private sector asks for land every month, is told no, and waits. Measured at 120 consecutive months in one ordinary run.

09

The builders & the queue

Every building in the city is put up by one sector out of one month's output, and until 0.7.17 that output was split equally by building type on site, so a single depot took as much as two hundred blocks of flats. Three batches made it the crew each building can use (0.7.17), a price that keeps up with the wages that build it (0.7.19), and a queue the player can put a hand on (0.7.22).

Every building gets the crew it can use

Jerus chose "Share building work fairly": "Builders' output split by buildings on site (every site gets a crew), and no more parking points on building types with nothing left to build". The rule it replaced shared the sites' output equally by building type. In his year-149 city four types were on site: the builders' own one-depot order, owed 400 points, took a quarter of the output, about 10,700 points, and carried the other 10,300 as progress on a stack with nothing left on it; the landlords' 219 blocks of flats got the same quarter, about 250 homes a month; and 7.82M points sat parked on stacks with nothing to build.

weight = buildings on site × points a building0.70 // CREW_SCALE_EXPONENT = 1 − Bromilow's B share = the month's site output after the repairs, one rate a unit of weight, each site capped at what it still owes, and what a capped site leaves shared by the same rule

The exponent is Bromilow's time–cost law, T = K × CB, with B about 0.30 on Australian building contracts and 0.32 on Malaysian public projects (Chan 1999): the crew a building can use is its work over its time, which grows as C1−B. So a building twice the cost takes about 1.23 times as long, and a university of 26,000 points has about eighteen times the crew of a depot's 400. Only the exponent is taken; K, the pace a building could go at alone, is not modelled, and nothing caps a site's crew at it. The weight is the whole building, never what is left of it, because a crew that shrinks with its work never finishes: the rule was briefly the points each stack still owed, and in Jerus's city twelve sites sat one building from done for years. Output no site can use is idle, read as the builders' utilisation, and never banked; a save's parked points are cleared on load and move no money — 7,801,561 of them in his city.

It took four readings to get there. One crew a building, whatever its size, gave a lone university beside two hundred blocks of flats one block's share, and it took ninety months where it had taken twenty-one. A crew in proportion to its points gave a founding's six shops of 120 points a four-hundredth of the output between them, beside a coal plant of 120,000 and the founding's other big works, and the shops did not open for a decade; those two are the exponent at nought and at one. Jerus's own hybrid, "every order has atleast one crew, and then spare crews are assigned in ascending or descending order sort of like a pyramid", cleared every small building a large order starved in the checks, but the builders bought depots for the small orders that rushed through and then stood laid off: thirteen banks failed over the eight default cities against four, and unemployment moved half as much again in a month. By the rule he left, "v3 if you think yes, otherwise go for the v that starving small orders is fine", Bromilow's weights ship, and a small order waits behind a large one. One wait is read off this rule by everything that asks how long an order would take (BuildingManager.waitFor()): the planners' lead times and order sizes, the shops' horizon and the build quote, each the order's points over the share it would get beside everything on site.

The builders count repairs and staffing

Jerus chose "Builders count repairs and staffing": "Construction's own planner counts repairs and how well it can staff a depot, both when it adds depots and when it sells them off." The builders' months of queued work are read against what the sites are left after the repairs, which take about 40% of a mature city's construction output, and at their staffed output; a depot is ordered only if the city could staff 80% of it; and retirement keeps what the repairs and the queue need, in staffed output. In his city the depots stood 36% staffed, because a depot is 70% unskilled posts and the unskilled rung was 8.5% filled, and the planner, reading its nameplate against the queue alone, sold its capacity from 232,000 points a month to 56,800 between months 912 and 1,020, after which homes sat flat for nine years. Since 0.7.19 a depot's profit estimate counts the wages of the posts it would fill; it had counted none, and priced a point at what a unit of material costs.

The crews the work needs. Jerus chose "Lay off idle crews". Before the labour market fills the month's posts the builders strike the share of their depots' posts the work ahead needs — the repairs plus every point still owed on site, less what the city's own works department does with no posts, over what the depots would do fully staffed — divided by last month's fill, so that the crews who come are the crews the work needs, and never under 25% of the posts (Construction.IDLE_PAYROLL_FLOOR) nor over all of them. The posts not offered are not jobs: the city counts fewer, the workers who held them are unemployed by the ordinary path and draw EI, and the allocator gives them any other post it has. The posts kept on are paid in full. Until 0.7.17 an idle builder kept every post filled and paid a quarter of the wages; once every employer's bill became what its workers receive, that quarter was what the crews took home, and the batch's first build, which kept it, turned MonetaryCheck's reading of the rate over and tripled the bank failures of the city held at 25%. The planners still count every post, because a builder that laid crews off for want of work is not short of crews for a queue. What it costs: unemployment moves about twice as much from month to month, the 95th percentile of the default eight's monthly move from 0.81 points to 1.93 (0.7.17).

The landlords hold a year of work

Jerus chose "Landlords hold work, not one order": "Landlords may hold orders worth a number of months of building work, instead of one order at a time sized on the whole city's output." One order at a time, sized on twelve months of the whole city's output, was delivered at about 250 homes a month in his city, and the next order was held already building for fifty months. Since 0.7.17 they keep ordering while what their sites owe, the order being weighed included, is at most 12 months of the builders' site output after the repairs (BusinessInvestment.MAX_ORDER_MONTHS, withinMonthsOfWork()), read with every post offered, since builders who have laid crews off hire them back for the order. When their best home does not fit they order the next smaller one that does (Jerus: "Try a smaller home"), and the homes on site count as their supply. Every other sector keeps one order at a time. In his city, run on for 120 months from month 1,792, 0.7.17 added 118,944 homes where 0.7.16 added 38,052, the city grew to 1,517,361 against 1,284,343, and all 47 of its care complexes were finished, the last at month 108.

The price keeps up with the wages

Jerus chose "Builders' prices keep up": "The labour part of building and repair prices rises with wages, materials are paid at the price when they're used, and sales tax is in the builder's quote." Until 0.7.19 the non-material part of every contract and repair bill was the template's cash cost in founding dollars. In his city at month 1,793 a Low-Rise paid the builders 4.25 a point of work against a full depot's wage bill of 6.59 a point, and they lost money in every one of the decade's 120 months, $46.97B in all.

labour = min(cash cost, points × a depot's founding wage bill a point) × (a depot's wage bill a point today / at founding) work = the rest of the cash cost at its founding value + labour price = (work + imports + plant × (1 − rM)) / (1 − rB) // rB the builders' sales tax, rM the materials plant's

A Construction Depot's 35 unskilled and 15 diploma posts cost $188.6k a month at the founding ladder for 400 points, 0.47 a point, and that much of a template's cash cost a point is its labour; the rest — overhead, equipment and margin, all but 1,132 of a Low-Rise's 10,196 — stays at its founding value. Indexing the whole cash cost by the wage index was measured by hand and rejected: in his city it would have been 13.5 times the cash cost, a windfall rather than a price. The split is the model's own, because Statistics Canada's Building Construction Price Index is an output index and publishes no labour and material weights. It reaches the quote, the repair bills and the planners' costing, and not the book values or the assessments, which carry a building at its cash cost as before.

The material is paid at the price when it is used, by an escalation clause on the pattern of the US Federal Acquisition Regulation's 52.216-4. The owner still pays the whole quote up front, the material priced at the day's rates as an allowance; as the crews draw it, each owner pays its units' share of what the month's draw cost the builders, less its allowance, or is refunded the difference (Game.settleSiteContracts()). The city pays on a treasury line of its own, Material escalation, owed to the builders as arrears past the advances ceiling; a sector pays from its till. And the sales tax is in the quote, grossed up so that the builders keep exactly the work: until 0.7.19 they remitted their rate on all of it out of the work, and in his city 108.6 of their 150.2 of tax fell on material passed through at cost. Who gets it back is the VAT's (tax, policy and GDP).

In his city, run on for 120 months, the builders went from losing $46.97B before tax to earning $9.03B, with 18 loss months where there had been 120; a Low-Rise paid 10.36 a point against a full depot's labour of 6.59, a margin of 36% of what it paid. The first pass took about 30% off the ensembles' cities — the default eight from 166,607 people at the median to 115,002 — because the landlords bore the tax on every home and the bank's first branch carried its upkeep; the rebates on a new rental home and the charter's exemption (the bank) took both back, and the shipped default city ended at 169,978 against 0.7.18's 166,607 (p 0.442).

The player's hand on the queue

Jerus wanted a construction panel that is "not just a blue loading screen", and then "not only repirotize and cancel but also destroy buildings, like you yourself destroy buildings". On 30 September he chose the mechanics — priority "Both", cancel "Keep the half-built shell", demolish "City's, plus buy-outs (Recommended)" — and since 0.7.22 each is a rule with a source (ConstructionControl). None of it runs unless the player uses it: with no order set and no rush, every wait and every quote is the old one to the bit, and the default playtest's traces did not move. A site is what the model keeps on site, one stack per building type and, since this batch, each of the city's demolitions; the city's sites are the stacks whose every order is the city's. A stack it shares with an investor's order cannot be reordered, rushed or cancelled, because its progress is one pool.

The five hands, with their sources (0.7.22)
HandThe ruleSource
Prioritythe city's share of the crews' rule is split among its own sites top down, in the player's order, each taking what it still owes; nobody else's site gains or loses a point; free; a new city site joins at the bottom, and the order clears itself once none of its sites is leftnone needed: an owner choosing which of its own contracts goes first
Rushone of the city's sites on 50-hour weeks: 1.137 times a normal month's work in its first month, 1.024 in its second, 0.938 in its third and 0.9375 after, less than a normal month; the city pays 0.375 of the crews' wage bill on the site's share, with the builders' tax, and the crews are paid it as wages; owed as arrears past the ceiling; the count resets after a month offBusiness Roundtable, Scheduled Overtime Effect on Construction Projects, Report C-2 (1980), Figure 4; time and a half, Canada Labour Code s. 174
Cancelat the month's end: the month's work done and billed, and the work not done, the material not drawn and the tax on both refunded out of the builders' book; the shell keeps its work, its material and its ground, to be restarted at today's quote or demolishedtermination for convenience, FAR 52.249-2; its settlement costs have no line in the model and none is invented
Demolisha site of 5% of the template's points, priced at that building's own work rate with the tax and crewed by the same rule; the building closes as the next month starts — its staff let go and, if it is a home, its households moved into the homes that stand, whoever does not fit left unhoused rather than deleted — its material is sold to the builders by the 0.7.8 rule when the work is done, and its ground comes back to the citySIGTARP, 26 April 2017: Detroit's average demolition of July 2015, $14,855, over NAHB's 2015 average new home, $289,415, is 5.1%
Buy-outa business's or a landlord's building: the owner is paid its book value, its ground at the land price, and its share of the owner's operating income for the months a replacement would take; its debts stay with it; then the city demolishes itOntario's Expropriations Act, ss. 13(2), 14(1) and 19(1); the 5% residential allowance of s. 18(1)(a)(i) does not apply, because no owner in the model lives in what it owns
What each hand did in the harness's fixture city, as 0.7.22's implementer probed it at about month 38 with the builders' tax at 15% (0.7.22)
HandWhat happened
Cancelan Elementary School ordered at $35.1M, cancelled at 1,023 of its 1,800 points: $15.17M back — $7.73M of work not done, $5.16M of material not drawn, $2.28M of tax; restarting the shell costs $13.68M at today's quote, demolishing it $0.60M
Rusha University on overtime for three months: 1,377, 1,243 and 1,253 points of work against shares of 1,211, 1,213 and 1,335, for a premium of about $230k, $231k and $254k a month with the tax
Buy-outa landlord's block of Low-Rise Apartments: $17.82M to the landlords — $17.12M for the building, $0.15M for the ground and $0.55M of business loss over 10.9 months — and $0.59M to pull it down
Demolishthe city's own Elementary School: $1.05M and about 3.6 months; 48 posts and 600 places close, and its 666 units of material are worth $12.2M at that month's price

Four calls were made while Jerus slept. A demolished building closes as the next month starts, because closing it on the spot made a city reloaded between the presses play a different month from the live one. A month whose normal share would finish the site anyway is not worked on overtime. Short of materials never shows as a site's status, because the model imports whatever the yard, the salvage and the plant do not cover. And the bank's branches can be neither demolished nor bought, since the bank closes them by its own rule. The docs pass's throwaway audit balanced the money to $0.00 through a rush, a cancel, two demolitions and a twenty-house buy-out.

A demolition can pay

The 0.7.8 rule sells a building's material back at the day's market price, so the fixture's school brought $12.2M of material against a $1.05M demolition, and real demolition recovers far less than new material costs. Whether to lower it or keep it as the 0.7.8 rule wanted is on the list (open questions).

The construction page

The hand is played on a page of its own (ui/ConstructionScreen), reached from the right panel's Open, from Construction at the head of Build, and from a build card's N on site, which opens it at that site. Sites carries the builders' gauge — BUILDERS, the month's points after the repairs; OWED ON SITE; the QUEUE in months against the landlords' twelve; and the MATERIAL in the yard — and then a row a site: the city's first, in the player's order with a rank to move, then everyone else's, then the stopped shells; who ordered it, the done of the total, this month's crews and the time left, paid and owed, a status (waiting for crews, stops at month's end, closes as the month starts among them) and the hand. Timeline draws a bar a site to its finish at today's queue. Demolish is a picker — the city's buildings, the stopped shells, and the businesses' and landlords' to buy out — and a staging card that says what would close, what it costs, what comes back, the land and the months, before Demolish or Discard. Every hand that spends money asks first, with the money in the question, and the clock stands still while any question is up. One clock times a site everywhere (Game.siteMonths()), because the docs pass found two: the page read the player's order while the right panel, the build card and the quote read the crews' rule. The inbox gained Overtime now costs more than it gains, the month before a rush's third, and A demolition is done. None of it has been seen on a screen.

10

The owners, the bonds & the exchange

For most of the project's life the corporate sector had no owners: it made money, held it, and nothing ever moved it out. Sixteen companies have a share register now — the fifteen sectors and the bank — the fifteen can sell bonds as well as shares since 0.7.12, and both trade on order books where nobody is obliged to take the other side.

The register

Every company is founded with shares issued to the city's households at $1,000 each against its book equity. A company reads its own record before it asks for money: new under a year, good at nine profitable months and not declining, bad at six or fewer or a losing year, normal otherwise. Its equity target starts at 30% of assets and rises with how volatile its income has been, capped at 70%; a new company aims at half. Households subscribe first — a cell with no debt, no lockout and three months of disposable income banked puts 30% of the excess in — and the world takes the rest, but only if the yield clears the world rate plus three points. New shares are sold at one price, the last trade or fair value before one, and never through the book, because a plan needs to know in the month it builds what it raised. The city's fund never subscribes (0.7.14).

Forty percent of a positive month is paid out, from the till, or from the company's own money abroad if the till is short — since 0.7.11 of what the month's net income leaves once the principal it repaid is taken off, Jerus: "Pay out after principal", and since 0.7.12 of that principal net of the new borrowing that rolled it, which is free cash flow to equity, the figure real companies pay dividends from. The bank pays by its own rule (the bank). A company's fair value is the higher of its book equity and the dividend it actually pays capitalised at the world's required return, per share; its price is its last trade, and before its first trade its fair value.

One book for each company

Jerus: "Order book for both." Since 0.7.12 every share that changes hands meets its buyer on an order book (OrderBook), one per company: price and then time decide who is filled, a trade is at the resting order's price, nobody trades with themselves, and nobody is obliged to trade — an order with nobody across it rests, and every order still resting is withdrawn at the month's step and posted again from each participant's view of the new month. Each participant posts from the rule it already followed, in this order:

  • The bank's desk, if it has capital: a bid one per cent under fair value for what its capital can carry, an ask one per cent over it for what it holds, and an ask at fair value itself for anything past its two caps — 25% of the bank's equity in any one company and 50% in all of them. Its own shares are capital, not trading: it bids for them only with what it holds over its target and sells new ones only while it is under it.
  • Emigrants sell on the way out, and what nobody bids for stays abroad with them.
  • The world buys when the dividend yield at the best ask clears its own rate plus a three-point premium by a margin, and sells into strength when it falls short, never into a crash, and never in a company under a year old.
  • Companies past their first year and not in a bad one, with more equity than their target and six months of their costs and their debt service in the till bid for their own shares at up to 10% over fair value, a tenth of the company a year at most, and cancel what they buy. What nobody sells them stays in the company: no special dividend is paid by any path since 0.7.12, because paying the unspent money out left the tills bare and brought the failures back.
  • Every household cell is a participant of its own (Jerus: "Each household type trades"): a cell under its cushion offers a tenth of the shortfall at the market, and a cell past it buys four per cent of its excess a month into the best yield over the bank's deposit rate plus a point, the pace its dollars abroad move at.
  • The city's fund last, taking what the others left, at fair value (tax, policy and GDP).

A household short of money sells in the middle of the month, in its waterfall, at the price at which the dividend it gives up costs what borrowing would, so the rich cells' resting bids are where a poor cell's shares go — a transfer between households that no audited pool sees. A share priced at a hundred times its founding price is split a hundred for one, and one that falls to a hundredth is consolidated, and every holding, every resting order and the last trade move by the factor; a cumulative split factor keeps the price history readable per founding share. On the default seed on 28 September the book traded $39.2bn of shares in 44,049 trades over 4,002 months, a listed company traded in 46% of its months, and the median last trade stood at 0.99 of fair value; on 1 October it traded $92.1bn in 32,065 trades over 4,005 months, a listed company traded in 38% of its months, and the median last trade stood at 0.990.

The bank was the dealer, until 0.7.12

From the exchange's first night until 0.7.12 the bank's trading desk was the counterparty on every trade, and quoted nothing when the bank was dead. It quoted around fair value, its own book moved the quote, it never sold what it did not hold, and it marked what it held at the lower of the quote and fair value. Its book was capped at a multiple of the bank's equity, which is money — and until 18 September that cap was the one figure in the exchange a currency reform did not divide. It was re-struck each month after the households settled, so for the first half of every month a reformed city's desk believed it had a hundred times the room it had; a household with no savings and a grocery bill it could not meet borrowed in the plain city and sold $1.03 of shares in the reformed one, and from there household debt was 4.2% apart in one month and the exchange rate with it. The bisect blamed the fourteenth sector's inventory term, and the term was right: it had moved GDP, GDP had moved what the desk's book was worth against its limit, and a cancellation that had been landing on one side of zero started landing on the other. A bisect names what perturbed a fault, not what the fault is; a latent member of that family is not a harmless one, it is one nobody has perturbed yet.

mid = fair × clamp(1 − 0.25 × (dealerShares − demand) / scale, 0.50, 5.00) // the dealer's quote, until 0.7.12 bid/ask = mid ∓ half of a 2% spread

The desk foots, since 19 September. Its opened lines on the bank's statement had left out the term that is usually the largest — the change in the mark on what it holds — so a loss sat over sub-lines that did not add to it. Re-marked what it holds (Bank.getMarkChange()) is a line of its own now, and when it is the bulk of a loss the note says why, which is Jerus's question — "explain to me the trading desk, cause a bunch of times it's losing billions": with buyers it could not fill the dealer quoted up to five times fair value to draw sellers, and it marked what it held at the lower of the quote and fair value, so a wave of emigrants selling into a bubble cost it the difference the same day. BankCheck asserts the lines plus the re-mark equal the total, and on a played city they did in 120 of 120 months. Whether a dealer should bid at a quote it does not believe in stopped being a question when the dealer went; the desk marks at the lower of the last trade and fair value. Since 0.7.1 the same desk also buys back the city's paper from the households, at the month's market value off the curve.

What it was worth

When the exchange arrived, eight seeds against the batch before it: GDP $65M → $77M a month, worst unemployment 46% → 37%, exports $104M → $124M, the currency inside 3.5% of parity, dividends to households $3–18bn → $30–56bn a run, every company home-owned in six seeds of eight — and bank failures 10 → 0, because the trading desk was a business and a profitable one, and a sector that has just paid its owners borrows for its next mine.

The firms sell bonds

Jerus: "notes are the loans businesses make to the banks, and bonds are debts that they issue to investors… households can buy them, but banks also buy them as, only if it wants, and yes they are tradeable so they have par value and all." The bank failed 115 times on 0.7.11's eight default seeds, and the cause was concentration: the one bank was every business's only lender, and the sector that broke it owed five to six times its equity. So since 0.7.12 a sector borrows through two doors. A bond (CorporateBond) is a ten-year bullet with a coupon every month, built at par: the underwriter announces the room, every bidder says what it would take at each yield, the coupon is the lowest yield that fills the issue and the rest is a bank loan, and the bank underwrites it for the city's own charge, $12k plus 0.75%. A sector borrowing for a building takes the largest bond whose all-in cost is no dearer than the loan and borrows the rest; the first reading let bonds raise what a capital-short bank had refused, at coupons up to 16%, and failed the bank more, so loan and bond together never pass what the bank would lend. A sector short in the month can be sold a bond by the shortfall desk the same way.

Every bond trades on a book of its own, and the buyers are the ones Jerus named, each by the rule it already had for the nearest thing it holds: the household cells by their rule for the city's paper, wanting a share of each issue that rises with its expected return over what savers are paid; companies that owe nothing by the rule that sends idle cash abroad, never into their own bonds; the world by hot money's; the bank only on the capital it holds over its target, at the yield that makes the bond earn what an equal loan would; and the city's fund. A household short of money sells its bonds after its dollars abroad and before its shares. A bond that falls due is paid from its issuer's till to every holder at face. When a sector's firms default the bondholders lose too, and since 0.7.12 each instrument recovers what it recovers in the world: 75% of a bank loan and 45% of a bond (LOAN_RECOVERY, BOND_RECOVERY, the midpoints of Moody's and S&P's long-run averages), and the eight seeds measured 74.8% and 44.9%. On the default seed on 28 September 2,266 bonds were issued for $71.2bn over the run; 44 were outstanding at the end, $2.36bn, the households holding $1.29bn of it, other companies $914M and the world $126M. On 1 October 1,857 were issued for $62.8bn, and 5 were outstanding at the end, $183M, the households holding $40.3M of it.

The player does not trade, bar the fund's hand. The Sector page's Its owners block shows the last trade and its month, or fair value where there has been none, the yield on the dividend actually paid, the month's trades, and five levels of bids and asks with the company's own buyback resting among them; there is a price chart per company, each household cell's shares at the last trade and its bonds, the foreign share of each register on the balance-of-payments page, and under Finances a bond market with every issue, who holds it and each bond's own book twelve levels deep.

11

Labour & schools

A wage used to be one of six constants, copied in once at startup. Labour is a market now, skill is an axis, and a school is the only way to make a doctor at home.

Four bands, one price each

NONE < DIPLOMA < COLLEGE < UNIVERSITY // substitution runs downward only tightness = posts / qualified supply // per band, at the fill that gives every worker the best-paid post they qualify for (0.7.18) target = minimumWage × ratio[job] × clamp(tightness0.5, 0.70, 4.0) wage += (target − wage) × 0.12 // 90% of a shortage prices in over 18 months

The minimum wage is the base of the whole ladder, so one Policy dial moves every wage in the city, the treasury's own payroll included. Because the unskilled base is the floor, an unskilled glut cannot be priced away — it clears in people instead, which is exactly what a binding minimum wage does. Wages also chase the cost of living in full, both directions, over 24 months, and since 0.6.11 a harness holds them to it: LabourCheck runs 240 months of steady inflation and asserts every month that the wage index moves exactly DRIFT_PER_MONTH of the way toward the published price index, through a currency reform and a reload, and on all eight seeds every one of 144 checkpoint readings sits at the level the two-year lag implies — the one-third overshoot measured on 15 September does not reproduce. Students and prisoners are out of the supply while they are away, and the pool is what is left when filled posts are taken off the labour force — an identity, not a second count.

Gated jobs carry a licence premium on top of their band's: posts over licence holders, same elasticity, never below one, the pair capped at 4×. A hospital with no medical school pays its doctors over the graduate band — measured at 1.26× against a band in glut at 0.83× — and that price is what draws licensed arrivals and sends graduates back to school.

Workers take the best-paid job they qualify for (0.7.18; PopulationManager.fillByBand()). Jerus: "Workers take the best-paid job they qualify for, instead of skilled jobs being filled first. Unskilled pay could then never pass diploma pay, but hospitals and schools would lose some staff." The bands used to be filled from the top down, with only a band's surplus passed down the ladder, so a city short of hands put the whole shortage on its bottom rung whatever that rung paid: in his year-149 city every one of 185,132 missing workers was an empty unskilled post, 8.5% staffed at $68.6k a month, more than a doctor's $60.4k, while diploma holders filled diploma posts at $25.5k. The rule people follow is occupational choice with skill downgrading — take the best-paid work you are qualified for, and when the lower rung pays as well the better-qualified move down it and push the shortage up (Beaudry, Green and Sand 2016, Journal of Labor Economics 34(S1)); no number is taken from it, only the rule. A worker may hold any post at or below their band. The bands are filled from the top as before, and a band that still has empty posts and would pay more than the band above it is joined to it, one market at one wage, until nobody holds a lower-paid post while a better-paid one they could take stands empty; the allocator, the wage, migration's chance of work and the planners' spare workers all read the one fill. How many are employed does not change, only which posts stand empty, and where the wages already fall with the ladder nothing is joined. In his city, run on for 120 months, 62,132 graduates took unskilled posts in the first month, the unskilled rung went from 8.5% to 39.3% staffed in that month, its empty posts at the end from 29,110 to 2,106, the unskilled and diploma wages became one at $17.41k, and the city's healthcare went from 91.5% to 99.4% staffed (0.7.18). The default cities carry unemployment, so it rarely binds in them; a default city is short of college workers all its life, at a median college fill of 0.57, and only a school or a dearer college wage brings them.

Nine schools, and a pipeline

StageTakesProducesCourse
Elementary, Middle, Highchildren and teensa diploma, at the narrowest stage's coverage4 + 3 + 5 yr
Community Collegediploma-holderscollege graduates24 mo
Universitydiploma-holdersuniversity graduates48 mo
Medical, Law, Business, Technologyuniversity graduatesa licence for one job type — raises nobody's band84 / 36 / 24 / 48 mo

Three things make an adult study, multiplied not averaged: a free seat, a return (nobody spends four years to earn what they already earn — a medical student's alternative is the best ungated university job), and the money, with tuition a Policy dial between the treasury and the household. Students are in flight: cohorts queue by months remaining, graduate at the front, thin at the adults' own attrition, and are out of the labour supply until they finish — living on savings, a grant worth $525 a month in founding money at the default dial, and since 0.7.19 kept up with prices, and a loan they begin repaying over 114 months the month they graduate. A university built today is graduates in month 49, not this month. Migration can still bring a licensed professional — some share of arriving graduates already hold one — so a small city imports its doctor and a city of eighty thousand cannot import two hundred at any price. That is the handoff to the schools, by arithmetic rather than by rule.

The price of a place

Jerus: "grants its just a menu where you can choose between a fixed amount, or a percentage of last month's surplus, or a % as it is now of living costs, or a % of tuition. and then another slider which is the interest rate for the student loans … and also make it so that you can tweak the price of tuition as well." Since 0.6.9 the Policy tab's Promises → Schools page — the Tuition page renamed — has carried the students' dials on one foot bar: five when it opened, and since 0.7.6 a price for each kind of school besides. The grant is a basis and an amount (TaxPolicy.GrantBasis): a share of the unskilled wage, which was the founding rule and the default until 0.7.19, and which is what "as it is now of living costs" turned out to be; a fixed sum a student, which since 0.7.19 is a real amount kept up with the price index and the default for a new city; a share of last month's surplus, as one pool; or a share of each student's own course fee. Each basis has its own ceiling, and all four are struck by one rule, TaxPolicy.grantBill, that the treasury's bill, the save's re-strike, the students' row and the page all call, so a grant on the wrong thing cannot look right on one screen and wrong on another. Since 0.7.19 the grant follows prices (Jerus: "Grant follows prices"): a new city's is $525 a month in founding money times the month's price index (TaxPolicy.DEFAULT_FIXED_GRANT, at founding the old default's bill to the cent); a save keeps the basis it was played on ("Old saves keep theirs"), and an older save's fixed amount is read as the real amount that pays the same at the load month's index. The wage share had been paying his year-149 city $1,425M a month, $9.72k a student; on the new basis it would have paid $415M, $2.83k a student. The student loan has a rate, 0 to 15%, nothing by default: the Canadian shape, nothing accruing while they study, charged on a graduate's balance and paid with the instalment, so the balance is only ever principal and falls exactly as it did. The interest is the treasury's own revenue line, Student loan interest; the principal still comes back through the treasury's journal. A prisoner's loan freezes with the rest of their debts, no instalment and no interest. And the price of a place is the player's: tuitionScale, 0 to 5× the founding table, read wherever a fee is read.

Since 0.7.6 it is a price per school. Jerus: "have it so that not only can you raise prices but also raise the price for a specific university, and beside the dial it shows the current space, the current students, and the current cost and revenue." The scale is nine, one for each kind of school (TaxPolicy.tuitionScaleOf(type)), in the shape 0.7.4 gave the income taxes: Every school at once sits at the top of the page and moves all nine, and taking it back unstages every kind's own; under it is a row per kind with its own dial and four figures off the model — the places its buildings seat, the students in it, what its staff and buildings cost the treasury this month, and the tuition its students paid. A kind with nothing standing says no school and its dial is greyed. The nine ride the end of the policy array, and a shorter array reads them as the one scale it carried. Building it found the trap 0.7.4 had found for the income tax: the month and the load path told every school the first kind's price, which would have put nine prices back to one on every load, and Game.tellTheSchoolsTheirPrices hands each kind its own now.

At the defaults nothing moves, bit for bit. At ×3 the poverty trap the Education header describes is back: a one-university city at five years has 1,718 students and 69% of its diploma-holders willing at ×1, 1,173 and 26% at ×3, and 1,756 and 90% at ×0. The default playtest has never built a school, so -Dplaytest.schools=true builds them, and eight seeds at tuition ×3, the grant half of tuition and loans at 5% all ran clean, the treasury taking $1.1–3.8M a month of interest and fewer people studying. On the way the schools' books were found never to have been restored on the load path — the sixth sighting of that gap, fixed.

12

Tax, policy & GDP

Four base rates since 0.7.4 — on profit, on sales, on wages and on property — and everything else is an offset in rate points from one of them. Resolution happens in one place, clamped, so no screen can quote a rate the city does not collect.

LeverDefaultMaxOffsets
Profit tax15%60%fifteen sectors; the bank is charged retail's rate
Sales tax15%60%fifteen sectors
Wage tax15%60%four education wage bands
Property tax1.5%/yr10%/yrfifteen sectors, plus farmland relief; quoted annually, charged monthly

Beside them: the minimum wage, the policy interest rate, tuition's split and since 0.6.9 its price, the pension contribution and replacement rates, the EI premium and benefit rates, the students' grant on its basis and the student loan's rate, since 0.6.8 the health fee's scale and the health premium, since 16 September the transit fare, and since 0.7.14 the city's fund's dial — each a dial with a stated maximum. The rollover and what happens when the bank fails are the treasury's settings, on Finances (the city's borrowing, the bank). The fare is the one that is a price rather than a charge: it is set per ride, $2.50 by default and $50 at the absurd end, and charged forty journeys a month, because a rider is a person and for its first day it was charged once a month against a constant that says a single journey. Jerus caught it by reading the number.

The policy rate reaches 100% since 0.7.2 (DebtManager.MAX_POLICY_RATE 1.00; it stopped at 25% before), a bound that never binds in play and is there so a typo cannot set 2,500%. The slider has chips at 0, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 10, 15, 20, 30, 50, 75 and 100. The Taylor rule beside it is printed as the rule says it (ruleRate()), and where the dial's bounds cut it the page says so: "the rule would set −4.60%, the dial stops at 0.00%" was the default seed's last month on 22 September, on −3.1% inflation, on 28 September it read −0.35% on −0.2%, and on 1 October −0.33% on −0.2%. Since 0.7.0 the dial is the price of money, and it has three companions on the same page: the target the rule aims at, a dial since 0.7.4, the holdings dial and the advances ceiling; all four are told in the central bank.

Setting them: five pages, one proposal

"Income tax" is not one tax. It is three — on profit, on value added, and on every payroll — and until 0.7.4 the three shared one dial, and the screens used to be split by where the modifier lived rather than by what the money is. They are split by the tax now: Everything · Profit · Sales · Wage · Property. Jerus: "sure you can choose sale tax for manufacturing, but what about just increasing sale tax for all at the same time? currently that's a hassle, i want to be able to do that for every type of tax, even wage tax." Since 0.7.4 each of the three has a base of its own, every offset rides its own tax's base, and each tax page's top dial is that base; the old city rate is Every tax at once on the Everything page, which moves all three and shows the three side by side. Splitting them found the load path reading the old single rate after the policy array, which would have put a split back together on every load, and the Government tab printing the bank's profit tax at the income rate while the city charged it retail's.

[−] ══════●══════ [+] 20.25% 0.00% to 60.00% · one step is 0.25 points · it is 20.00%

Drag to cross the range; it snaps hard on release, and each button is worth exactly one step. The step is a quarter point everywhere except property, which gets a twentieth — the same step has a very different bite: 20.00% to 20.25% of income is one part in eighty of what income tax raises, 1.00% to 1.25% of assessed value is one part in four.

Since 0.7.6 it is every dial's shape, not the tax pages' own. Jerus: "all the dials in the policy in the promises section, make them a slider with steps, and a + − on the ends, basically i think it's better if you create an object or class of slider". There had been three shapes of one control, and there is one class now, ui/Ladder: a minus, a slider that snaps to the step, a plus, the reading in the accent colour when it is off what the city charges, and an ends line — x to y · one step is z · it is w. The sensitivity is the step and nothing else, because a drag coarser than a click would be two dials in one. The reading follows the thumb and the change waits for the release, because restaging on every pixel redraws the screen under the pointer. On it are every tax dial and the farmland relief, the wage floor and the policy rate, the target, the holdings and the ceiling, which apply at once and keep their chips as shortcuts, the pension's two, EI's two, both health dials, the tuition split, every school's price, the grant and the loan, and the fare on Services; each kept its key, range, step and formatter exactly, and PolicyCheck did not notice. The standing subsidies are switches and the currency reform is a choice of three, so neither is a ladder.

Changes stage and apply together. A budget is not one rate moved in isolation, so several dials can be moved and applied as one proposal; the foot bar lists them in the order they were made, and a dial dragged back to the live value unstages rather than staging a no-op. Each dial registers its own setter, which is what lets one bar apply a city rate, three sector offsets and the farmland relief without a switch on the key. The foot bar strikes the same arithmetic twice — once at what the city charges, once at what is staged — and says plainly that both figures are models: comparing a model against last month's actual would make a batch that changed nothing read as a swing. The difference is the answer. Since 0.7.23 each dial the bar applies writes a line of its own to the city's decision log at that month, so a proposal of five dials is one flag of five on City History, and a dial set to what it already reads writes nothing (the screen).

The wage page totals what a payslip actually loses — wage tax plus the pension contribution plus the EI premium, and since 0.6.8 the health premium — which no screen in this game had ever added up. The premiums and the contribution are read-only there, with a door to Promises, because a second copy of a lever is how two screens start disagreeing about what the city charges.

What the treasury actually did

The Government tab has a bridge from the budget balance — an income measure — to what the treasury's cash actually did between one press and the next, and until 19 September its last row, Everything else the treasury did, was a residual with a name on it: land, buildings, reserves, capital put into the bank, bonds bought back, on any month the player did one of them. Jerus: "it just says 'everything else' — that should be expandable, cause a lot of times that's where a bunch of important things happen." It opens now. TreasuryJournal records every movement of the city's cash that is neither a budget line nor paper raised or repaid, where it happens and in the player's words — Put capital into the bank, Bought reserves, Bought back a bond, Lent to students, net of repayments, since 0.7.0 Repaid the central bank and Advanced by the central bank (printed), since 0.7.6 land bought out of the vault, since 0.7.14 what the dial paid into the fund and what resolving the bank cost, and since 0.7.22 Sold a demolition's material to the builders — press to press, for the month that ended and the one in progress, both carried in the save. What the lines do not explain prints under them as Not accounted for, and on a month the city borrows it is the first coupon's timing, to the cent. Land and buildings stay on the budget's own line and are deliberately not named twice. Opening the row found that Raised by issuing paper had read $0 on every month the city borrowed, because its counter was cleared before the strike; that the city's own repair bill and the transit fares are on the Government screen and not in the budget's totals, which are journalled by name until they are; and, not fixed that night, that the bank had never paid for the city's paper (see debt). The budget gained its own lines in the batches since — the health premium, student loan interest, the central bank's remittance and the interest on its advances, a Subsidies line the Government tab had never had, since 0.7.11 the premiums the landlords pay to insure their mortgages under revenue and the claims the city pays on them under spending, and since 0.7.14 Transfer from the fund; since 0.7.19 Material escalation on the city's building contracts and since 0.7.22 Overtime on the city's rushed sites, both discretionary and owed to the builders as arrears past the ceiling, with a cancelled order's refund netted against the capital spending — and since 0.7.6 Land bought opens into what was converted, what came out of the vault and what was bought back. And since 0.7.0 every payment the treasury makes goes through one door, Game.treasuryPays(TreasuryLine, amount), which is what lets the central bank's ceiling decide who is paid.

VAT with input credits

Sales tax is a real value-added tax: exports zero-rated with credits still claimable, imports charged at the buyer's rate and fully creditable, the city exempt both ways, residential rent an exempt supply. One deliberate departure from the real thing — the rate follows the producer, not the product — because under a product rate most of the sector dials would be cosmetic.

And the builders' tax is in their quote since 0.7.19 (the builders), so who finally bears it is the real rules'. A business that makes taxable supplies claims the tax on its buildings back as the work is billed, and on its repairs as an input (CRA RC4022; Excise Tax Act ss. 169(1) and 123(1)). The landlords, whose rent is an exempt supply, claim no credit, but get the tax on a new rental home back by a rebate: all of it on purpose-built rental housing, a building of four homes or more held for rent (RC4231; s. 256.2(3.1)), which the Studio and the Low-Rise Apartments are; and on any other new rental home the new residential rental property rebate, 36% of the tax and at most $6,300 a unit, phased out between $350,000 and $450,000 of value, those figures in founding money at the month's price index, so a House, about $454k with its plot at founding, gets about nothing. No rebate reaches a repair. The city's own rebate (RC4034) is the tax coming home, because the city is what collects it, so it has no line; and the bank's branch, an exempt financial service, bears it. Jerus chose "Businesses claim it back", then, after the first measurement, "Both rebates", and on the landlords' repairs still being taxed, "Leave as the law has it". In his year-149 city the landlords' rebate came to $20.13B over the decade, 15.1% of what they built (0.7.19).

Net remittance is allowed to go negative and is not floored: under producer rates a sector buying high and selling low genuinely is owed money, and flooring would quietly turn a rate cut into a partial one. When it happens the screen names who is owed. And the producer pays it — out of its own cash, as it pays the profit tax on its statement's after-tax line. Both used to be collected by the treasury from nobody; closing the money identity found them.

GDP

Measured by expenditure, C + I + G + NX, replacing an income-approach figure that went negative the moment firms started paying interest. Profit is not production. Since 0.7.20 a ratio to it says of GDP, annualised while fewer than twelve months are recorded and of annual GDP after. The screens had already scaled a young city's months to a year; only the words changed, and the Dashboard, which had summed the months there were and called them a year. A founding city's revenue really can be 169.7% of its annualised GDP, as the play-through of 0.7.19 read it in month 7: its revenue is the builders' sales tax on its own orders, large against an economy that is mostly those orders.

C what the households bought over three counters — the grocers', the boutiques', the kitchens' — + rent paid I construction work put in place + the change in inventory as a VOLUME, priced today: the food shelf at fixed weights, the makers' materials, the boutiques' stockroom G utility payroll + the health service's gross cost + the police's NX everything sold abroad − everything landed: scrap, food, materials, machinery, vans, watches, and the railway's fuel

Inventory is measured as a volume rather than a value, because valuing it books every price move as production — a rule that had to be re-learned the day food became thirteen goods, since the obvious way to add kilograms of grain to kilograms of fish is to value each at its own price. The thirteen are aggregated at fixed weights instead, so the figure is still a quantity. The builders' yard is deliberately excluded from the inventory term — work in progress is at contract value and already embodies it, so counting both subtracted the same brick twice — while the materials plant's warehouse is output made and not yet sold, and counts like a mill's. The boutiques' stockroom is the third term, and it was found by a negative month: a Boutique's three months of stock, 960 pieces at $9,000, lands in one month, NX took the landed cost out and nothing said the city still had the goods, so a city that took up shopping reported negative GDP sixty times over a run. Priced at what the shop paid, the arrival nets to zero and the margin is the only thing that scores; sixty negative months became one across eight seeds and 32,040 months. Any good a sector can hold and does not consume within the month belongs in that block the day the good is written. A household's car is not in C: a household turning money into a thing it owns is a portfolio move, like a share. Land sales appear in the government's books and nowhere in GDP: selling a field moves an asset that already existed, and counting it would let a city manufacture growth by trading land with itself. Education's cost is still outside G, which healthcare's and the police's are not, and the transit payroll joined it on the outside when the modes arrived; that is an inconsistency and it is on the list.

And since 0.7.6 it can be drawn in its layers. Jerus: "have it so the gdp graph can be a toggle, and if toggled it switches from line to mountain graph is it? layered, aka showing how much is made up of investments, net exports, government spending, aka breaking it down." The four parts are series of their own — consumption, investment, government, netExports, each with its rule in the year book and deflated the way real GDP is — and a layers chip on City History, the Reports page until 0.7.21, stacks C, I and G from zero with the real GDP line drawn over them, so the gap between the top of the stack and the line is net exports, above it when the city exports more than it lands and below it when not. A stacked area cannot hold a negative layer, and the caption says so. HistoryCheck asserts that the four add up to the month's GDP to the cent. Since 0.7.23 the layers are drawn on City History's new chart, with the rest of its lines (the screen).

Standing subsidies

Any sector can be put under a standing policy that covers its losses exactly, uncapped, month after month. It is booked as a capital contribution, not revenue — the sector's cash rises and its income statement still says it lost money. The city goes overdrawn to pay it, and the overdraft — the central bank's advances since 0.7.0 — is priced as debt, so an over-generous policy bills you through the interest rate; and once the advances have reached their ceiling a subsidy is paid only from cash at or above zero, and what it is refused is owed to the sector as arrears.

The city's fund

Jerus: "Rule plus your hand", and "Home only". Since 0.7.14 the treasury has a fund (TreasuryFund), a pool of its own inside the city: a market book of company shares, the bank's among them, and company bonds; a rescue book of a resolved bank's shares, the city's preferred and its warrants (the bank); and its cash. Its holdings live where everyone else's do, as a city holding on each company's register and a city holder on every bond, and it posts on both books by a rule and, separately, by the player's hand. It never subscribes to a new issue and never buys the city's own paper.

The dial takes 0 to 300% of the year's surplus, nothing by default, paid in once a year at December's close and before the rollover nets the same surplus, so that the two cannot spend it twice; a deficit year saves nothing, and past 100% the rest comes out of the treasury's cash but never takes it below a month of its own spending, the liquidity rule Canada keeps and the rollover already reads. Yearly, because a monthly pay-in would save in the good months of a deficit year. The rule is Norway's Government Pension Fund Global: 70% shares and 30% bonds, never more than 10% of any company, rebalanced when shares pass 74% or fall more than four points under 70%, every order at fair value, and money that does not fit waits as cash, which earns nothing, as the treasury's does. The transfer is Norway's fiscal rule since 2017: 3% a year of everything it holds, a twelfth a month, to the budget as Transfer from the fund, from its cash only — the rule never sells to pay it. The hand places orders at fair value, good for a month, and may sell any share or bond the fund holds, the rescue book's bank shares included, though not the preferred or its warrants; paying in and drawing out are transfers between two government pools and stay off the budget. The fund has its own area under Finances, The city's fund, with a Holdings page and a By hand page.

At the default dial of nothing the default seed on 28 September still ended with a fund of $116M, fed by the dividends on the bank shares its one resolution had handed it ($239M of the $270M of dividends it took), and paid the budget $659M over the run; on 1 October it ended worth $724M, fed by the dividends on its rescue book ($2.61bn of the $2.86bn of dividends it took), and had paid the budget $3.63bn, $435M short of the rule's 3% because the transfer comes from its cash alone. On eight seeds at a dial of 100% it was paid $15.3T and held about 94% of it as cash, and the cities ended at 174,264 people on average against 166,717 at nothing (0.7.14).

13

The city's borrowing

Three instruments with genuinely different shapes, priced off their own cash flows rather than off a formula that assumed a bullet — and since 0.7.1 all three off one curve that sits on the policy dial, held by whoever bought them: the households, the central bank, and the bank for the rest. Since 0.7.13 the treasury can roll what falls due without the player's hand. What lends to everybody else is the bank.

InstrumentDurationGranularityShape
T-Bill3–12 months$1Mzero coupon, sold at a discount, one lump at maturity; the short end of the curve, at the dial
Serial bond1–10 years$10Mmonthly coupon on a declining balance, annual principal slices
Term bond10, 20, 30, 40 or 50 years$100Mlow coupon on full face, whole face at maturity
Why the term bond is solved from present value

The old simple-interest premium made issue-then-repurchase an infinite money button — $2,145 of free cash at ten years, rising to $28,059 at thirty. Solving the face from the bond's present value makes the round trip neutral by construction rather than by a patch.

The dial, and the curve on it

The policy rate is the player's dial on the Policy tab, 0% to 100% since 0.7.2, and since 0.7.0 it is the price of money rather than a floor under somebody else's: the commercial bank's spare cash earns it at the central bank, and its shortfalls pay it and a quarter point at the window (the central bank). The city's own borrowing rate is that dial — never less than what the money costs the bank that buys the paper, plus a point, while the bank and the households hold all of it — walked up by the market's read of the city's books, so a dial at 3% and a debt-free treasury with a bank whose money costs it nothing is a city paying 3%. Until 0.7.7 the bank's strain added up to eighteen points on top of that, and it is gone (the bank). Since 0.7.15 the floor is split by who holds the paper, Jerus's reading of the backstop: "if bank is in a pickle, and central bank is at 100% then your debt isnt priced at tge bank, ira proportional". Until 0.7.0 it was the dial less a two-point discount, a named constant its own comment called the one number in the file that did not describe anything real; with it went a city borrowing below its own central bank's rate.

floor = max(MIN_RATE, policy + (1 − s) × max(0, the bank's cost of funds + MIN_MARGIN − policy)) // s: the central bank's share of all the city's paper, notes included, because the bank holds the notes

At s = 0 that is the floor 0.7.14 had, to the bit; at s = 1 the bank's state no longer touches the city's rate. Because the bank's money costs it almost nothing when it is flush, the floor is often the bank's point rather than the dial: on the default seed on 28 September the dial ended at 0.01% and the floor at 1.00%, and on 1 October the same.

Two independent measures move the rest — debt against annual GDP, and debt against annual tax revenue — each a linear ramp to five points of spread and then flat. The city's own overdraft counts as principal, because a city over a million in the red with no bonds was being quoted the floor, and since 0.7.0 so does what it owes its central bank in advances. The cheapest money the market will ever offer is 0.5%. Because face depends on rate and rate depends on face, every quote walks that fixed point six times. The quote is the arithmetic: what the screen shows is what gets booked, down to the rate before and after and the points the ask itself cost. A letter grade — AAA to CCC — reports the city's position within the market's own band rather than against typed-in thresholds. Since 0.7.14 a reloaded city's rate is the live city's to the bit: the debt market strikes it from four inputs, and one of them, the overdraft, used to be struck again on load from cash that had moved after the month's strike, up to a basis point away, so the save carries the four and the rate.

The curve. Jerus: "the short term rates, aka the one you choose, those should be basically the tbill rate, the others change just as in real life." Since 0.7.1 every price of the city's own paper — the quote, the issue, the buyback, the market value and the borrow page, which lists the curve one row per maturity — is read off one function at the paper's maturity, DebtManager.priceAt(debt, months):

rate(months) = floor + credit spread + termPremium(months) − compression(months) termPremium nothing to a year · linear to the ten-year entry · linear between entries · flat past fifty years compression what the central bank's holdings take off it — see the central bank
The term premium, in points over the dial — DebtManager.TERM_PREMIUM_10Y … 50Y, provisional, Jerus's numbers to settle
Maturity10 yr20 yr30 yr40 yr50 yr
Premium0.500.901.151.351.50

A note carries no premium, so the short end is the floor and getRate() — what every screen means by the city's rate, the header's RATE tile's tooltip (the strip's city line until 0.7.21) and the year book's interestRate — stays exactly that; a five-year serial carries 48/108 of the ten-year entry. The credit spread is the same at every maturity, so a hike moves the whole curve in parallel and a city that over-borrows pays more at every point of it, and paper already on the books keeps its coupon and its months, so locking in fifty-year money before a hike is a real decision. Term loans are issued only at ten, twenty, thirty, forty or fifty years (LongTermBond.MATURITIES), at home and in dollars: any other term is refused in words — "Term loans are issued at 10, 20, 30, 40 or 50 years." — and the Finances chips step by ten. Issue and buyback read the same function, so RestructureCheck's round trip is still neutral by construction, and CreditCheck's section 11 walks the rest: a debt-free note at exactly the dial, each maturity the dial plus its entry, the interpolation, a hike and a debt both parallel, a 25-year request refused and booking nothing, and a twenty-year bond bought back the same month for what it raised, bar the issuance cost.

Issuance costs $12k fixed plus a 0.75% underwriting spread, taken off the proceeds and never rolled into the face — otherwise the bond would be worth more than it raised the instant it was signed. Buyback pays cash only and refuses to overdraw, because borrowing at the market rate to retire cheaper paper is strictly worse; since 0.7.1 it pays at the curve's rate for the months the bond has left, and it pays every holder its share, where until 0.7.0 it paid nobody at all and a dollar bond's price left the treasury for nowhere on the books. A city that runs out of cash no longer sells its bank an emergency note; since 0.7.0 its central bank advances the gap, up to a ceiling the player sets (the central bank). When the treasury cannot pay for an order, the Build screen offers two things: since 0.7.10 first a twenty-year bond (Game.BUILD_BOND_YEARS) whose face is solved so that the cash it brings covers the gap (Game.quoteLongBondForCash()), because a building outlives either loan, and then the six-month note it always offered (Game.BUILD_NOTE_MONTHS), each with its rate, face, cash, monthly cost, cost in all and what happens at the end. In a probe on 0.7.10, a Standard city buying its water plant on the note alone went D$16.2M overdrawn when the note fell due, and on the sized bond never went below D$0.53M and never drew an advance; NewGameCheck's section 11 asserts the sizing at every maturity.

Who holds the paper

Jerus: "yes households should be able to hold." Since 0.7.1 every domestic bond says who holds it — the principal held by the households and by the central bank, the bank whatever is left — and the split is what makes a bond market rather than a loan. At the settle after an issue the households go first, a fifth of it for every point it yields over the deposit rate and never more than half, out of savings past the cushion; the bank buys the rest and is the residual buyer; the central bank never buys at issue, because buying from its own treasury is printing, which is the advances' line — bar one exception since 0.7.15, the par of its own maturing paper, which it takes again at issue as an add-on (the central bank). Coupons and principal are split by the shares struck before the payment: the bank's the way they always went, the households' into savings as untaxed investment income, the central bank's destroyed and remitted. A household short of money sells its paper back to the bank's desk at the month's market value before its dollars and its shares, and a leaver sells on the way out; since 0.7.15 a central bank buying past what the bank holds takes the households' paper by the same door. Sent home by the playtest's -Dplaytest.borrowAtHome, the households took 38% of each issue at the settle and one at the cap of half, were paid $6–92M of coupons over a run and sold back in 15 to 239 months, every sale a household short of cash (0.7.1). HoldersCheck proves each of those, and fails with a residual if the dollar buyback's audit line is deleted; its ninth section has the central bank at the whole of the paper buying the households' once the bank's is gone.

The discount accretes. A term bond's discount — face less what it raised — used to be booked as one month's interest at the settle: $60M of it in one month on seed 0 sent home, taxed, and 45% of it payable to savers. Since 0.7.1 each bond carries its own discountLeft and earns it straight-line over its life, the bank earning its share a month at a time and carrying what it has not yet earned against its book (Bank.unearnedDiscount), so its equity does not move at the settle.

The bank never paid for the city's paper, from at least 0.4.3 until 0.6.11. Every bond and bill the city sells is sold between two presses, and the month snapshotted what the bank owed for it after the top-of-tick clear, so bank.lend() was handed zero for every domestic issue: the paper went onto the bank's book at face, the coupons and the principal came in, and the cash never went out — equity from nothing on one side, the treasury's money from nowhere on the other. The snapshot is taken before the clear now, and what the bank still owes is carried against its pool in the audit and saved, so a city saved between the issue and the settle still pays. A section of BankCheck causes it — $31,000k of paper sold for $30,490.55k, the bank's cash moving by exactly the ordinary month plus that — and fails four ways on the old order. No seed moved, because the playtest's advisor only ever borrowed the cheaper dollars. What the fix uncovered is why the central bank exists: held at a 10% dial, six seeds' treasuries ran dry, and the emergency notes they had lived on free priced at 27–36% once the bank really paid for them and compounded to $193 quadrillion on seed 0 and NaN on seed 6. A free number had been holding a floor.

Rolling what falls due

Jerus: "the game checks whats going to mature next month, and issues what the treasury is lacking … this doesnt take care of the treasury short of cash, thats the central bank thing". Since 0.7.13 the treasury has a setting on both borrow pages with three positions — by hand, same structure and 12-month notes — and a new game founds on same structure; an older save and the bare city the harnesses and the playtest use keep it by hand. Rollover runs at the top of the month, in the gap between presses where a player's own issue lands:

  • What falls due is the principal due next month, piece by piece, at home and abroad, off each instrument's own schedule.
  • Last year's surplus nets it first (Jerus: "Net of last year's surplus"): the last twelve months' budget surplus that no earlier roll has used, capped at the treasury's cash and at what falls due. A deficit year nets nothing, and a ledger makes each year's surplus net once — shared since 0.7.14 with the city's fund, which takes its December share first. Since 0.7.15 the surplus pays the market's part of what falls due before the central bank's par (Jerus: "Surplus pays everyone"), so a city in surplus clears its paper whoever holds it.
  • The rest is issued pro rata, sized to raise the cash it needs (Jerus: "Enough to raise the cash"). In the same structure each piece rolls into its own instrument, term and currency — dollars into dollars abroad, or at home when the window is shut, and a serial's instalment into a new serial of its original term; on 12-month notes everything rolls into a local twelve-month note. Below the smallest issue the model makes nothing is issued and the maturity is paid from cash.
  • A month early, because the proceeds wait a month in the treasury: pre-funding, which Canada's own rule asks for — liquidity for at least a month of net projected cash flows, refinancing included.

It does not cover a treasury short for its spending; the central bank's advances still do that. And rolling for cash capitalises interest: paper sells under its face, so an issue sized to raise the maturity's cash has a larger face than the paper it repays, and the old paper's interest is borrowed into the new principal at every roll. Face for face was measured and Jerus chose cash. The default and autopilot runs were line for line 0.7.12's, because their cities owe nothing at a maturity or net it whole; on a Lean founding seven seeds of eight rolled and repaid, $1.68B of face for $1.26B, and seed 0 spiralled — its twenty-year paper raised 0.34 to 0.59 of its face as the rate climbed to 26.8%, each roll was 1.7 to 2.9 times the face of the last, and it ended owing $476.6T with the central bank owed $370.7T and 2,423 people (0.7.13).

Nothing limits the paper at home

The price of the city's paper stops at the dial plus ten points of spread and the term premium; the bank buys whatever the households leave, funded at a window with no cap; debt service is a promise, paid past the advances ceiling; and only abroad does a window shut. Lean seed 0's $476.6T was that, and so are the spirals of 0.7.14 and 0.7.15 — a held 25% city borrowing from its bank to buy the bank's preferred, and five of eight Insane cities. The holdings dial at 100% makes the central bank the buyer when nobody else is; it does not limit what the city can sell. A market limit — a buyer's refusal, or a debt-service rule at home — is on the list, and Jerus's to design.

Borrowing in somebody else's money

USD versions of all three instruments, treasury only. A foreign bond holds its face and coupon in dollars; every public getter answers in local money at today's rate, so a devaluation makes the debt dearer everywhere in the game at once without a call site knowing. Priced on exports, not GDP — the only dollars a city has ever earned came from selling something abroad: a 2% world base, deliberately below the domestic floor (that is the trap), plus a solvency term on dollar debt as years of exports and a service term on next year's bill, capped at 16%. Because the measure is taken in local money, a devaluation worsens the ratio, raises the premium, and makes the next bond dearer — Mexico 1994 and Argentina 2001 without a special case.

The world's paper has a curve since 0.7.2: foreignCurveRate(months) is the world's base plus the country premium plus the same term-premium table, so a dollar issue prices by maturity and a dollar bond is valued at its remaining months on it — the dial moving no longer moves a dollar bond's value, and the country premium moving does. On the way the dollar quote was found to be a money pump: it scaled the service the city already owed and left the new bond's own out, so a thirty-year dollar round trip netted the city $7.5M over eight rounds. It prices its own coupons into the premium now, by a fixed point (quoteForeignRate(Debt, months), walked by Game.selfPricedForeignRate()), and RestructureCheck's dollar round trip, which fails on 0.7.1, is neutral by construction. A dollar bond bought back is declared leaving the country.

The window shuts on conditions the player can see coming: 14 years of exports owed, service past 45% of exports, dollars owed and nothing sold abroad, or five years after a default. Existing paper still falls due. At issue the player chooses to convert and spend or to hold the dollars as reserves. Default tears every foreign bond off the books, no cash moves, the ratios read as perfect — and nobody lends a defaulter a dollar for five years, with a scar on the price that outlasts the closed window. It also fires on its own when the treasury goes a full year of tax revenue into the red with dollars falling due — and since the central bank advances every shortfall at the top of the month, only on a month whose own bills, a balloon falling due, come to more than a year of revenue; whether the rule should read the advances is on the list.

Since 0.7.13 the land office borrows dollars too: a plot paid for out of the vault that the vault cannot cover opens a funding page offering a twenty-year dollar bond or a six-month dollar note sized to the dollar gap (quoteForeignForCash()), whose dollars are held in the vault and the plot bought from them (land); a young city's first dollar loan for land can shut the window for its next one, by the rule that shuts it on dollars owed with nothing sold abroad. And since 0.7.14 an Insane city is founded owing a twenty-year dollar bond at 3% for the ground it starts on (founding a city).

14

The bank

Every loan in the city is the Commercial Bank's money, and since 0.7.7 the bank is run as a business: it prices a loan from what the loan costs it, chooses what it pays its savers, holds a capital target of its own and pays its owners what it does not need. Since 0.7.8 a sector defaults a slice at a time, since 0.7.11 the landlords borrow on insured mortgages, since 0.7.12 a firm that cannot pay ends the way a firm does, since 0.7.14 a bank that fails passes to the city, and since 0.7.19 every deposit reaches it and its branches answer to their customers.

What was asked for

Jerus: "lets make banks realistic, and remember, its a business, it wants to make money." The reading of his 0.7.5 city that came before it found the bank deepening both of that city's crises without starting either: a strained bank added up to eighteen points to every rate in the city, which caused the defaults, the write-offs and the lost equity that strained it further. The plan was three batches — the price, the capital, the tab — anchored on the figures of Canada's big banks, and 0.7.7 to 0.7.9 built them. Of its capital he said "the bank chooses."

The bank is somebody
