Every loan in the city — to a sector, to a household, and whatever of the city's own paper the households and the central bank do not hold — is the Commercial Bank's money: one building, 29 jobs a branch, its own tab, and a trading desk that trades on the exchange's books like anybody else; since 0.7.12 it also underwrites every business's bonds, and holds them when it chooses to. Its capacity is the tighter of two limits, what its capital allows and six times everything the city has banked with it, and how much of that its book uses is its strain. Until 0.7.19 the second was six times the savings its branches could reach, 250,000 a branch in founding money; in Jerus's year-149 city $1.24 trillion was banked and its 24 branches reached $6.0B of it, and the bank funded an $86.5B book at the central bank's window while the savings earned the deposit rate on money it could not lend. He chose "Drop it: online banking", and every deposit reaches the bank wherever its counters are. Past its capacity the bank does not refuse, and whatever it lends that its own money and the city's deposits do not fund it borrows at the central bank's window — abroad, until 0.7.0 — and what that money costs goes into the price of whatever it lends, where until 0.7.7 its strain added a premium ramping to 18 points on every rate in the city. A city with no bank is simply a bank with no capacity, and since 0.7.7 it is priced the way any loan is, with the window's money as its funding: Jerus's decision was that a city with no bank yet pays "normal" prices, not a punitive one. Branches used to build themselves through the retail advisor when strain passed 70%, and close when their book had not kept their staff for two years.

Since 0.7.19 a branch answers to its customers. Jerus: "bank branch i think should be customers perhaps, but make it be sustainable even if just account fees, aka one branch maintence and operating costs should be less than the revenue it makes of fees for that specific branch, that should always be true." The first branch is the city's charter ("First branch exempt"): it never closes on the rule, and it pays its staff and its repairs but not its template's operating cost of 190 founding thousands a month ("Exempt first branch"), because a founding village of 59 fee-paying households pays $708 a month for a branch that costs $180,800, and with the upkeep a village's charter ran through its founding capital by month 119 to 128 in six of the eight default cities. Another branch opens through the retail advisor only while there are more than 16,000 customers for each branch standing (Bank.CUSTOMERS_PER_BRANCH, TD's clients per branch: about 16 million through more than 1,000 branches), the month's account fees would cover every branch with it, and the city could staff it; never while the bank is under its minimum. A later branch pays its staff, its repairs and the operating cost, and branches the month's fees do not cover close that month, as many at once as it takes, never the charter. The rule reads last month's cost, so a month whose wages rise can leave a branch short once and the next month closes it (Jerus: "Accept the lag"): in the eight cities that built schools it happened in 11 of the 13,646 months that stood more than one branch, the least cover 0.958. So the fees cover the branches as the rule last read them, not in every month without exception. A default city keeps its charter all its life: at 0.7.19 it ended with 79,513 customers to its one branch, and on 1 October it opened no other in 4,005 months. In Jerus's city, run on for 120 months, the bank owed the window nothing where it had owed $73.2B, and no branch's fee cover fell under 1.120 (0.7.19).

The bank lends to households, for cars and for a short month, and the audit already carried both legs of that: the cash leaving the bank and the debt arriving on the household balance each other with nothing new written. A write-off costs the book, not the cash.

Nothing borrows below what the money costs

For most of the project the bank could be made to lend at a rate below what it was paying for the funds. Three separate mechanisms did it — the city's bond floor, the business desk's spread over a risk-free rate, and a carry trade that borrowed abroad to buy domestic paper — and none of them was wrong on its own terms. The bank simply had no veto.

cost of funds = depositRate × (1 − wholesaleShare) + windowRate × wholesaleShare // the window: the dial and a quarter point since 0.7.7, a point from 0.7.0 any rate = max(riskFree, costOfFunds + MIN_MARGIN) + its own premium // MIN_MARGIN = .01, one point; every rate until 0.7.7, the city's paper since

One rule, three call sites until 0.7.7, and the city's paper alone since. It is blended, not marginal — the first cut used whichever source the last dollar came from, and the floor flipped between 1.9% and 13.6% across twelve consecutive months. And it is struck once, at the close of the month, and carried in the save: the live path priced mid-month and the load path priced at the end, so a city reloaded from disk quoted a different rate than the one it had just been running at — 0.0135 against 0.0133, which is the kind of divergence a harness catches and a player never would.

Sixteen seeds, before and after

Bank failures 5.0 → 0.0 median. Recapitalisation $3.8bn → $0. Closing equity $6.6bn → $9.1bn. Wholesale funding 24–47% → 0–8% of income. Months run at a loss 44–58% → 3–14%. Every seed lifetime-profitable, and the "gentler financing" calibration was not touched to get it.

The obvious next step — make the bank a real intermediary, funding domestic investment out of domestic savings — was measured and abandoned. The bank lends 1.87% of its deposits at home and runs at 31–49% of the capacity it already has. The city's savings are on the order of two hundred thousand times what its productive sector wants to borrow. The bank is demand-limited, not funding-limited, and no amount of deposit-gathering changes that. On the default seed on 28 September it ended with $293bn deposited and $131M lent, 13% of its capacity, and on 1 October with $260.5bn deposited and $1k lent, at 6%.

What a loan costs

Since 0.7.7 a loan is priced the way a bank prices one, as the sum of four costs (Bank, WHAT A LOAN COSTS): the funds-transfer price, what the next dollar costs it for the loan's term — the policy rate while it holds spare reserves, which would otherwise earn the dial at the central bank, the window's rate while it borrows there, blended by how it is funded, and the curve's term premium for the maturity; the running costs, a trailing year of payroll and upkeep over the larger of its book and what its capital could carry, so a young bank is not charged its whole payroll on an empty book; the expected loss of a sound book through the cycle, BASE_LOSS_RATE 0.4%, about what Canada's big banks provision in a normal year; and the capital charge, the equity the loan ties up at the return the market prices the bank's shares on — 12.5% at the defaults, the world's rate and the three-point premium over the 40% payout (Equity.requiredYield()) — over what that money would cost as debt. Prime is the four for a sound business borrowing for thirty-six months. The carry trade pays the three without the loss; a household pays the four at its own weight plus its spread for the months of income it owes; a business's own risk and a landlord's insured mortgage are below.

prime = funds-transfer price + running costs + expected loss + capital charge // the default seed's last month on 28 September: 0.12 + 1.06 + 0.40 + 2.04 = 3.62% on a dial of 0.01% // ...and on 1 October: 0.12 + 0.86 + 0.40 + 2.04 = 3.43%, each part as the playtest rounds it, on the same dial

The expected loss is a constant because the bank's own record, tried twice, was a disaster twice: a whole sector was one borrower in the model then, so the record was lumpy by construction, and a five-year window priced prime at 77.9% in month 12 of seed 0. In calm years on 0.7.7's eight seeds prime ran 1.7 to 2.0 points over the dial, where Canada's runs 2.2; the cities grew more slowly in their first century and ended about 9% smaller, and the bank failed 4 times against 19.

What savers get, and the fees

Since 0.7.7 the bank chooses what it pays its savers — Jerus: "it can pass on less or more if it wants" — and it chooses by how it is funded: 0.35 of the dial when it is flush with reserves, because its next deposit would only earn the dial at the central bank less the cost of the account, rising toward 0.90 while it borrows at the window, because every deposit it finds saves it the window's rate, and moving a sixth of the way a month, so most of a move reaches savers inside a year and none of it in one step. It never pays them more than the smaller of what its interest margin leaves after payroll and upkeep and the savers' own share of that margin. The second bound was the gate's catch: the bank counts the city's savings as deposits without holding them, the one stated simplification in its header, and held at 10% the first finished build paid its savers every dollar it earned on them and failed 184 times against 0.7.6's 78. Over the eight seeds savers got 0.37 to 0.44 of the dial on average, where real banks pass on about 0.4. On the default seed on 28 September the bank passed on 0.29 of it on average, its margin holding its savers under the rate it chose in 3,157 months of the run, and on 1 October 0.19, held in 3,221 months.

It replaced two rules that are history now. From the bank's founding it paid savers 45% of what it earned in interest (DEPOSIT_PASS_THROUGH), and bid higher only when it was short of deposits; and since 0.7.3 it quoted no more than it charged, because a founding bank's first month paid savers 45% of everything it earned, the placement of its own capital included, over $5.7k of deposits, which read as 7,567% a year and would have put the households' spending at its floor in month 3 of every seed. That cap bound in 17 to 236 months of each default run. Both went with 0.7.7.

Fees, since 0.7.7: every housed household pays an account fee of $12 a month at founding prices, indexed to the cost of living, on its own line of the household books and declared to the audit, and orphans, prisoners and the unhoused pay nothing; and new lending pays 1%, out of a business loan's proceeds, grossed up so that the firm still covers its shortfall, and added to a household's or a car's loan. On 0.7.7's seeds the running costs were 0–7% of the bank's revenue against a real bank's 49–60%, and fees 0–6% against about 39%, because its payroll is tiny against what it lends; that was left as measured. Since 0.7.19 the account fees are also how the bank counts its customers, the households paying the month's fee, and what every branch past the charter must be covered by (above).

Its capital, and what it does with its profit

Since 0.7.8 the bank keeps its capital as a business does:

  • A loss allowance in IFRS 9's two stages: a year's expected loss on a sound borrower and the lifetime loss on one in trouble — a sector's firms past 0.9 of their assets, a family past half its credit ceiling — with write-offs drawn against it first and the provision the month's move in it. Its loans sit on its balance sheet net of it.
  • Its own capital target: the 8% minimum plus the larger of Basel's 2.5-point conservation buffer and the worst year of provisions it has lived through, capped at the whole Basel stack of 8.5 points (MAX_BUFFER) — uncapped, one restructure put the target at 48–108% and stalled a city at 8,348 people. A young bank aims at 10.5% and one that has lived through a crisis at up to 16.5%, and the top of its band is 2.5 points over that.
  • A payout rule: nothing under its target, 45% of its profit after tax inside its band (RBC paid 43%), and past the top of the band the excess as well, a twelfth a month. 0.7.7's bank paid out almost nothing, because its dividend was capped at its cash, and sat on $26.6–50.5bn of equity against a book of $1.7–10bn.
  • Lending that tightens with its capital: free at or over its target; between the minimum and the target a borrower's debt may grow at most 1% × x/(1 − x) a month, where x is how far its capital stands from the minimum to the target — nothing at the minimum, 1% halfway; under the minimum only what keeps its borrowers going, a business's interest reserve and a family's month of interest. The families' credit reads the bank's state for the first time.
  • Its own shares issued only under its target and bought back only with capital over it, and booked as capital, not as trading income — 0.7.7 had booked $8.2bn of them as $8.65bn of desk profit on seed 0 — and its desk buys other companies' shares only while the bank would still hold its target with them on its books at the weight Basel gives a trading book.
  • A leverage ratio since 0.7.11: equity of at least 3% of everything it has lent, whatever that weighs (LEVERAGE_RATIO_MIN, Basel III's), which floors every requirement above — the minimum, the target, the payout, the two spare-capital limits and the lending rule. It came with the insured mortgages, which weigh nothing: without it the bank paid out the equity they never asked of it, grew thin and fell back into its old branch trap, and with it and two other fixes the failures with no loan loss went from 72 to none.

On 0.7.8's eight seeds, in its growth years, the bank held 19.2% against a 16.5% target, earned 13.0% on its equity (RBC 16.3%), paid out 76% of its profit and provisioned 0.41% of its loans a year (RBC 0.43%), and its worst year was 38–109% of its weighted book, so its target sat at MAX_BUFFER in every seed. On the default seed on 28 September, over its 216 growth years of 333, the median year held 63% against a target of 15.5%, earned 6.1% on its equity and paid out 82% of its profit; on 1 October, over 259 growth years, it held 41.6% against 16.5%, earned 18.5% and paid out 99.7%.

Business credit

A separate hierarchy, because a business loan must never charge the city's cash. Interest reaches the treasury only through the sector's income statement. Loans are interest-only bullets over 36 months, fixed for all of them at the rate they were written at, originated automatically to cover a hole and, since 0.7.8's pricing, for buildings too; a landlord's homes are on mortgages instead. Until 0.7.7 rates ran the city's rate + 1% to 8% on debt-to-assets, the base never under the bank's cost of funds plus a point, plus a point for each prior write-down up to three — on the default seed on 22 September, with the city's note at 1.00%, the spotless sectors with little debt borrowed at 2.00%, a leveraged landlord with no write-down at 7.40%, and a sector with three or more write-downs at 5.00%. Since 0.7.7 they sit on prime, and since 0.7.8 on the default curve below.

The underwriter leaves a gap. It will lend to 0.9× assets; the default point is 1.5×. They used to be the same number, so the desk parked every borrower exactly on the write-down line. Until 0.7.8 a sector past 1.5× was written down whole to 0.6× and blocked from borrowing — twelve months the first time, twenty-four the second, thirty-six after that, a ban that also stops its investment advisor expanding on credit — and over 4,000 months that pair of changes took write-offs from $25.8bn to $1.35bn. Since 0.7.8 only a sector with nothing left is written down whole, and the rest of its trouble is a slice at a time.

There was no concentration limit, and a fixture found the hole by accident on 17 September: Materials built its first plant, had to buy a fleet of lorries as well as the plant, borrowed $57,939 from a bank with $50,292 of equity — thirty-eight per cent of the whole book in one name — and defaulted in month 70, taking the bank's equity to minus $2,628. Neither half is a bug in the bank: a sector that buys vehicles as well as machines is a riskier borrower, which is the mechanic. And 0.7.2 found the hole again from the other side: on the new path Manufacturing borrowed $74M against a bank whose equity was $30M, for a Fabrication Works that loses money before interest, and was written off at month 166 and its restructured remainder at 178 in four seeds of the eight — all of the rise in the ensemble's bank failures, 8 to 19. From 0.7.8 on, one sector holding 30–73% of the book was under every failure the bank had, and 0.7.12 answered it with a price rather than a limit (below).

A sector is many firms

As 0.7.8 was first built, the bank failed 92 times on the default eight against 0.7.7's 4, and the trace said why: a whole sector was one borrower, and when Real Estate or Manufacturing restructured, the bank lost 60% of everything that sector owed in a single month, 37–185% of its weighted book in its worst year. Jerus was offered three ways on and chose the third, "go for option C": a sector stands for many firms, and only its weakest part goes bust, a slice at a time. How many is Merton's structural model, the basis of Moody's KMV:

PD(L) = N( ln(L / 1.5) / σ ) // a year; L the sector's debt over its assets, 1.5 INSOLVENCY_TRIGGER h = 1 − (1 − PD)1/12 // the month's share of its debt whose firms default σ = ASSET_VOLATILITY 0.25 // the one new number, Jerus's to settle; the literature puts it at 20–35%

So 1.5 is where a firm defaults. The sector's own books carry the common shock and σ is the spread of fortunes among its firms, which averages out over many of them, so the flow is deterministic. Each month the defaulting share of its debt is written off pro rata across its loans and bonds at each instrument's own loss, and the firms keep their plant; its leverage falls, so next month's hazard is smaller. Only a sector with nothing left, or one still past the line that nobody will lend to after a write-down, is written down whole by the old rule, and only that backstop goes on its record, with the surcharge and the ban. The allowance reads the same curve, staged firm by firm. A loan is priced by it: prime, plus a loan's loss given default times PD(L) less what prime already carries, plus a point for each write-down on its record up to three, plus since 0.7.12 the capital its book's concentration asks of it — at the leverage the loan would leave the borrower at, a project's building counted, and read on the borrower's last quarter (STATEMENT_MONTHS 3), as a bank reads statements, while the defaults read the month, because firms fail on what they actually owe. A sound borrower pays prime, one at the watch line 0.11 points over it, one at 1.2 times its assets 4.25 and one at the default point 12.1.

It took four attempts to stand up. Pricing by the curve cut lending past 1.0 by two thirds; Basel's large-exposure rule, with the rest of a big loan syndicated to banks abroad on Jerus's choice, sent 99% of business lending abroad, ran prices to 3.9 times founding and still failed the bank 101 times, and was deleted, because a sector is many firms and Basel's rule is for one firm or a group tied by control, not an industry; reading a borrower on its quarter was the best single fix of the four. It shipped at 39 failures, and under every one of them one sector held 30–73% of the book. A failing sector's buildings are sold to the builders for their material (the private sector).

How a firm that cannot pay ends

0.7.12 put a bond beside the loan (the owners, the bonds and the exchange) and found, partway through, Luxury Retail's late collapses running on an interest-free overdraft: a till allowed below zero, owing nobody, for hundreds of months. Everything after that was about how a firm that cannot pay ends, and each answer is a real rule that Jerus chose:

  • Can't pay means default: beside the balance-sheet test the model had, the cash-flow test of Canada's Bankruptcy and Insolvency Act, s. 2. A short month recalls its dollars abroad, sells the other sectors' bonds it holds and borrows, and what it still cannot pay defaults a slice the size of the part it cannot pay.
  • Credit lines stay open: the capital rule rations growth — project loans and new mortgages — and working capital, a short month's cover, the interest reserve and a rollover, is honoured up to the 0.9 ceiling and the default point, as banks did in 2008 and in March 2020. The first cut treated a short month's cover as growth and set off a credit crunch.
  • Interim financing: after the write-down, what is still unpaid is lent as a loan that ranks ahead of all other debt (InterimLoan; Canada's CCAA s. 11.2, and §364 in the United States), and nothing is forgiven on that path; a sector still past the line after the write-down finds nobody to lend and goes to the backstop, and a ban stops growth lending, not what keeps a firm running.
  • Nothing past the default point: no door lends to a sector whose leverage, read on the quarter, is past 1.5.

Concentration is priced, not capped, which was Jerus's choice. The bank holds Basel's IRB capital against each sector's loans, with a correlation that rises with how concentrated its book is across sectors, allocated to each sector by the Euler rule and so into its loan rate; the single-industry multiplier is Basel III's 1.25, set for large financial institutions and kept and flagged. And since 0.7.12 each instrument recovers what it recovers in the world: 75% of a bank loan and 45% of a bond. Against 0.7.11 on eight seeds, failures went 115 → 1 in the default setup and 265 → 9 on autopilot, the money the city put into its bank $134.7B → $3.1B and $228.8B → $2.0B, and the money created by forgiven overdrafts fell from $19.9B partway through the batch to $0.095B, all of it the backstop's. What still failed the bank was the young one on its $32M of founding capital, before month 1,000, when Manufacturing or Automotive went. On the default seed on 28 September 1,535 sector-months defaulted for want of cash over the run, 1,276 of them shells — sectors holding no plant that still owed something — which nothing winds up; on 1 October 663 did, 616 of them shells.

The landlords take a mortgage

A trace of 0.7.10 found what had stopped the cranes. The landlords financed fifty-year buildings on three-year interest-only bullets: on autopilot seed 0, months 2,280 to 2,490, their maturing loans went unrolled, the till fell to minus $6.8B, every new building had to borrow that hole on top of its cost, and nothing was built for about 200 months with homes 37% short. Held at 10%, the test asked gross rent to cover 1.25 times the interest on the whole cost, about 14.5% a year, where a home earned 8–9%. Jerus chose "Mortgages", on "CMHC (Canada)"'s rulebook, and "Insured by the city". Since 0.7.11 a landlord's residential building is bought with at least 15% of its own funds and a mortgage for the rest (Mortgage): at most 85% of the cost, a level payment over 40 years at a rate fixed for 10 and renewed at the day's rate, and the lender's test is the building's net operating income — rent less maintenance and property tax — at least 1.20× the payment. The city insures it: the landlord pays CMHC's premium, 5.00% of the loan and 0.75% more for forty years, added to the loan and paid to the treasury as revenue, and when a landlord is written down the treasury pays the bank what came off insured balances, as a promise. The bank weighs an insured mortgage at nothing and sets nothing aside for it, and prices it at its ten-year funds-transfer price and its running costs, with no expected loss, plus the capital the leverage ratio ties up, 0.41 to 0.64 points at the defaults. Everything else keeps its thirty-six-month loan and its 1.25 interest test.

The stall went: through seed 0's old window homes rose from 35,141 to 36,464 and the till stayed at or above zero. The 10% city stayed small, which is the honest answer at a 10% real rate: the 1.20 test on a forty-year paydown asks net rent of 12.2% of the cost a year. The insurance took in $3.9B of premiums over the eight default seeds against $2.1M of claims, and held at 10% it lost money for the first time, $404M of claims against $56M of premiums. And the bank failed more, 115 times against 42: without the landlords' $6B of fully weighted bullets it was small, $161M of equity at month 2,400 against $1.13B, and the industrial sector that failed it owed five to six times its equity. Jerus shipped it with that open, and the next batch was the bonds. On the default seed on 28 September the landlords wrote 293 mortgages over the run and renewed 879, and the insurance took $185M of premiums against $70k of claims; on 1 October they wrote 370 and renewed 1,110, and it took $208M against $1k.

When the bank fails

A bank that has lost more than it owns has failed, which is one of the shocks that can start a run on the currency. The path from 278 jobs a branch to 29, a deposit limit from $60k to $250k, honest underwriting and an escalating exclusion once took it from 308 failures in 4,000 months to none in seven seeds of eight; what each batch since has done to that count is in the table below. Until 0.7.14 the city could put capital into a failed bank and got nothing for it, and the hole was declared to the audit as absorbed from outside the city. Jerus, on the owners of a failed bank: "City takes the shares … but later bank can dilute by issuing shares... we dont want commercial to be owned by government fully in the long run". Since 0.7.14 the treasury has a setting, When the bank fails: Automatic, which new games found on, or Wait for my button, which older saves and the harnesses' bare city keep. Either way the resolution (Game.resolveBank()) is the same:

  • every common share passes to the city's rescue book, the households', the world's and the desk's alike, and the old owners get nothing, as under the bail-in regime of Canada's deposit insurer and as with Northern Rock in 2008;
  • the city's preferred and its warrants are cancelled, because the hole took them;
  • the city pays the hole and the capital to reopen at one and a half times the minimum, as a promise: the treasury's cash first, and the central bank advances the rest at the next settle, past the ceiling if it must (Jerus: "Central bank advances it");
  • and the bank reopens the same month.

Nothing enters from outside the city any more; a bank waiting for the button carries its hole at the window. The city's stake falls only as the bank sells new shares (Jerus: "New issues only"), which it does when it is under its target and when it repays the preferred: on 0.7.14's default runs the median stake was 72.8% five years after a resolution, 51.0% at ten and 58.8% at twenty-five, and the player can sell the rescue book by hand.

A standing bank under its minimum asks for preferred shares. Jerus wanted "a popup message saying bank wants to issue you shares or something", and since 0.7.14 the inbox carries the bank's offer, on the terms of the United States' 2008 Capital Purchase Program: enough to take it back to its target, but at least 1% and at most 3% of its risk-weighted assets; a cumulative dividend of 5% a year, 9% from the fifth anniversary, paid only out of equity over its target, and no common dividend while any is unpaid; for three years no buybacks and no rise in the common dividend; and warrants on common shares worth 15% of the amount, for ten years. The player accepts or declines, and the bank asks again a quarter later either way (Jerus: "Quarter"); a treasury short of the money is shown the build screen's two funding offers. Each block is repaid at its third anniversary at par with its unpaid dividends, from the bank's capital over its target and the rest by new common sold to the public (Jerus: "Sell new shares to repay"), which dilutes the city's own common, and after the last block the warrants are bought back at fair value. The balance sheet has a third equity line for it, Preferred shares (the city).

On 0.7.14's default runs the city bought 45 blocks for $1.26B and was repaid $1.22B at three years, $996M of it by new shares, with nothing outstanding at the end. Held at 25% the preferred is circular capital: the city borrows from the bank to buy the bank's preferred, every offer is larger than the last, and the eight seeds bought $962T of it with the advances peaking at $20.4 quadrillion; Jerus kept it, "Keep as built", and the limit on the city's own paper would close it. On the default seed on 28 September the bank failed once, in month 442: the city paid $40.9M to resolve it, all of it from its cash, the old owners lost $45.6M at the last price, and the city still held 4.2% of the bank at the end; six preferred offers were accepted for $234M, and none was outstanding at the end. On 1 October it failed once too, and the city took it over in month 664: the resolution cost $36.5M, all of it from the treasury's cash, the old owners lost $69.2M at the last price, and the city still held 62.2% of the bank at the end, never under half; eight preferred offers were accepted for $52.8M, and none was outstanding.

0.7.14's runs part from 0.7.13's only at the first month the city puts capital into its bank, where Jerus's rules replace the old playtest's gift: its player had put up to a quarter of the treasury into any bank under its minimum, every month, for nothing. 0.7.15's default and autopilot runs are 0.7.14's to the byte, and 0.7.16's are 0.7.15's. From 0.7.17 to 0.7.19 the batches measured the default eight beside a city that builds schools and one held at 25%, not on autopilot or at 10%: the schools city went 6 → 4 → 4 → 4, and the city held at 25% 43 → 27 → 16 → 8. The 25% city lived on 0.7.18 on the strain of the deposit cap, which opened branches and brought their owners' capital in, and on the window, where a median seed owed $181.9 trillion; 0.7.19 removed both on purpose, and it ended at 428 people at the median against 8,168, its prices 22% lower and its builders losing money in 3,924 of 3,999 months. Jerus: "Let it fail." From 0.7.19 on the default playtest writes the same eight trace files to the byte: none of the interface batches moved it.

The Bank tab

Jerus: "a redesign of the bank UI info, cause when you click on bank you dont even see all the relevant stuff, lets make banks realistic." Since 0.7.9 the tab's landing answers whether the bank is healthy, and why, without a click: a status sentence with the figure that decides it (Bank.status() — healthy, rebuilding, under its minimum, failed, or no bank); a scorecard of eight — profit this month and over twelve, return on equity against the owners' 12.5%, the capital ratio on a bar against the minimum, its target and the top of its band, credit losses as a share of loans, net interest margin, costs over revenue, what is lent, and deposits; and the rate ladder, from the policy rate through what savers get, the funds-transfer price and prime in its parts to a rung for every borrower, every step labelled in points. Behind it are six pages — Profit, a Balance sheet since 0.7.13, Lending, Funding, Capital & owners and History — and every figure on them is a model getter, because a dozen on the old tab had been recomputed on the screen and several were wrong: per-branch figures in founding money after a reform, a weight table with no term and no desk that did not foot, 99900.0% capital with nothing lent, an alert on the wrong line. Since 0.7.11 the capital figures read the leverage ratio when it is the larger requirement and the ladder has an insured rung. The balance sheet sets this month against a year ago, every line opening into its detail, and splits equity into what was paid in and what was retained so that the two add up to it to the cent; deposits are a memo line beside it, because the model does not carry them as the bank's liability. Retained earnings ended below zero in 27 of 0.7.13's 32 runs, because dividends that return excess capital come out of retained while the city's rescues sit in paid-in, and Jerus kept it so. Since 0.7.14 the landing carries the resolution and the preferred offer, and the balance sheet a third equity line. Since 0.7.19 the Funding page shows What it can lend against, every deposit and the money from abroad, where it showed what the branches reached, and Its branches by their customers: the customers a branch against the 16,000 one needs, a branch's fees this month against what a branch past the first cost last month, the charter's exemption and the lag. Near the line its figures can disagree with its verdict: the rule counts the charter's exemption and the figures beside it do not, so the tab can say a branch would pay beside a figure smaller than the cost printed above it. BankCheck's thirteenth section asserts the arithmetic the tab reads; the play-through of 0.7.19 opened the tab without walking it, so none of this has been checked on a screen.

15

The central bank

Until 0.7.0 the game had a central bank, and it was the rest of the world. The commercial bank placed its spare cash abroad at the world's 2% and funded its shortfalls abroad; the policy dial only floored the lending rate on top, which is why it stopped mattering past 17%; and money was created only from outside — the founding, exports, capital coming in, the bank's wholesale funding — so the price level had nothing at home to answer to. Five batches, 0.6.11 to 0.7.3, brought the marginal role home, and 0.7.15 made it the buyer of last resort for the city's paper. This is what the central bank is, what it holds, what each of its four dials does, and — measured, and said plainly — how little the rate still moves prices.

What was asked for

Jerus: "…we implement a central bank, and basically the central bank would buy gbonds or sell gbonds from thin air … the feds sheet would show how much debt it holds, like debt to itself aka money printing, like QE and QT … and that is the M2 supply or what do you think?" Decided the same night: a floor system, households who can hold the city's paper, and an autopilot beside the dial. Each batch was gated on the whole suite and on the eight seeds, and the design note that set them out is the-central-bank.md.

The books

CentralBank is a balance sheet the player reads, on the Money page under Finances. Assets: advances to the commercial bank at the window; advances to the treasury, its overdraft, which is what "printed" means on the screens; the city's paper it holds, at face; and the vault, read from ForeignAccounts rather than moved there, because the treasury still buys and sells reserves and the currency's code has to see them. Liabilities: reserves and currency, which together are M0. Currency is zero — nobody in this city holds cash outside the bank — and kept so that the day somebody does it has a line. Its equity is what the two sides leave.

Money is made and destroyed here and nowhere else. An advance, interest on reserves and the remittance are money issued; a repayment, the window's interest and the advances' interest are money retired. MoneyAudit sees the central bank as a boundary participant, like the world, and declares both under a scope of its own, so the month's issued less retired is the change in M0 to the cent — the difference between money from thin air and a residual. CentralBankCheck section 1 walks a month with every kind of flow in it and asserts exactly that. M0 is a ledger of what this bank has made and not taken back, not a reading of the commercial bank's till, because money reaches the pools without passing through a central bank — exports, capital, the founding — and the two could not both hold the identity.

M2 is what the public holds: the households' deposits, the sectors', the world's, and currency (Game.getM2()). It grows when the bank lends reserves on or the treasury spends printed money, not when the central bank buys from the bank. Both are series, with a year of each on the Money page and a column each in the year book. On the default seed on 22 September the run ends with M0 at $2.39B, all of it reserves, M2 at $78.0B, the central bank having made $18.5B and destroyed $16.1B since founding and remitted $486M to the treasury — and its equity at minus $2.39B, a loss carried from paying interest on reserves in money it made, which is exactly what printing to pay interest on reserves looks like. On 0.7.2's eight default seeds it ended below zero in every one, at minus $1.1B to minus $3.3B. On 28 September, with the dial's median at 0.44% over the run, the default seed ended with M0 at $1.48M, M2 at $293.4B, $8.08B made and $8.08B destroyed since founding and $426M remitted, and its equity at $32.1M: the vault it counts as its asset, less $1.48M of loss carried. On 1 October, the dial's median 0.46%, it ended with M0 at $4.94M, M2 at $261.0B, $44.1M made and $39.1M destroyed since founding, nothing remitted over the run, and its equity at $13.9M, the vault less $4.94M of loss carried.

The rate is a floor

The dial is the rate the central bank pays on reserves. The commercial bank's spare cash sits there earning it, where it used to earn the world's 2% abroad, and its shortfalls are borrowed at the window at the dial plus WINDOW_PENALTY — a quarter point since 0.7.7, the Bank of Canada's own spread, and a point before — where they used to cost the city's own paper rate plus two points and a stretch; a failed bank is charged nothing at the window while it is resolved (0.7.1), because its equity is zero and a charge would fail it again. Savers got their share of what reserves earned — a bank whose only income was its reserves paid them 0.675% at a 3% dial and 2.25% at 10%, the first time the dial had ever reached a household — and since 0.7.7 they get the share of the dial the bank chooses (the bank). Nothing lends below what reserves earn and nothing borrows above what the window charges, so the dial is the price of money: the city's note prices at its floor plus its spreads, and MonetaryCheck's section 6 reads the city's rate at exactly the dial at 3, 10, 20 and 40% and the bank's lending rate about one to two points over it — 5.02%, 11.28%, 21.02% and 40.98% on 28 September, and 5.00%, 11.27%, 20.99% and 40.93% on 1 October. On the way it found a premium counted twice: the carry trade and the bank's deposit bid were priced off a rate that already carried the strain premium, and lendingRate() added it again. The carry trade itself is untouched, and is now the priced alternative rather than the marginal source.

Interest on reserves is paid in money made for it, and that is the one place the floor has no floor of its own. Held at 30% or 50% for a whole run, M0 compounds to 108 to 3×1014 $B while prices stay near one — spare cash earning the rate in new money that becomes spare cash — and the same mechanism took M0 to $1.25 quadrillion in the six broke seeds held at 0.7.1's stop of 25%. It is on the list.

Autopilot. A toggle on the monetary page hands the dial to the Taylor rule each month — the rule the page already prints, which since 0.7.2 is clamped only by the dial's bound at 100% — until the player's hand takes it back; saved, and held in a playtest by -Dplaytest.autopilot; the dial itself is held for a whole run by -Dplaytest.policyRate. With the rule on the dial on all eight seeds, 0.7.2 took bank failures from 27 to 17, the dial peaked at 30.5% and no seed spiralled: with the rule's hand on it, a credible real rate holds. Since 0.7.13 a new game founds on the autopilot; an older save keeps the hand it was played with.

The target is the player's. Jerus: "i want to have the dial not target 0 inflation, set it so that you can choose what is your inflation target". Since 0.7.4 the rule aims at inflationTarget, 2% by default (DEFAULT_INFLATION_TARGET, the constant it had been), in half points from nothing to 10%, and since 0.7.15 to 20% — Jerus: "inflation target can be higher tgan 10%, up yo 20%" (MAX_INFLATION_TARGET). It sits under the autopilot on the monetary page and applies at once, like the ceiling, with chips to 5%, and the rule's sentence is struck live at the target and at another (ruleRate(inflation, target)), so that at 5% inflation the page can say the rule sets 7.5% aiming at 2% and 10.5% aiming at nothing without doing the arithmetic itself. It is saved under its own key, an older save reads 2%, and -Dplaytest.inflationTarget holds it for a run. The colours of the header's INFLATION tile read from it, as the strip's did until 0.7.21 (the currency).

Advances, the ceiling, and who is paid

A treasury below zero at the top of the month is advanced its gap at the policy rate, repays from the first cash above zero before anything else, and may owe up to a ceiling in months of its trailing revenue. The emergency note it used to sell its bank is retired, and a save still carrying one runs it off. The ceiling is a dial since 0.7.2 — advancesCeilingMonths, 0 to MAX_ADVANCES_CEILING 36, default DEFAULT_ADVANCES_MONTHS 6, chips at 3, 6, 12, 24 and 36 months on the monetary page — saved under its own key, so an older save reads six, and held in a playtest by -Dplaytest.advancesMonths.

Past the ceiling the treasury pays by Jerus's rule, asked what a treasury past its ceiling does: "pay promises first, cut the rest." Every payment goes through Game.treasuryPays(TreasuryLine, amount). Promises — pensions, EI, health, the schools, the police and prisons, the city's own services, coupons and principal at home and abroad, the interest on the advances, the student loans already lent — are paid whatever it takes, past the ceiling if they must. Discretionary lines — the subsidies, the students' grants, repairs to the city's own buildings — are paid only from cash at or above zero once the ceiling has bound, and what they are refused is owed as interest-free arrears, by line, listed on the Government tab and paid down first when cash returns. Purchases — land, buildings, capital into the bank, reserves, buybacks — are simply not made. The central bank's profit — the interest it takes on its advances and, since 0.7.1, on the paper it holds, less what reserves cost it — is remitted to the treasury the next month as a revenue line; a loss is carried and recovered before anything is remitted again.

It is the floor the overdraft never had. On 0.7.0, held at a 10% dial, the six seeds that had compounded to $193 quadrillion and NaN ended owing $0.1–2.0B, the audit closed every month, and seed 0's bank failures went from 161 to 16; the arrears grew in a straight line rather than compounding, and none of the five that drew recovered. On 0.7.2, with the currency answering the real rate, none of the six draws at all. The default seed on 22 September never drew an advance: it ended owing nothing against a ceiling of $2.71B, six months of its revenue; nor did it on 28 September, against a ceiling of $3.20B, nor on 1 October, against $2.94B.

Since 0.7.15 a city can run on them from its first month. Asked whether an Insane city should play before it borrows, Jerus chose "Play runs on advances", and the refusal 0.7.14 had put on the play clock is gone. An Insane city that never borrows pays its land bond's dollar coupon and its founders' pensions in month one from nothing, ends it $6.47k overdrawn, and is advanced the gap at the next settle; its discretionary lines are refused, because a ceiling of no revenue leaves no room, and its private sector pays tax from month two, so a year on its treasury holds $7.1M. The time skip no longer stops at an empty treasury either ("Skip runs too"); it says what the central bank advanced. A failed bank's resolution is paid through the same door since 0.7.14, the advances taking whatever the treasury's cash does not (the bank).

The holdings dial: QE and QT

Quantity, beside the price. CentralBank.targetShare is the share of the city's outstanding term paper — serials and term loans, not notes — the central bank aims to hold, from nothing to MAX_QE_SHARE — half until 0.7.15, because past half the market it is the market, and all of it since, as a backstop (below); chips every ten points on the monetary page, saved, and held in a playtest by -Dplaytest.qeShare. Once a month, in openMarketOperation() — after the treasury has settled with its central bank and before the market is priced, so this month's quotes carry it — the holding moves toward the dial by at most QE_SPEED, a quarter, of the largest of the dial, the setting before it and the share actually held: four months at most, exactly four to or from nothing, so a sale finishes where a share of the gap never would. It never buys at issue, bar its own maturing par since 0.7.15, and it trades only paper that has already been paid for, pro rata, at the curve's market value — the bank's first, and since 0.7.15 the households' after it; buying is money issued and selling money retired; it carries the paper at face and takes what it paid above or below face into the month's profit, so the remittance carries it. The coupons on its share are destroyed and come back as the remittance: debt to itself. The setting before the dial is saved beside it and restored without moving (restoreTargetShare()), because the first build forgot the pace on every reload.

compression(months) = termPremium(months) × min(1, share of the term paper held / FULL_COMPRESSION_SHARE) × QE_COMPRESSION // FULL_COMPRESSION_SHARE .5 · QE_COMPRESSION 1.0

So at 30% held the fifty-year sits exactly 0.90 points under the table and the note has not moved, and at half the term premium is gone. With the playtest's borrowing sent home and the dial at 30% (0.7.1), the central bank bought exactly 30% of each issue's face in four months from the month after its settle, at 0.99 to 1.05 of face, and took 0.90 points off the fifty-year while it held it. It moved nothing else — the runs match the ones without it month for month until the first purchase and drift apart after, trajectory noise rather than a channel — because the playtest's advisor reads only the short end, which the compression never touches. CentralBankCheck sections 11–15 assert the purchase at QE_SPEED × 30% at face and at the market value, M0 moved by exactly it, compression(240) exact and the note untouched, the coupon destroyed and remitted, the dial to nothing selling back within the speed's months, the pace across a save, and a reform that scales the holdings and not the dial.

The backstop. Jerus: "central bank bond holding can ve 100% if one wants" — "for when banks and investors dont want government bonds, its also f bank is in dire state, to not harm it further". Since 0.7.15 the dial runs to the whole of the term paper (MAX_QE_SHARE 1.0). It buys the bank's paper first and then the households', at the curve's value, through the door a household short of money sells by; the compression stays whole from half, with nothing more past it (FULL_COMPRESSION_SHARE); and the city's floor is split by who holds the paper, so that the more of it the central bank holds, the less the bank's state reaches the city's rate (the city's borrowing). And it rolls what it holds: 0.7.15's first build found that when a piece it held fell due, the rollover sold the replacement to the bank, which funded it at the window and failed, so on Jerus's word, "Build the rollover fix", it takes its maturing par again at issue as a non-competitive add-on at the issue's price, par for par, the Federal Reserve Bank of New York's practice; past its dial it lets what it holds run off, as the Fed did under QT; and at 100% it takes the whole issue when the market's part is nothing ("Yes, the backstop"), which the Fed never does. And last year's surplus pays the market's part of what falls due first and then the central bank's par ("Surplus pays everyone"), so a city in surplus clears its paper whoever holds it; when a surplus pays only part of the par, the paper shrinks under the central bank and it ends that month over its dial, and the holdings step sells the excess to the bank — Jerus: "Leave it (as built)".

The default city never owes paper at home, so the dial cannot be measured on it: its runs, and the autopilot's, are 0.7.14's to the byte. Where the dial bites, on eight seeds a setup: a Lean founding at 50% failed its bank 12 times and ended at 159,372 people on average, and at 100% 11 times and 156,877, paying all its paper off again between months 252 and 1,080 with the central bank rolling in only 10 months across the eight; held at 25%, where the spiral is, the city ended at 4,529 people with the dial at nothing, 5,626 at 50% and 1,047 at 100%, with 43, 105 and 61 failures. That first build's better 25% city at 100% — 9,476 people — was a loop: each rollover sold the bank about three times the maturing face for cash, and the central bank bought it back under face and remitted the gain. Par for par ended it. At 100% the central bank is the buyer when nobody else is; it does not limit what the city can sell. A 0.7.15 save with the dial past half, opened in 0.7.14, loads at half, and Jerus left it so. CentralBankCheck's sections 17 to 20 assert the whole of the paper with the compression whole from half, the floor split by holder, the par taken again at issue, and the surplus paying the market's part first.

The defence spends

Until 0.7.2 the vault damped a push on the currency and not a dollar left it: a capacity that cost nothing, which is why the default player never saw it work. Now the central bank sells. On a month the currency is pushed weaker and the city ran a deficit, it sells the vault's capacity times the month's own deficit — its current plus financial account when negative, in dollars at the month's rate, not the trailing window the push reads — and never more than the vault holds. What damps the push is the realised absorption, what was sold over what was wanted, so a full vault damps what its cover says, one running low damps less each month, and an empty one damps nothing. A push with no deficit that month sells and damps nothing; a rise is never met, because leaning against appreciation is buying dollars, which is a decision, not something a vault does by standing there.

push = trade pressure + ratePressure // ratePressure = −realRateDifferential × RATE_PULL, held within MAX_RATE_PRESSURE either way absorption = MAX_ABSORPTION × min(1, cover / COMFORTABLE_COVER) // .85 at six months of imports dollarsSold = min(absorption × deficitUsd, vault) // only on a push to weaken, in a month that ran a deficit realised = dollarsSold / deficitUsd rate ×= 1 + push × (1 − realised) × openness × DRIFT_SPEED rate += (parity − rate) × REVERSION

A capital transaction, not money destroyed. The buyer is the world, and the local money it hands back is money the pools had already paid out for the imports: the deposit half of the textbook happened the day the goods were bought. So the vault falls by the dollars, the central bank's equity falls by their local price (vaultSpent(), a line under equity on the Money page, spent defending the currency since founding), M0 does not move, no pool moves and the audit declares nothing, and the month's profit and the remittance are untouched. It is booked at the reprice, after the audit, through dollarsSold(), with the month and the lifetime kept apart (getDefendedThisMonth(), getDefendedLifetime()). The first build retired the money instead and took seed 0's M0 to minus $255M, a bank that had destroyed more than it ever made. The Exchange page says what was sold this month and since founding, and the Trade tab's forces page shows the four terms that exist: trade, the real rate, the vault's defence and parity.

What it did with the founders' billion, when a city was founded on one (0.7.2), was spend it on structural deficits, not crises. On the eight seeds every one sold more than half of it and six all but 1%, over 22 to 225 sale months, half of it gone by month 628 to 3,747 — while the playtest's advisor buys it back with the treasury's cash, so that one seed sold US$1.29B out of a US$1B vault. MAX_ABSORPTION .85 at a COMFORTABLE_COVER of six months spends about 0.85 months of cover per deficit-sized month, so a vault at six months of cover meets the first deficit-sized month at full strength and damps less every month after, about half as hard five months in. On the default seed on 22 September the defence sold US$1.06B in 174 months, the most US$62.3M in month 725; half the founders' dollars were gone by month 726 and all but a hundredth by month 1,774, the vault ended at US$134k, and $531M of the central bank's equity went on it. Since 0.7.10 a city is founded on US$25M, not a billion (founding a city), and the default playtest's vault was half gone by month 141 against 739 and empty by month 374 against 987, the defence spending US$202M against US$1,189M, while the worst currency month fell from 3.34 to 1.54 local to the dollar (0.7.10). On 28 September the defence sold US$24.9M in 63 months, the most US$3.7M in month 66; the vault was at its lowest, US$973k, in month 973, half the founders' dollars were gone by month 753, $17.3M of the central bank's equity went on it, and the advisor's purchases left US$53.1M in the vault at the end. On 1 October it sold US$39.2M in 114 months, the most US$9.0M in month 3,447; the vault was at its lowest, US$2k, in month 132, half the founders' dollars were gone by month 40 and all but a hundredth by month 51, $31.7M of the central bank's equity went on it, and the vault ended at US$33.0M. Whether the defence should answer only a crisis is Jerus's.

The demand channel

For the rate to bite at home a household's saving has to answer the real return, and since 0.7.3 it does. What a household spends above a basket a head is scaled by one factor a month, struck on the real deposit rate — the bank's deposit rate less the year's inflation, Game.realDepositRate() — before the households plan. Deposits and not the curve, because the marginal saver's return is what the bank pays on every balance every month. Subsistence never moves; the propensity's share, the wealth term and the surplus the counter and the table spend out of all scale by it, so a hike does not hand what the grocer lost to the counter — scaling the want alone had pushed the surplus up, and a hike raised spending. At a factor of one every line is the old plan to the bit. Nothing is saved; the load path strikes it again. The monetary page says it in words: "savers earn X% real, so households spend Y% of what they would at zero."

spendFactor = clamp(1 − SAVING_RESPONSE × realDepositRate, SPEND_FLOOR, SPEND_CEILING) realDepositRate = the bank's deposit rate − the year's inflation // SAVING_RESPONSE 1.0, provisional, Jerus's number · SPEND_FLOOR 0.5 · SPEND_CEILING 1.5

So the chain runs from the dial, to the deposit rate the bank chooses as a share of it (until 0.7.7, a share of what its book and its reserves earned), to the real deposit rate, to the factor, to the plan, to the shelf. MonetaryCheck section 6 — the assertion that had waited since the first batch — holds one founding at 3, 10, 20 and 40% from month 25 to 60 and asserts that inflation falls with the rate: each row no higher than the one before within MEASUREMENT_NOISE, and the 3% row at least TRANSMISSION_FLOOR, a point a year, above the 40% row. The noise was 0.05 points a year until 0.7.11, when a ten-year fixed mortgage that locks in the month it is written put the rows held a month late up to 0.053 apart, and on Jerus's word it is 0.10; on 28 September they differ by 0.000 to 0.035, on 1 October by 0.000 to 0.066. It went green at 1.032 points on 0.7.3, where 0.7.2 read 0.996 and would have failed, read 1.428 on 0.7.7, and read 1.304 points on 28 September. Since then its founding has been made to build a hospital through the window, so that its builders keep their crews (0.7.17), and to put up its bakery, diner and boutique (0.7.18), and on 1 October it reads 4.960 points, with the city at a 3% dial inflating 3.82% a year: a different town from the one 28 September measured, so the two readings are two towns, not a trend.

The channel is in, and it moves very little

Most of that point is still the currency's: the rate moves inflation by making the currency dearer and imports cheaper, which it did before the channel existed. The channel fires every month and reaches almost nothing, for four reasons. Weak pass-through. Until 0.7.7 the bank paid savers DEPOSIT_PASS_THROUGH, 45%, of what it earned, not a rate, so the deposit rate carried about a quarter of the dial — 11% at a 40% dial — and the factor ran only from 0.99 to 0.88 across the whole range. Since 0.7.7 the bank passes on a share of the dial it chooses, and on 28 September the same founding's deposit rate ran from nothing at a 3% dial to 13.21% at 40%, and the factor from 1.000 to 0.858; on 1 October the re-caused founding's ran from nothing to 6.52%, and the factor from 1.083 to 0.930. Nothing to act on in the index. Food is four fifths of it, and the shelf does not answer money at the margin: at the founding it sits at its floor on every row, and in a grown city the want is thirty times what the shops can serve, so the scarcity mark-up reads the shops' coverage and a tenth less want moves no price. The counter and the table are not in the index, and that is where discretionary money goes. Saved money compounds: at a held high rate savings earn interest paid in new money, and the wealth term spends a fixed share of a stock growing faster than the factor cuts it. On 0.7.3's default eight the factor's median was 0.994; held at 10, 30 and 50% the six broke seeds swung 1.25×, 1.75× and 2.24× where they had swung 1.24×, 1.90× and 2.01×, and their index ended higher at 30 and 50%. A held high rate does not deflate through demand.

A demand channel needs a price that answers to demand. The assertion is green on the transmission as a whole, not on this channel, and the next rate that bites will be a shelf priced by scarcity against money rather than coverage, or a deposit rate that passes more of the dial through.

The chapter's harnesses are CentralBankCheck (twenty sections: the identity, the floor, the window, the note's price, advances and the ceiling, the arrears rule, the autopilot, the save and a reform, the holdings dial and the ceiling dial, and since 0.7.15 the whole of the paper, the floor split by holder, the par taken again at issue and the surplus paying everyone), HoldersCheck, CurrencyCheck, MonetaryCheck section 6, CreditCheck section 11 and TreasuryCheck, and they are listed in how it's checked. The central bank's month is not saved, so after a reload the Money page's this month lines read nothing until the month turns; that is on the list too.

16

Prices, the currency & the world

The city has a price level, a currency with a name, a vault of dollars, a balance of payments, hot money that comes for the spread, a rent market that no longer follows wages, and — since the surplus had nowhere to go — somewhere for its savings to sit.

A price index, and prices that ration

A fixed basket — the shelf price and rent, weighted by what households actually spent in a base month two years after the city started shopping — is the city's price level. The basket does not re-weight to hide inflation. It is fixed only after 24 months of real shopping with both food and rent in it (PriceIndex.SETTLING_MONTHS), about month 37 in a Standard city, because a city based earlier had a basket that was 96% rent and never saw food prices; and the first year-on-year rate comes a year of readings after that, about month 50. Until then every screen and the central bank read inflation as zero. The play-through of 0.7.19 read the price card's no year yet at month 13 as a bug; since 0.7.20 it counts down, rate in ~37 mo, a floor while the basket is not fixed and exact once it is, and Jerus chose to "Keep the countdown" over fixing the basket sooner or adding a provisional reading. The shelf price is cost-plus and rises to 1.6× at total shortage, so a congested city spends more at the shops for fewer goods, and the household screen says who went short.

The index remembers its own high and low water-marks, and the month each was set. An end-of-run figure says where a city finished, not what it lived through: a run that ends at 0.98 having been at 1.31 and 0.62 is not the same city as one that never moved, and until the marks were kept there was no way to tell them apart from the outside. The pair and the swing between them are carried in the save; a city saved before the marks existed opens with both sitting on today's level, which is the only honest thing to say about a past nobody recorded.

Rent is two markets: family homes and studios, each with a cost floor from the cheapest template that serves that segment, at today's cash and materials prices spread over a landlord's yield of 5.8%, times that segment's households per front door, unbounded both ways, closing a twelfth of the gap a month because a lease is twelve months. Taking one floor across the whole portfolio and applying it to both legs was what turned rent into a constant — every building earning the same $445 a head whatever it cost, and a city that built 138 low-rise blocks and no studios with 1,847 households wanting one. Struck per segment, studio rent went $445 to $706 and studio doors over 4,000 months went from zero to 19,920. Land is deliberately not in the floor: land price is a capitalised rent, an output of this market, and feeding it back in grew a number because it grew. Overbuild and rent falls below cost and the landlords eat it.

The city's dollar, and what moves it

The local currency was the Danzik dollar, D$, until 0.7.10, and since then it is the city's own: named after the city by default — a city founded as Arden pays in the Arden dollar, A$, ARD — or by hand, with a three-letter code that is never USD, and a save from before loads as Danzik, in the Danzik dollar, D$, DZD. This page writes D$ for it, against the US dollar. Since 0.7.2 its rate moves on three things: pressure from the balance of payments; the real rate differential, Game.realRateDifferential() — the policy rate less the city's inflation, against the world's base rate less the world's realised inflation — at RATE_PULL 4 a point, so money moves toward the higher real return; and a pull toward relative parity (REVERSION, a fifteen-year half-life) that says where the rate should end up. The drift that moved it every month by the whole of the inflation differential, rate *= 1 + (local − world) / 12, is deleted. With imports repricing through the rate, wages chasing the whole index and the shelf cost-plus, a gain of one on that link meant a city whose prices rose 40% a year saw its currency fall 40% a year by rule, whatever its central bank did; Jerus's 0.6.9 city, 399× its founding prices in twenty-five years, was that loop run for 306 months. The inflation differential still reaches the currency, through parity, which rises with local prices, and through the trade balance a cheap or dear currency earns — but not month by month, by rule. A spiral needs an actual outflow to continue, and a credible real rate stops it: in CurrencyCheck, ten months of 40% inflation with the real gap at nothing move the rate to 1.0067, the parity pull alone, where the drift would have taken it to 1.3649. The world has its own inflation, a persistent moving average drawn low in 1–8%, deterministic, with its level pulled back to trend so that 333-year runs stay usable.

current account exports − imports − interest paid abroad − foreign savers' interest financial account what the city's owners send abroad, less what comes home position reserves − foreign debt // revaluation lives here, not on any statement real rate gap (policy − inflation) − (world base − world inflation) // RATE_PULL 4 a point hot money spread = max(depositRate, cityRate) − worldRate − countryPremium // at most MAX_SPREAD

The caps on the channel are numerical guards now: MAX_RATE_PRESSURE went from .8 to 4.0 and binds only at a hundred-point real gap, and the hot money's appetite, CapitalFlows.MAX_SPREAD, from .06 to .25, measured, until 0.7.7 took the strain premium out of every rate, against the city's rate net of it (getRateBeforeStrain(), gone with it), because what a strained bank charges is not a return. RATE_PULL went from 6 to 4, the one retune the batch allowed itself: at six the loop's gain was 1.44 against the deleted drift's 1.0, and one seed whose dial sat at 0.08% went to the guard with its prices at 115× founding; at four the gain is 0.96, and no default seed goes near a guard. The cost is that a credible real rate buys two thirds of the support it did. The channel is only as good as the hand on the dial. The Trade tab's forces page shows the four terms that exist — trade, the real rate, the vault's defence and parity — and what the reprice was handed rides the foreign accounts' saved array, the defence at slots 25–27 and the real differential with its two inflations at 28–30, so the page survives a reload.

And since 0.7.3 there is no guard a player will meet. ForeignAccounts.MAX_RATE and MIN_RATE were 100 and .01 and pinned the currency — the last acts of Jerus's year book were a currency at its guard with imports no longer rising — and they are 1e9 and 1e-9 now, scaled by a reform: a guard against a rate run to nothing or to infinity, never a price the game expects to see. Past a hundredth of a cent the rate turns round and says what a US cent costs — the strip's second line did, and since 0.7.21 the RATE tile's (i) does — US¢1 = D$1,000 at a rate of 100,000, instead of printing 0.00¢, and every page prints a rate through one formatter (Money.fxRate()). On the default seed on 22 September its dearest dollar was 1.000 local, in month 2; it spent no month weaker than twice parity and none at a guard, prices averaged +0.05% a year, and the run ended with the rate at 0.482 local to the dollar, 53% under a parity of 1.016. On 28 September its dearest dollar was again 1.000 local in month 2, with no month weaker than twice parity and none at a guard; prices averaged +0.04% a year, and the rate ended at 0.632, 37% under a parity of 1.006. On 1 October its dearest dollar was 1.428 local, in month 137, with no month weaker than twice parity and none at a guard; prices averaged +0.02% a year, and the rate ended at 0.571, 39% under a parity of 0.929.

The vault

The founders leave one. Jerus: "of the 3.5B you start with, 1B is in usd in the reserve, so you only see 2.5B start with" — because 99% of players would never buy reserves. From 0.6.10 to 0.7.9 a new city opened with D$2.5B in the treasury and US$1B in the vault, and since 0.7.10 it opens with D$100M and US$25M, the IMF's old three months of imports for a town of eight to ten thousand (founding a city); the dollars are bought on day one at the opening rate and booked as the purchase they are (Game.FOUNDING_CASH, FOUNDING_RESERVE_USD), and for the city's first ten years the Exchange page says where they came from. It is kept in dollars. Checking the founding reserve found it in the wrong currency: it was a local figure at the price paid, so a hundredfold fall turned a US$1B vault into US$10M and its import cover fell with it, while the dollar debt beside it was revalued every month. The stock is dollars now, its local value the dollars times the rate, cover no longer moves with the currency, and the month's move is a revaluation line, not cash and not an audit flow. The treasury still buys and sells reserves, and since 0.7.6 the land office can be paid straight out of the vault (land); the central bank reads the vault as its asset and, since 0.7.2, spends it defending the currency (the central bank).

A reserve defends a currency; it does not hold one down. Jerus's rule, chosen from three after the founding reserve caused the very year book it was meant to prevent. The vault used to damp the pressure on the rate both ways, so a deep one muted the surplus a weak currency earns and the support of the policy rate — the two forces that pull a currency out of a spiral — and with the founders' dollars in it three of eight seeds reproduced Jerus's city, two of them past 140× their founding prices with the currency at its guard. It damps only a push weaker now, and the eight went to a median price swing of 1.60×, none past 2.81×, with sudden stops 8 to 0.

And the header carries them. From 0.6.10 to 0.7.20 a strip did, three panels between the population and the cash: prices against founding over inflation year on year; the rate both ways in the currency's own names, US$1 = D$x over D$1 = US¢y, grey at or above parity and down to 5% under it, amber more than 5% below it and red past 25%; and since 0.7.4 the price of money, Jerus: "on the top of the UI the bank rate, the central bank rate, both should be shown, as well as the rate you borrow in" — three lines at the caption's weight, central the dial, bank the bank's lending rate on it, reading no bank or failed in red when the bank lends nothing, and city what the treasury borrows at. The inflation readout was grey within three points of the 2% target, amber outside it and red past 10% either way, and from 0.7.15 read from the player's target. Since 0.7.21 two of the header's six tiles carry them (the screen). INFLATION reads the rate against the player's target, or rate in ~N mo before the first rate comes; it is green within three points of the target either side, amber further off, and red more than five points over it (STRIP_INFLATION_OVER_TARGET, Jerus: "Red at target + 5 points") or with prices falling faster than 10% a year, whatever the target — so at the default target inflation of 7 to 10% reads red, where until 0.7.15 it read amber. RATE shows the dial, with what a US dollar costs under it, grey near or above parity, amber more than 5% weaker and red past 25%; the bank's prime and the city's rate are on its tooltip, and the three rates, the currency both ways and parity behind its (i). Every figure comes through a getter, and the header recomputes nothing.

The surplus, and where it goes

A city that exports steel used to run a surplus of 30–50% of GDP with nowhere for it to go: no household bought anything abroad, nobody was paid a dividend, and no saver could own a foreign asset. The currency simply appreciated — about 10% a year, 1.20 down to 0.44 over 250 months — until every exporter in the city was dead, twenty times a run.

Outward investment is the door. A sector with an idle till buys the world's paper when the world pays more than the bank — forty times the spread, up to nine tenths of its wealth, moving 4% of the gap a month out and 10% home — and brings it home to fund a plan, pay a dividend or clear an overdraft. Households run the identical rule on their own savings. The coupon is rolled where it is earned, an income credit matched by a financial debit, so a rentier's surplus does not appreciate the currency, and the currency is priced on the whole balance rather than the trade half. The median run went from 33% below parity to 3.5%; exports went $33M to $124M a month. Since 0.7.12 a sector that owes nothing also puts a share of its wealth into the other sectors' bonds by the same rule, never its own, and still most of it goes abroad: after 0.7.12's eight default runs the companies held $6.7T abroad against $1.7T in their tills.

And since 17 September the households spend abroad too, through three doors the surplus did not have. A car the city did not make is an import at the ceiling; a watch always is, deliberately, so that wanting luxuries is money that leaves rather than going round again, and import substitution, if it ever comes, is something a player achieves; and every tonne the railway hauls burns fuel priced in the world's money, charged as a real import before there is any oil good, so that a reform cannot forget it. A meal is the exception — the food in it was already counted when it crossed the border, and the margin is wages and rent here.

Hot money comes for the spread net of the risk, at 8% of the gap a month, and leaves at 20%, straight into and out of the bank's deposits — which are its lending capacity, so the boom and the sudden stop are procyclical by construction. A run needs a fragile position (a material stock of it with under 25% backed by reserves) and a shock: the currency down 12% in a year, the bank insolvent, or a recent default. Always painful, never fatal.

Who owns the bank is a curve of financial deepening: a city with no savings has no domestic investors, a city with $124B on deposit owns its own banks. The bank's capital follows that curve. Its wholesale funding followed it too, which is what stopped a $156B book declaring its entire funding cost as interest paid abroad, until 0.7.0 moved the funding to the central bank's window, where the interest is paid at home and declared as money destroyed.

The policy rate, and dropping zeros

The policy rate is the player's dial, with an advisory Taylor rule beside it saying what it would do and why, obeying the Taylor principle — and since 0.7.0 an autopilot that hands the rule the dial; the dial itself, and everything it now moves, is the central bank. The playtest works the dial a quarter of the way toward the advice each time, because a rate that sat at 3% for three centuries would leave the whole monetary half untested. And once the city's own price index passes ten times founding, a currency reform unlocks: divide everything by 10, 100 or 1,000, the player's choice, never automatic. Every money constant in the game is an instance field seeded from its constant and redenominated with everything else; foreign prices are the exception, quoted in someone else's money and reached through the rate. The reform instant is bit-exact and conserves money; the following month is a few percent adrift in the discrete decisions, which is filed and has a plan.

17

Founding a city

Until 0.7.10 every city was the same city at month zero: Danzik, founded on D$2.5B and US$1B, with no reason ever to borrow. A player founds a city now — its name, its money, what it starts with and the world's inflation — and the default start is small enough that the city borrows for what it builds.

Jerus: "lets reduce the cash the city starts with, both the foreign usd and the starting cash, what do you think makes sense? cause the city should borrow right". A trace of 0.7.9 found the treasury never going below D$2.467B in thirty years: the young city spent $0.1–2M a month and took in $0.4–5M, owed nothing for about ninety years, and held a vault of 270 or more months of its imports. The endowment had been sized on 9 September to buy the opening, a coal power plant, and nothing since had asked it to do less. Since 0.7.10 a city is founded on D$100M and US$25M, Jerus's choice from the options put to him (Game.FOUNDING_CASH, FOUNDING_RESERVE_USD), sized so that the treasury could pay for the playtest's founding village and one of the first big works, and the city would borrow for the rest, the way towns pay for long-lived works, on bonds repaid over the asset's life — Britain's New Towns were built on sixty-year loans from the Exchequer, not on endowments. The vault follows the IMF's old rule of three months of imports. The endowment is still a game number, and the code says so.

The village is the playtest's, not the player's. The playtest places a founding village by hand before its advisor takes over — sixty houses, five shops, two farms and a depot — and the play-through of 0.7.19 found that a player's city gets none of it: after Found the city the treasury still held D$100.0M, and month 3 had one house and thirteen people. A player's city starts with the treasury and the vault, and builds the rest. The sticker is not the invoice. What a new city pays for a building is the building and the material beyond its yard at the world's price, and since 0.7.19 the builders' sales tax on both: the water plant's D$65.8M sticker was invoiced D$109.6M and is D$129.0M with the tax. So D$100M pays for the village, D$38.8M, and one of the wind farm, D$36.5M, the school, D$35.3M, or the police station, D$52.9M; the option Jerus picked had been described on stickers, and he kept D$100M once it was described on invoices, so the water plant is a bond from day one. That is why the build screen offers a twenty-year bond sized to the gap (the city's borrowing): as 0.7.10 measured it, a Standard city a year on was D$26.1M short of the water plant, issued D$40.3M of face at 4.22%, raised D$26.13M, paid about D$56k a month, and had the plant in 57 months; on 1 October the same fixture was D$58.3M short, issued D$113.8M of face at 7.19%, raised D$58.4M, paid D$253k a month and had the plant in 137 months, with the audit closing every one (NewGameCheck, section 11).

Found a city

Jerus, the same afternoon: "a small thing at the start of the game where you choose the name of the city and the currency … and perhaps the settings to choose the starting cash and usd cash and offworld inflation rate … (with option to just start at default)". From 0.7.10 Start New Game opened Found a city (ui/FoundingScreen): the city's name; its money, named after the city or, with a tick, by hand, a name and three letters that are never USD; the money it is founded with; the world's inflation, moved there from Settings because it acts only at founding; and Found with defaults, which founded straight away. Each start showed what it would buy. Jerus, on the play-through: "even the screen shouldnt say what the money could buy, the start screen should be real simple". Since 0.7.20 a start shows only what the founders leave, and since 0.7.21 New city on the main menu opens one panel over its dimmed skyline: the name, in a field that opens on Danzik; one line for its money, "Its money: the Danzik dollar, D$ · name it yourself", where the link opens the name and its three letters in place; WHAT IT STARTS WITH, four cards, each its money in figures and one line on how hard it is, never what it buys, Standard marked default, and Set your own amounts for Custom; THE WORLD'S INFLATION, a row of five, with "A faster world makes your money stronger. It can't be changed later." and the rest behind an (i); and Found the city and Back. Found with defaults is gone, because the page opens on the defaults. None of 0.7.21's panel has been seen on a screen.

The starts, in the order the page lists them (Founding)
StartTreasuryVaultWhat it is
InsaneD$0US$0"Nothing. The ground is owed on a 20-year bond." Since 0.7.14: the ground it stands on owed abroad, a twenty-year dollar bond at 3% for US$2,100k
LeanD$25MUS$10M"Tight. Borrow early." Two-thirds of the playtest's village at a new city's invoices, three quarters until the builders' tax went into them; the city borrows from its first months
StandardD$100MUS$25M"The usual start." The default, sized on the playtest's village and one of the first big works at their invoices
WealthyD$2.5BUS$1B"Plenty to spare." The start every city had from 0.6.10 to 0.7.9

The choice is a record on the city (Founding), set once, saved under eight keys and never changed; the currency is a value per city, read through game.getCurrency(), because one process holds several cities — the harnesses', and the playtest's reloaded twin. The window's title and the save lists name the city, and every screen that prints its money names it. A save from before 0.7.10 loads as Danzik, in the Danzik dollar, founded on D$2.5B and US$1B, which is what it was. A new game also founds on the autopilot and with the rollover on same structure since 0.7.13, and with the bank's resolution automatic since 0.7.14; an older save keeps what it was played with.
