Building the screen found two bugs on the founding path: a new game after a load kept the loaded world's inflation, and a new city's first year was back-cast at the previous city's rate.

Insane

Jerus: "on game start, add a new difficulty called Insane, which is just you start with 0 cash, 0 vault, and a 20y bond 3% for the initial land cost … if easier the starting debt is abroad in usd". Since 0.7.14 it is the first start on the page: D$0, US$0, and a twenty-year dollar bond at 3% owed abroad for the starting land — 3,000,000 square feet at the land market's opening price of US$0.70 a square foot, US$2,100k. No cash moves, because the founders' land was bought on it, and on the world's curve it is worth US$375k on day 0. On day 0, as FundCheck's section 12 prices it, a city with no revenue and no output pays the full spread: the founding card quoted the village's twenty-year bond at 13.90%, $85,500k of face to raise $32,978k, the six-month note at 13.00%, and the window abroad shut, until 0.7.20 took every figure off the founding screen. Since 0.7.21 Insane's card reads nothing for its money, and what is owed abroad for its ground is in its tooltip. 0.7.14 made the clock refuse to run until such a city had borrowed (Jerus: "Borrow first"); since 0.7.15 it runs on the central bank's advances from its first month instead (the central bank).

Of 0.7.14's eight Insane seeds, three became cities of 148,000, 206,000 and 148,000 people, and five spiralled on the city's own paper, ending with 3,686 to 10,118 people and $420T to $6,416T of it; the playtest's player borrows exactly the village's invoice, so every seed ran on the advances from month 3. Lean is hard too: D$25M covered three quarters of the village then, and two-thirds since the tax went into the invoices; every seed borrows by month 4 to 14, and on 0.7.10 half of them ran out of cash around month 140 and lived on the advances for 450 to 500 months.

What the smaller start did

As 0.7.10 measured it, making the founding a choice changed nothing else: founded Wealthy, the city reproduced three old ensembles byte for byte, all 24 seed-runs. The default playtest never borrows on D$100M; its treasury bottoms out at D$61–67M in its first two years and then grows. What the default run does show is the vault, half gone by month 141 against 739 and empty by month 374 against 987. Under the autopilot the bank failed 76 times against 49, and all of the rise was the vault's: D$100M with the old US$1B vault gave the old counts, and D$2.5B with US$25M the new ones, because without dollars to steady the currency a rule-held rate sees more imported inflation in the city's middle years. Held at 10%, every seed now runs dry in years 185 to 253, where three of eight did before: a city held at that rate loses money for centuries, and the D$2.4B had been paying for it. And 42 fixtures in 25 harnesses had been living on the old treasury without saying so — ten went red and three were green only because they ran on a short calendar — and each funds itself explicitly now, with no premise and no tolerance moved.

18

Land

The city starts with thirty blocks, allocated so that a power station and a water plant leave two spare — land is taught on turn one.

Nine plots are on offer at any time (LandMarket.LISTING_SIZE; earlier editions said ten), generated deterministically from the plot's own id, so parcel 47 is the same plot in every session and in every reload. Sizes are multiples of a floor that rises with how much the city already owns; 15% of plots are enormous tracts, and the tract is what makes the listing worth reading.

Two prices, moved by different things

what the city pays a base per sq ft in US dollars, raised by blocks already owned and by population — the two premiums no longer multiply — at the day's rate what businesses pay the same base and premiums in the city's own money, × a scarcity multiplier from 0.65 to 1.90 on allocated/available

The multiplier passes 1.0 at about 61% built — below that the city sells land at a loss, which is the intended cost of over-annexing. The earlier version multiplied by utilisation, which sat between 86% and 100% for 2,400 straight months, so "scarcity" was decoration. From 9 September the city was founded with a $3.5B endowment — D$2.5B in the treasury and US$1B in the vault from 0.6.10 — enough for a coal plant at 41% of it, a works department at 400 points a month, a shed of material, and a bank standing on day one. Since 0.7.10 it is D$100M and US$25M, and the big works are borrowed for (founding a city).

Land is bought in dollars

Jerus: "when you buy land, make it so that it costs USD not domestic currency, and basically how it would work is a little toggle at the top to choose, when you buy land, to use up your USD reserves or to convert cash into usd exactly to buy the land, and the default is that you convert." Since 0.7.6 the land office prices every parcel in US dollars — the same base and premiums, so at the founding rate of 1.00 every number is what it was — fixed when it is listed, and the treasury pays usd × rate on the day it buys: a weak currency makes land dear and a strong one cheap. What businesses pay the city stays local money, struck exactly as before, so the city's margin on land carries the currency, and a currency reform no longer clears the office's board. There are two ways to pay, a chip pair at the top of the office. Converting, the default: the treasury buys exactly the dollars and hands them over in one movement, and the vault ends where it began. From the vault: the dollars leave it and no local money moves, a capital transaction like the defence's, and the treasury's journal carries the land back against the budget's land line. A vault too short for the plot spends what it holds and converts the rest, and the receipt says so. On the default seed on 28 September the advisor paid US$10.2B abroad for land over 151 months, none of it out of the vault, and it cost D$6.15B at the day's rates, 39.6% less than it would have at the founding rate; on 1 October it paid US$10.6B over 149 months, none of it from the vault, for D$5.95B, 44.1% less.

Since 0.7.13 the office speaks the paying currency and square kilometres — Jerus: "for the land … instead of blocks, say km^2, and add a button to buy multiple". A plot's large price is in local money when converting and in US dollars from the vault, with the other as a caption beside it; areas read in km² on the exact international foot, to three figures, so a block is 0.00929 km², and so do the Summary's land panel, the build screen's Not enough land page and the time-skip report. A plot the city cannot afford no longer refuses: its button opens a funding page built from the build screen's own pieces — converting, the twenty-year bond sized to the gap and the six-month note; from the vault, a twenty-year dollar bond or a six-month dollar note sized to the dollar gap, whose dollars are held in the vault, or the old top-up from cash; with the window abroad shut, the reason and only what remains. And Buy the next N plots, five by default, buys the office's first N, cheapest ground a square foot first; because a listed plot's price is frozen, buying them at once costs exactly what buying them one by one does. Converting for land has never pushed the currency, which is on the list. Since 0.7.22 the player's hand on the queue reaches the ground too (the builders): a stopped shell and a demolition on site hold their ground, a finished demolition's ground comes back to the city as free land, and a buy-out pays the owner for its ground at the price businesses pay the city, recorded as a buyback. Land purchases are not on City History's lane of decisions, as build orders are not; a big one the treasury borrowed for shows as the borrowing.

Ground is what a mature city runs out of — land sits at 88–90% for the last decade of most long runs — and the newest sectors are the hungriest for it. A Rail Terminal is 5,500,000 square feet, nearly three times a Coal Power Plant and the largest footprint in the catalogue; a Livestock Farm is 120 acres, the dearest ground of any farm at $108k an acre; a Metro Line is 30,000 square feet for thirty thousand journeys a month, five hundred times a gravel road's capacity per acre. That ladder is the whole reason the modes exist — see transport. Every sector's planner assumes the ground is free and none of them can see they are bidding for the same land; that is on the list.

Iron is a pooled resource. A parcel carries a deposit count and a tonnage; deposits cap how many mines can stand, and every mine draws from one reserve. Roughly 28% of ore parcels carry more than one site. Ore in the ground is worth a fraction of what it fetches once it is out — the value is in getting it out — and a deposit that is worked out closes its mine inside a year, which is what makes the bust when a mining city runs dry a real event rather than a slow leak.

19

The catalogue

Seventy-three buildings in buildings.json, hot-editable next to the game, every one on a sourced real capital cost at the size the game actually builds it. Edit a number, restart, look — no rebuild. Ids are permanent, because saves are keyed by them; the next is 73, and eighteen were added in the three days to 18 September.

CategoryTemplatesWhat it does
Residential3House (six), Studio Apartments (two), Low-Rise (four) — a home has a size and a household has to fit it
Commercial3Convenience Store, Small Grocery — coverage is how many people can shop — and the Commercial Bank
Industrial5Industrial Bakery and Bakery, which buy crops and bake bread and bakery goods out of them (until September 2026 they were a Textile Mill and a Food Processing Plant making a good called Food out of nothing); and the eleventh sector's Meat Works, Snack & Oils Plant and Bottling Plant — five, six and four posts, among the smallest investor-built job steps in the catalogue
Heavy industry5Steel Foundry and Steel Mini-Mill, plus manufacturing's Fabrication Shop, Fabrication Works and Machine Works — the first domestic customer steel ever had
Business services3Contact Centre, Shared Services Centre, Engineering Services Office — seats sold abroad; the last needs licensed engineers before it will open
Agriculture4Mixed Farm, Grain Farm, Greenhouse Complex and, since 16 September, the Livestock Farm — 13.8, 55, 3.4 and 120 acres; the glass is the one that survives expensive ground, and the herd is the dearest of the farms per acre — $108k against the Grain Farm's $22k — and sells 6.5% of its build cost a month against the Grain Farm's 18%
Mining1Iron Mine — sixty jobs, a quarry lifting 30,000 t/yr
Construction2Construction Depot, and the Construction Materials Plant — the seventh sector's only building
Electricity2Coal Power Plant — 280 MW at $1.43B, far more than a city of 74,000 needs — and a Wind Farm, the first small plant beside it
Water1Water Treatment Plant
Infrastructure6Gravel Road, Paved Road, Elevated Highway — each the cheapest per trip across a band of land prices — and since 16 September the Bus Network, Light Rail Line and Metro Line, the same ladder two rungs on: 2,500, 8,000 and 30,000 journeys a month, and the first things in this category that ever had a payroll
Rail3Rail Spur, Freight Line, Rail Terminal — 50,000, 200,000 and 500,000 tonnes a month across the city boundary at $140M, $520M and $1.24B, owned by the twelfth sector and financed like a steel mill; a terminal is 1,482 jobs on 5,500,000 square feet, the largest hiring commitment and the largest footprint in the game
Automotive4Vehicle Works and Assembly Plant (120 and 500 cars a month), Commercial Vehicle Plant (150 vans), Locomotive Works (four wagon sets) — all fed on fabricated steel and machinery, and anchored on heavy manufacturing's revenue per head rather than the food plants', because the first draft never cleared the interest test
Luxury2Boutique and Department Store — 320 and 4,200 pieces a month over a counter, importing every one of them
Hospitality2Diner and Restaurant — 13,500 and 40,500 meals a month, each buying the Convenience Store's basket scaled to the person-months its meals come to, to the kilogram
Healthcare14childcare, general, senior and death care, grouped by function in the menu
Education9three basic stages, college, university, and four professional schools that each license one job
Safety4two police buildings and two prisons — the city pays for all four

Every building declares a cash cost, construction points, materials, maintenance, capacity, lot footprint in square feet, electricity and water draw, road load, its job mix across eleven types, and — since the template — which sector owns it and what it makes and uses. A healthcare building also declares what it treats, a school what it teaches, a safety building whether it is a station or a cell block, a transit line the journeys it can carry and a rail building the tonnes. The road load is one number and it is read as three: fitted to the catalogue itself, 0.726 × jobs + 0.878 × homes + 0.0386 × tonnes a month, so a mine keeps its 420 and learns that 68% of it is ore, a university is all commuters, and a shop's freight is a van at a back door. Money throughout is in thousands: a cash cost of 30 is $30,000. Every row of the build menu prices itself — sticker and all-in, from the same method that charges, and since 0.7.19 with its labour at today's builders' wages and their sales tax in it — and opens a card that explains the building from its own numbers.

Both halves of a price are sourced now

A unit of construction material was $2,000 and is $18,000, defined by the House: ten units against a real house's $180,000 of structure. Every template's split was re-derived at a real material share — 40% for a building, 25% for a plant, 60% for a road — with every total held to the dollar. It also found that the builders' material import bill had never been paid: $2.75bn crossed the border over 4,000 months and the expense struck at zero, so Construction ended every run holding the money and the balance of payments never saw a unit move. Range runs from a Home Daycare with two staff to a Rail Terminal with 1,482 jobs, the Regional Medical Centre's 996 the largest the city pays for, and the terminal's 5,500,000 square feet the largest footprint in the game, ahead of the Livestock Farm and a Municipal Cemetery's 2,000,000.

20

Utilities & roads

Power and water

The grid starts with 10,000 W free and the reservoir with 8,000 units. Both are gated by the same utility workforce. Power is $0.01 a watt, water $0.05 a unit — about $5 per thousand gallons.

Only businesses are invoiced. Houses, depots, roads, the water plant's own 900 watts and the city's own buildings all draw power nobody pays for. Booking revenue on the whole city's draw was money from nowhere scaling with the housing stock — 73% of it, measured. The utility books exactly what its customers' statements charged them, not a recomputation at the end-of-month ratio.

Roads

The city starts with 400 units of capacity — the streets that already exist. Traffic flows freely to 90% of capacity and degrades after that, floored at 35%. Three roads, and the land ladder was in the catalogue before anybody pointed at it: a gravel road is 500 square feet per unit of capacity, a paved road 208, an elevated highway 42 — twelve times the capacity per acre for two and a half times the money. Since 16 September the load is three streams and the network can be relieved by a highway, a tram or a train, but never deleted; that is transport, and this is the baseline it is measured against.

throughput = 1 if load ≤ 0.9 × cap = max(0.35, 0.9/util) otherwise

Congestion slowing the sites is the pointed part: a jammed city builds the roads that would unjam it more slowly, and the floor is what keeps that from being a trap.

Construction output

round(working capacity × construction fill rate × road ratio × health work ratio) working capacity = the city's own 400 points + the depots' capacity × the share of their posts offered // since 0.7.17

One definition, used by the screen, by the sites, and by what the sector is paid for. The three had to be the same number: a copy that drifts books revenue for work nobody did, and very nearly did when roads arrived. What reaches the sites is what is left once the standing stock has had its repairs — about 40% of the total, permanently — and since 0.7.17 it is shared among them by the crew each building can use (the builders). The depots count at the share of their posts the builders kept on that month, and the city's own works department, which has no posts, counts whole; whoever is weighing an order reads the same figure with every post offered (getConstructionOutputAtEveryPost()), because an order is work and work hires the crews back. Since 0.7.22 a rushed site's crews work longer hours on top of it, more points in their first two months and fewer from the third, and the builders count them beside the output.

21

Transport & vehicles

Until 16 September the entire transport model was one scalar: a road network with a capacity and a load, one throughput ratio between 0.35 and 1, and three road types that were the same road at three prices. Jerus: "decomposing the existing import/export bands into world price + transportation cost… then later, transportation efficiency can make that freight component smaller or larger." Nine batches followed in three days under the same version number, each one there to make the next possible: the band decomposes, the load splits, the modes and the fare, the railway, the vehicles, and the screen that finally shows a player any of it.

The band was a freight bill

Every traded good carried two constants and the gap between them was the whole local market — and nothing in the game said why the gap was the width it was. Each good carries a third number now, the cost of moving one unit, and the two ends split into four:

worldImportPrice = worldBuyPrice + baseFreight // the world's own ask, plus getting it here worldExportPrice = worldSellPrice − baseFreight // the world's own bid, less getting it there

Three quarters of the wedge is freight and one quarter the world's margin, and the split is a game decision rather than a measurement: it sets how much of the band good logistics can ever win back. At three quarters a 23% band bottoms out near 6%. The price table was already a freight model and nobody designed it that way: freight as a share of the world price sits at 21–25% for fifteen of the seventeen goods that had both ends, and the two outside that band are iron at 49% and materials at 43% — the two lowest-value-density things in the game, where real freight genuinely is half the delivered cost. The delivered prices are the stored literals and the world band is derived, which is the opposite of how it reads and the only way round that is exact: storing the world band and adding the freight back gives 0.8470000000000002 for steel and 0.27999999999999997 for crops, and an ulp in this codebase is never nothing. Not one price moved on the day it shipped, and eight seeds over 333 years came back byte-identical; TradeCostCheck asserts every delivered price against a table of what it was that morning, because a test that reads its own subject proves nothing.

One road load is three

Traffic { COMMUTERS, GOODS, BULK }, and which mode can carry what falls out of the classes rather than needing a table of exceptions: commuters can ride a tram and nothing else can; goods — a shop's bread and milk — are a van at a back door however many rails a city lays, and take the train only across the boundary; bulk — ore, steel, crops, grain, aggregate, beams, machines, a locomotive on a low-loader — is what a railway is for. The shares were fitted rather than guessed, by least squares against the catalogue itself:

roadLoad = 0.726 × jobs + 0.878 × homes + 0.0386 × tonnes a month // R² = 0.74 over the 56 templates that loaded a road when the fit was struck; seventy do today

The implied rate is one worker per nineteen tonnes a month — a truckload — and nobody set out to encode that. The residuals are all explainable and all one way: an Iron Mine 280 over the fit (heavy vehicles), a Penitentiary 288 under and a Long-Term Care Complex 136 under (their occupants do not commute), a University 258 over (students travel and the fit counts only wages). None of it matters, because the fit supplies the share and the stored road load supplies the magnitude: a mine keeps its 420 and learns that 68% of it is ore.

Commuters / goods / bulk, as the fit reads the catalogue
BuildingRoad loadSplit
Steel Mini-Mill50017% / 0% / 83%
Grain Farm1814% / 0% / 86%
Iron Mine42032% / 0% / 68%
Bakery4053% / 16% / 31%
Small Grocery5587% / 12% / 1%
University520100% / 0% / 0%
A played city—86% / 3% / 11%

A real city is 86% people, which is what urban traffic actually is. And the ensemble found a defect whose fix is the better design: the first version summed the three streams to get the network's total, and eight seeds came back subtly different from the build before them — a thousand dollars in three trillion, one person in forty-eight thousand — because every building's three loads add to its own road load to the bit, and what does not survive is adding them up in a different order. So the total keeps the exact sweep it always had and the breakdown is read alongside it. The demand did not change; only the ability to see what it is made of.

The modes, and a fare that is a price

The land ladder, per unit of capacity — a journey a month for the transit lines
ModeCapacityLandSq ft a unitCash a unit
Gravel Road900450,000500$2,584
Paved Road1,200250,000208$4,500
Elevated Highway1,50063,00042$6,400
Bus Network2,50020,0008$4,800
Light Rail Line8,00045,0006$8,000
Metro Line30,00030,0001$10,667

A metro is five hundred times a gravel road's capacity per acre for four times the price a journey. A city that has run out of ground and not out of money builds one; a city with ground to spare never should — which is the first real answer this game has had to its own late-game wall. Every mode is a relief on an unchanged baseline, and that is the design rather than an implementation detail: a city with no highway and no transit computes exactly the load it computed yesterday and degrades on exactly the curve it degraded on. A highway relieves freight, not by carrying more but because a tonne of ore on a grade-separated road does not share a junction with a school run — bulk by 40%, goods by 15%, commuters not at all. Transit takes people off the road entirely, and three things stop it being the answer to everything: at most 65% of commuters ever ride, whatever is built; what transit can carry is capped at twice the road capacity under it, because buses are road vehicles and a metro station needs a street outside it — a city that builds a metro and no streets gets a metro nobody can reach; and it never touches freight. Measured on a load of 10,000 in the played mix: 5,590 riders with room to spare, 4,800 with fewer streets, 800 with none, and with the best of everything the road still carries 39% of what it would. The road can be halved and it can never be deleted. It is also why the stream split matters to a business: an office and a mill on the same jammed road read 74% and 47% against the road's own 42%, because the mill beats the road by exactly as much of its own traffic as rides the tram, and a lorry in a jam is in a jam. A sector with nothing standing reads the city's own blend rather than a clean road, because the planner costs a first plant at the sector's operating rate and a congested city was cheerfully building plants a 38% road said it could not afford.

A fare is a price, not a charge. Nobody chooses to be policed and a patient in a clinic is not shopping; the person on a bus chose to be, and can choose a car instead. So the ridership ceiling is what a free system would carry and the fare walks it down on a straight line to nobody at the maximum: free, 5,590 riders; at the default, 5,311; at the ceiling, none. And the two lines move against each other, which is the whole decision — six times the fare takes $58,695 a month instead of $13,276 and puts 1,398 more cars on the road. Jerus asked for a dial that can be made profitable "by alot"; it can, at a ridership the city may not want. The fare is money, so a reform moves it — the twenty-third of that family and the first caught in the same edit that created the field — and the ridership curve is written against the cap rather than against dollars, so a reform moves both and nobody changes their mind about the bus. Two things were found on the way: nothing in the infrastructure category had ever had a job, so nobody had written a line to pay one, and a Metro Line is 828 posts; and the fare was charged once a month against a constant that says a single journey, so a monthly pass cost $2.50 against a $3,460 wage, seven hundredths of one percent, and the buses could not have paid for themselves at any fare a player would set. That was the transit loss the list had carried as a balance problem. It is 40 journeys a month now — out and back, twenty days — a pass is $100 or 2.9% of an unskilled wage, and the dial stays per ride because that is the number a person on a platform recognises.

The fare was collected from nobody

From the day transit was built until 16 September the city credited itself the fare and debited no household. Nobody saw it because nothing had ever built a bus: the playtest's advisor answered every jam with tarmac, so four thousand months a seed, eight seeds a run, not one city ever carried a passenger, and every fixture that needed a road bought a road. The first ensemble in which the advisor bought a Bus Network failed the money audit on all eight seeds, and the residual was the fare to six decimals — 2,186 riders at $2.50 is $5.47, and the audit said 5.465207. A harness cannot check a thing nobody does. MoneyCheck's own header says every money bug this codebase has had was a flow with one side, and the twenty-ninth harness could not see this one because its city had no buses. It builds three now. Fixing it found its sibling at once: setTransit was missing from the load path, the fifth sighting of that shape, invisible until the fare became real money.

The railway

Rail is the twelfth sector, and the business that wants to be paid the freight every price in this game has always had inside it. Jerus: "the freight cant reach zero cause freight needs profit, its its own sector… it wants and will do everything possible to stay profitable and maximize profits. freight money goes to that sector." There were two ways to write that and only one keeps the audit honest. Paying the railway out of the band would have a payment the books record as going abroad in fact going to a company in the city. So the band carries only what the lorries still move, and the railway bills the shipper at home, like any other supplier of a service — the invoice goes on the shipper's input line, where freight belongs in anybody's accounts, the money never leaves the city, and the VAT falls out for free because the ledger already credits a purchase at the supplier's own rate.

what a shipper pays a unit = (1 − share) × baseFreight // still to the lorries, inside the band + share × quote × baseFreight // to the railway, on its invoice allowed = what a month costs to run + 1.2% of the track it has sunk quote = clamp(allowed / what it would bill at the lorry rate × scarcity, 0.30, 1)

A shop's pricing rule cannot price a utility, and the measurement is what said so. Written first as Retail's shelf — cost plus a quarter — it quoted 32% of the lorry rate, earned $4.5m a month on ten billion of track, and was written down six times: a railway's running cost is a fraction of its capital, so a mark-up on running costs allows a return of nothing on the track. It prices like a regulated network now, running costs plus 1.2% a month on the rate base, floored at 30% of the lorry rate because rail is cheaper than a lorry and not free, capped at one because nobody ships by rail at more than the lorry charges, lifted up to 1.6× by what it cannot carry. It settles at 38–43%. Building too much rail does not make freight cheaper; it makes a railway that cannot pay for itself, and RailCheck asserts that a network with four times the track it needs pins at the lorries' price and earns less on four times the capital. The link that falls out of it is real and welcome: a railway earning 1.2% a month on money that costs it 1% is barely a business, so dear money is what keeps a city's trade band wide. And the band alone was lying to the sectors: at a 92% quote on 92% of the traffic it said steel's wedge had fallen from 52% to 11% when the shipper had saved eight per cent, because the railway's invoice was not in it. Every place that asks is this worth exporting reads a net price now; every place that settles a trade reads the band; with no railway the two are the same number to the bit.

Track is half a railway. Capacity is the lesser of the track laid and the wagon sets to run it, at 2,500 tonnes a set and a twenty-year life, so track with no locomotives carries nothing: a Rail Spur is twenty sets, $48m of rolling stock on a $140m spur, and a mature city's million tonnes of track is 420 sets and about 1¾ sets a month of replacement for ever, which is half a Locomotive Works running. Rolling stock is a pantry rather than an input — that one line is what makes a bought set accumulate into a fleet instead of vanishing into a month's production — and a save from before trains has trains, because it was hauling, so it had locomotives. The railway will not lay a line under 60% used, Materials' lesson relearned the afternoon a spur went up in a town of four hundred houses and a bank failed over it. It relieves 75% of the bulk it carries off the road, never all of it, because the last mile off a siding is a lorry and a spur reads as 94% trucks; bulk goes before goods, which decides almost nothing today because bulk is 99.7% of the tonnage crossing the boundary. Every tonne it hauls burns $30 of fuel in the world's money, a real import before there is any oil good, so the day OIL is a good nothing else changes.

What a railway is worth

Eight seeds, four thousand months each: population 113,178 → 178,975 (+58%), GDP $416.8M → $711.2M a month (+71%), per seed 32–91% and 14–113%. On the default seed steel's net wedge goes from 51.6% of the world price to 25.9%. And it is a healthy business in all eight: zero write-downs, zero loss months, the best credit rate in the game, and in seven of eight no debt at all. Three rungs — 50,000, 200,000 and 500,000 tonnes a month at $140m, $520m and $1.24bn — $2,800 of capital per tonne a month on a spur and $2,480 on a terminal, and a terminal nearly three times a Coal Power Plant on the ground, which is the land trade Jerus asked for.

The vehicles

Automotive is the thirteenth sector, and it is not for the cars. Ore becomes steel, steel becomes fabricated steel and machinery, and until 16 September both left, because nothing in the city bought a beam or a machine. An assembly plant is the first thing that does — the fourth link of a chain that stopped at three — and the chain cannot be shortcut: fabricated steel is deliberately not importable, so a city with money and no fabrication shop cannot be in the car business, it can only buy cars. Machinery can come off a ship, at the ceiling its own header had predicted for the day something here bought one, because a car is three and a half tonnes of fabricated steel and four hundred kilos of machinery and the bulky input is the one a city must make. Cars export; vans and locomotives do not, from three measurements of one failure: allowed to sell wagon sets to the world, the playtest built 19 Locomotive Works, 9,880 posts in a city with 25,102 jobs; allowed to sell vans, 240 Commercial Vehicle Plants shipping 36,000 vans a month; and it was already building 179 Fabrication Works and zero Machine Works. An unbounded export market at a fixed floor means the single best export is built for ever and nothing else ever is; bounded world demand is the general fix and is still its own batch. A plant may draw at most a quarter of what the city fabricates, because the first supply gate asked the mills' question — is there spare local supply — and nothing fabricated is ever spare when the world will take all of it; a $172m Assembly Plant sank in month seventeen of a fixture that was measuring tuition, and the harness was right. Eight seeds: GDP $711.2M → $758.8M a month, up in six of eight; seed 0's mature city runs 84 Assembly Plants on 23% of its fabrication.

The cars are the fourth mode, and the only one that costs. Highways, transit and rail are all reliefs on an unchanged baseline; a car is the opposite, and it has to be, because the baseline this network always computed was an unmotorised city, unlabelled. It is named now rather than moved:

onRoad = (commuters − riders) × (1 + ownership × (3.0 − 1)) // exactly 1 at zero ownership, to the bit willingToRide = (1 − ownership) + ownership × 0.75 × jam // jam is a memory of the commute, a quarter a month

Only the people still driving pay it, so a city that motorises and builds transit is not charged twice for one journey, and that is what closes Jerus's loop: "people drive until the road is full, then take the tram." A car owner rides nothing on a clear morning and three quarters of them ride at a standstill — motorise, nobody rides, it jams, they ride, it clears — read off a memory of the commute rather than last month's road, or it is a two-month oscillator. Transit is a ceiling on ownership, not a brake on adoption, and the difference is the whole of whether a player has a move: on the rate it only delays, on the level a well-served city tops out at half a car per household for ever. What motorising costs, eight seeds against the same build with adoption off: 191,194 people and $848.3M a month at 80% road, against 176,228 and $695.7M at 73%, cars in 60% of households and 37% of commuters riding — and the chain is not congestion alone: a worse road costs every new plant at a lower operating rate, marginal projects stop clearing the interest test, business borrowing collapses ($6.6bn lent against $18.5bn, the bank at 45% of capacity instead of 77%), Manufacturing's assets fall from $23.4bn to $6.3bn, exports fall 26%. A six-point road ratio, compounded over three centuries. CAR_LOAD_AT_SATURATION = 3.0 is the one constant here that is a game decision rather than a measured one. The advisor learned to buy a bus the same day, and what decides between six candidates is the catalogue: each is costed in trips taken off the road per dollar — a gravel road 900 for $2,326k against a bus network 2,500 for $12,000k times the car factor — so a bus loses outright in a city where nobody drives and wins once about half the households own a car. Nobody chose that crossover.

The vans are the fifth throttle, and the first one a business buys. Energy, water, road and health all arrive; a lorry is the sector's own capital, sized from a measurement: a city of 106,612 moves 1,169,640 tonnes a month at nameplate, both sides of the door, which at 120 tonnes a vehicle is a fleet of about ten thousand, one commercial vehicle per eleven people, and eighty a month of replacement for ever. Built once without teeth, every ratio in the measured city read 100.0%, because vans are importable and a sector asking for a fleet got one in a month; you cannot put a thousand lorries on the road in a month, so a fleet is built up at an eighth a month and a firm short of its own lorries hires haulage — slowed to 60%, not stopped. Re-measured: 132 sector-months short, worst 65%, every growing sector hitting it and none stopped. A save from before vans owns a fleet, on the railway's own flag: it was moving steel, so it had lorries. And it is what finally gives the Commercial Vehicle Plant a customer.

What the whole vehicles arc costs a mature city — eight seeds, 4,000 months each
BuildPopulationGDP a monthRoads
No cars, no vans191,194$848.3M80%
Cars176,228$695.7M73%
Cars and vans165,914$673.3M75%

About 13% of population and 21% of output against a city where nobody owns anything to move with — for two industries and three decisions it did not have. The households' side of the cars, who buys one and who borrows for it and who sells it last, is in people.

The instrument panel

Nine batches of transport shipped before any of it was on a screen: the road carried three streams and showed one number, the fare was a real dial with a real elasticity and nothing in the game could turn it, the railway repriced itself every month and told nobody, and a mill running at 65% because it had not taken delivery of its lorries looked exactly like a mill running at 65% for no reason. Infrastructure has been a tab of its own since 0.6.5, the rail's twelfth, put after Services because that is where the eye goes looking for it, with four pages that each answer a question somebody actually asks. Roads: why is my throughput 73% — the free-flow curve as a banded meter, the three streams as a table with what one trip of each kind costs the street after the highways and the railway have taken their cut, then the cars. Transit: the three ceilings and the lowest wins, the two things that walk it down, the books, and the dial. The railway: track against the sets that track needs, what it hauls and off which stream, what the rule allows against what it billed, and a box that tells an over-built railway so. Freight: the band good by good with what is left of the wedge after rail, the month's bill split into the half billed at home and the half that leaves inside the band, and every sector's fleet — the first time the fifth throttle has been visible anywhere. The fare is the one control, staged like every other policy dial, with a preview that shows riders, fares, net cost and the trips that go back onto the road, and that says plainly what it cannot preview: the second round, where a worse road puts some of them back on the tram. Nothing on the screen builds anything; a second place to buy a building is a second place to maintain. Cars show in both places, on People beside the household's other assets and on Roads beside the congestion number they explain. The screen shipped without anyone looking at it — the only JavaFX natives here are the Windows build and a Java window cannot be put in the screenshot allowlist — so InfrastructureCheck gained a ninth section that walks every accessor the four pages call, in the four city states that break screens, and asks the two questions a renderer cannot survive the wrong answer to: is it finite, and is it inside the range the label claims.

22

The screen

Jerus played 0.7.19 for its interface alone, and four batches rebuilt the screen from what that found (0.7.20 to 0.7.23); none of them moved the model in a city whose player leaves 0.7.22's new hand on the queue alone, and after each the default playtest wrote 0.7.19's eight trace files to the byte. The window is a header, a rail and a page. Twelve destinations sit on the rail at the window's left edge — Build, Land office, People, Services, Infrastructure, Sectors, Government, Finances, Bank, Trade, Policy, History — with Menu at its foot where the gear was; the header carries the clock and six headline numbers; and since 0.7.21 there is a main menu again, drawn over a skyline the game draws. A new or loaded city opens on Build, the one screen a player who does not yet know what they are doing can do something on. Pressing a rail button you are already on takes you back to that rail's own front page rather than doing nothing, so there is always a way out of a sub-page without knowing which one you came in by; Build is the exception since 0.7.20, and keeps the category it was left on for the session.

The play-through, and what it asked for

Jerus asked for a play-through on the PC, the interface and not the numbers: "take notes about the ui, any issues, any thoughts, any ideas, things that should be better... dont change anything". On 30 September a new Standard city was played for thirteen months, and his slot-10 city of 1.05 million from month 1,792 to 1,814, his own autosave copied off first and written back byte for byte. It found a founding screen that promised a village the game does not build, City History opening scrolled to its foot, Esc doing nothing on the Build tab, a price card still saying no year yet at month 13, Quit with no question, a building on site invisible on its own build card, a build quote that drifted from five months to nine, tooltips with no background, Died −0.0 and Moved in 8.9, revenue at 169.7% of annual GDP in month 7, and a centre column that jumped under the pointer whenever its height changed. What worked: the tax dial's staging, the Needs you rows that go straight to the fix, the order line — "$33.6M with $5.0M sales tax, back to the treasury as it is built · ~5 mo" — and the land office's cards. Jerus: "ok yes, thats good, all you listed is good", and he added the textbook problem, everything "worded very long, like the player is basically... welcome to the textbook?", and asked for colour, "gdp on the main screen at the top always visible with a tiny little chart", charts that move with their crises named, a chart that can fill the screen, and the construction panel. Four batches were agreed: the bugs and the layout (0.7.20); colour, the header, the menu and the founding screen (0.7.21); the construction panel (0.7.22, the builders); and the charts (0.7.23). On the mockups for the last three, Jerus: "that is damn pretty, go for it".

The page stays put

0.7.20 shipped nineteen fixes. City History opens at the top: Write the year book, the page's only control that takes the focus, took it on arrival and scrolled the page down to itself. Esc and P reach the menu with a tooltip showing, because a showing tooltip hid itself on Esc and swallowed the key, and on the Build tab the pointer is nearly always over one; P still types in a text box. The centre is top-aligned on every screen, the build hint and the receipt's dot keep their space whether or not they show, and a scrolled page ends with room under it. A build card reads N on site · ~M mo at today's queue for anybody's order, a Needs you line whose fix is already on site adds N on the way, ~M mo and falls from red to amber, and the order line, the card and the right panel read the quote's one wait (BuildingManager.waitOnSite()). Tooltips have a panel. Flows are whole people, under 1 below one, and no shared formatter prints a negative zero. Quit asks first, "Quit to the desktop? This city is autosaved.", and while it is up Esc closes it and every other key goes to its buttons. Urgent notices are toasts at the bottom right: three at most, each fading after eight seconds and held while the pointer is on it, a click opening the inbox at that notice. Text wraps; Build remembers its category for the session; the receipt is a popover with the last five purchases, each with its total and its sales tax; trillions print $3.1T and the load list Autosave · month 13; and a chart with nothing recorded yet says from the end of the first year rather than drawing an empty frame.

It was checked by eye on Jerus's PC the same night, a new Standard city to month 13 and then a cold start, with his autosave backed up first and restored byte for byte, and that check found two more, fixed the same night: the right panel's site line was squeezed back to 371 af… once the build log under it needed the room, and Resume on a cold start opened the empty world the game builds at start-up rather than the autosave — older than 0.7.20, and that world would have autosaved over the city the player meant to resume. Esc could not be checked, because the remote session's Escape never reaches the game; it is Jerus's to try by hand.

Colour, type, the header and the menu

One palette since 0.7.21, the mockups', in ui/Palette: near-black blue grounds, three greys for text, four area colours that say where you are and never how it is going — people teal, money blue, business violet, building pink — on the rail, as a swatch before every page title, on the header's tiles and in one-line charts; and three verdict colours, green, amber and red, for good, watch and bad and nothing else. So a labour shortage's 0% out of work reads amber, jobs going unfilled, and not red; a sector earning nothing draws grey; the treasury's cash is white unless it is overdrawn; the build tabs carry a dot for who builds them — blue for the city alone, violet where investors build too — with a key, where amber words were; and the Government's rings take five colours that can be told apart. Words are IBM Plex Sans and every figure IBM Plex Mono, bundled with the SIL Open Font License, where about fifty hard-coded Courier New faces were; a face that fails to load leaves the platform's, and the log says which.

The header is one bar where the date bar, the floating time controls and the net-income bubble were. The clock is docked at its left — a green play button, the date, the month and ‹ 10× › — and then six tiles, each a figure, a line of change and a sparkline of the last ten years: POPULATION, with the month's net; GDP, annualised from month 1, with real growth over twelve months once there are two years to compare; INFLATION, against the player's target, or rate in ~N mo before the first rate (prices); OUT OF WORK; TREASURY, the cash with this month's net under it; and RATE · D$, the dial with what a US dollar costs. A click on a tile opens City History on its lines over their last ten years, and the long explanations sit behind an (i) on three of them. At its right are the credit rating's chip, green from AAA to BBB, amber at BB and B and red below, and the inbox's envelope with its unread count. At 1,366 pixels a big city's first three tiles draw no sparkline, because the figure takes the tile; that was accepted while Jerus slept, figures never being cut.

The main menu is drawn over the whole window on a still skyline the game draws, the city's line rising behind it: CityBuilderSim, "A city, its money, and everyone in it."; Continue, saying which city and month it goes back to — the city in play, or the autosave on a cold start; Save while a city is open; New city, which opens the founding panel (founding a city); Load a city; Settings; Quit; and YOUR CITIES, the three slots saved most recently with their name, people and month. Esc, P or Menu on the rail open the same menu over the city, and since 0.7.22 Settings, Load and Save on a cold start are drawn over its backdrop rather than over an empty city's header.

Text in three layers. On the screen, the number, a label of two to four words and at most one short line; one click away, behind a shared (i), the full explanation in a popover that closes on a click elsewhere or on Esc; and in this manual, the history — nothing on screen carries how the code used to work. Since 0.7.21 the (i) is on the build tabs, Construction's How it bills, the tax dial, the Government's last row, the founding screen, the menu and three of the header's tiles; since 0.7.22 on five more of the longest paragraphs the game had — the bank's lending table, the central bank rolling its paper, buying bonds, the households who skipped their care bills, and the preferred shares' terms. The short lines dropped some clauses the long paragraphs had, which is on the list.

The inbox, and the money on the screen

The four red banners became an inbox: one live notice per condition, raised from the city's own state rather than restored from a record, carrying this month's figures and one button that goes to the screen that fixes it. A resolved notice gets no button. Since 0.7.8 it says when businesses are going bust and opens the bank's Lending page, since 0.7.14 the bank's request for preferred shares waits in it with Answer the bank, and pauses a running clock if the player has asked the game to pause on events, and since 0.7.22 it says when a rush is about to cost more than it gains and when a demolition is done. Since 0.7.20 an urgent notice is also a toast, and since 0.7.21 the envelope is the header's, red while something urgent is unread. Services carries a Safety area beside the utilities. City History plots the city's recorded series, with one heading per group — including The dead, which plots the running totals by band since the city was founded, the orphans and the people with no home among them, and the killed — on an axis that is the window rather than forced through zero, bar a per-cent axis with nothing negative on it, which starts at zero since 0.7.23 (below). The goods page on City History is thirty-one rows: what a unit costs here this month, the world's band around it, and what crossed the border. There are keyboard shortcuts, the window's own full screen on F11, and since 0.7.20's check by eye Continue, Resume as it was, loads the autosave on a cold start; earlier editions said Resume did, and it opened the empty start-up world. Since 0.7.5, on a build page, Enter places every card with a quantity in the page's own order and stops at the first refusal with the rest still pending, and Backspace or Delete takes every quantity back to none (Jerus: "when you have let's say 3 ready to build, i want to be able to press enter to build"); the Build button's tooltip says so, a caption under the grid says so while anything is pending, and the Settings screen's list of keys, which had named three, names all six, Esc first leaving the full-screen chart since 0.7.23.

The money came onto the screen with it. Prices and the exchange rate sat beside the cash from 0.6.10, and the price of money from 0.7.4, on a strip that the header's tiles replaced in 0.7.21 (the currency). The Money page under Finances is the central bank's balance sheet in the player's words — issued and retired this month, printed since founding, the remittance, what the defence has spent, and M0 and M2 with a year of each. The monetary page under Policy carries the dial and its chips, what the rule would set and where the dial stops, the autopilot, since 0.7.4 the target the rule aims at, the holdings dial, the advances ceiling, the real rate and what savers earn in real terms. The borrow page lists the curve one row per maturity and steps a term loan's chips by ten, and both borrow pages carry the rollover since 0.7.13 and what happens when the bank fails since 0.7.14; the book page says who holds each bond; Finances has the businesses' bond market since 0.7.12 and the city's fund since 0.7.14; and the Trade tab's forces page shows the four terms that move the rate. The Government tab's bridge opens into the treasury's journal and lists the arrears by line, and the bank's desk foots; the Bank tab was rebuilt in 0.7.9 (the bank). Since 0.7.4 the Sector economy list draws on each card the last 24 months of its net income, with its workers under it, and a Show more chip opens every card to revenue, margin, cash, what it owes the bank and posts filled against posts offered — Jerus had asked for "a little graph of its net income, perhaps how much workers it employs total"; the line was green or red on the latest month, and since 0.7.21 and 0.7.23 it is the business violet, grey for a month that made nothing, with a faint mark at each January. Since 0.7.21 every sector page prints its money in the short form Construction has used since 0.7.20, as Jerus chose. And the People tab's EI paid this month reads what the treasury paid since 0.7.3, after the docs pass found it reading next month's bill, while the People and Summary screens read the care coverage the month actually used rather than one worked out from the beds.

City History's chart

The page was redrawn in 0.7.5. Jerus: "two graphs always displayed on the graph rail, the real gdp yearly figure, and the other is the population, and then the third one, the bigger one, the one where you set the stuff, it defaults to the borrowing rate, price level and inflation year on year". Two small charts sit at the top, real GDP over the rolling year and the population until the player pins others — a preference that follows the player from city to city and never enters a save; under them the big chart, with the presets, clear all and a log switch in a row above it, seeded on a first visit with What money costs, the city's rate, the price level and inflation. One unit is one real axis, two units are two real axes, and only a third brings back each line squashed to its own range. Recessions are shaded on all three charts, and from 0.7.5 to 0.7.22 the year book's episodes were named under the big chart's axis — Jerus's "little name generator": bank equity under zero is a Financial crisis of 2045, the rolling year of real output under the year before a Recession of, two years of it a Depression of, and so on through currency crises, inflations and deflations, epidemics, the treasury under zero and slumps, with runs under three months dropped and runs under six months apart joined (YearBook.episodes()), the same lines as the year book's WHAT HAPPENED, so the chart and the file cannot disagree. The picker folds into its groups under a filter box.

Since 0.7.23 the chart moves. Jerus: "in teh graphs you should be able to pan the chart just like yahoo finance does if you get what i mean, and it actually have interactive graph, and like also the crisis labels and all, dont you think that it should be more clear". City History's charts are drawn on a canvas (ui/TimeChart), with their arithmetic in ChartModel, which a harness can call without a screen. Drag to pan, wheel to zoom about the pointer, double-click to go back; 1Y · 5Y · 10Y · 50Y · All, opening on ten years (ChartModel.DEFAULT_RANGE), and a window left alone keeps to its range as the city grows. Under the plot an overview strip of the whole history carries the window, to drag, stretch at its edges or click outside. Years on every axis, and months once there is room for them; a crosshair card with every line's value in its own unit; legend chips that hide and show lines and say each line's unit and, with two axes, which side. Lines take their area's colour and never a verdict's. Each axis takes its own scale over the window, and a per-cent axis starts at zero when nothing on it is negative, which is what lifts a new city's flat rate off its flat price level: they sat on each other, and sit 136 pixels apart now. The second chart is no longer laid transparent over the first, and the two small charts follow the big chart's window.

Crises are named on the chart. Each recession band carries its recession's name; a hover on it gives the month, every line, the rule that named it and the band's depth, and a click, or a hover on the name, a card with the rule, the band's months and depth and the whole recession's span and worst, all off the year book's own records. Under the plot an episode lane draws every episode as a labelled span, overlapping ones on rows of their own, crises and depressions in red and the rest in amber — verdict colours, as the mockup drew them, a call made while Jerus slept. Under that a lane marked you carries the player's decisions as flags in their area's colour, because a decision is not a verdict: several in one month are one flag with a count, flags too close to tell apart merge, a hover lists them and a click pins the list, ten lines and a count of the rest. The names under the axis are gone, because the lane carries every one.

Full screen. Jerus: "let the player click fullscreen on the graph so the whole screen concentrates on the graph". The chart's Full screen lays it over the whole window, with City History's name and the clock's line in its toolbar; Esc or the button comes back, P leaves it and opens the menu, and any other screen closes it. The clock runs as it was, and each month redraws the chart in place; toasts and the inbox are under it, and a notice that pauses the clock shows as paused in its toolbar. F11 is still the window's own full screen. The game's other charts gained years under them too — the Bank tab's six, Finances' two and a company's share price — and their red, amber and lilac lines moved to the palette's colours; a sector card's sparkline and the header's tiles mark each January. The docs pass found a young city's chart that never narrowed again once it had opened on a short history, a grant change written to the log twice, a timer that could draw History over the next screen, a drag that could outlive full screen and a violet under the palette's own 4:1 contrast, and all five were fixed before it shipped.

The decision log

The plan Jerus agreed asked for the player's own decisions on the timeline — "taxes to 17%", "central bank cut to 0%", "bank rescued" — and nothing in the game kept them: a tax rate is saved as what it is, not as when it moved, so the month a player cut it could be guessed from a step in a line and the reason for the step could not be. A decision is a flow, and a flow cannot be reconstructed from the state a month ended in, so since 0.7.23 it is recorded as it happens and saved (DecisionLog): a month, a kind and a line — Taxes to 17%, Central bank rate to 0.00%, Bank rescued for its shares, Rushed University. It is written in the one place each policy is applied, never in the interface, so a decision made from any screen or by a harness is recorded the same way, and a value set to what it already was writes nothing. What is logged: every tax and the farmland relief; every promise — the wage floor, the pension's two dials, EI's two, tuition, the grant and the student loan's rate, both health dials, the fare and a standing subsidy; the central bank's dials and the hand taking or handing back the rate; the bank — a rescue, marked (automatic) when the treasury's setting made it, the preferred offer and the rescue setting; the fund's dial, what is paid into it or drawn from it, and each order of its hand; an issue or a buyback of the city's paper, the rollover's setting and a default chosen abroad; a currency reform, reserves bought or sold and how land is paid for; and every hand on the construction queue. A proposal of several dials applied together is one flag with its count. What is not: ordinary build orders and land purchases, which a city places every month and which would smear the lane — a big one the treasury cannot pay for shows as the borrowing that paid for it; the rule moving the rate each month; the rollover's own issues, a foreign default the treasury could not avoid and the reserves a dollar issue buys as part of itself; and anything while a city is founded or loaded. Flags are coloured by their area — money blue, promises teal, the queue pink. A label is fixed text in the money of the day, and a later reform does not rewrite it; the log has no cap, is saved whole under one key and is shown nowhere but the chart.

The interface is a package

Until 18 September every screen was one class: UserInterface.java, 25,098 lines, 492 methods, a hundred banner sections, and a session working on the bank had to carry the policy tab's text in the same window. It was nineteen files in ui/ then and is twenty-three now, with Ladder (0.7.6), FoundingScreen (0.7.10), ConstructionScreen (0.7.22) and TimeChart (0.7.23), and the model never imports it. UserInterface is the window — the stage and its theme, the header with the clock, the six tiles, the rating and the inbox, the debt bar, the rail, the main menu and its backdrop, the panels and the dialogs, the time-skip dialog, the scroller every screen draws into — at about 5,500 lines, where it was about 4,000 at the split. Every tab is a class of its own, BankScreen, PolicyScreen, PeopleScreen and so on, its banners moved verbatim with a scripted ui. prefix wherever it reaches the window, the Infrastructure tab drawn by ServicesScreen, and the left panel's content — the summary, the dashboard, the problem list — is SummaryScreen. What every screen shares is four toolkit classes of stateless helpers, Money, Statement, Pieces and Levers, reached through an import static so that not one of their call sites changed. Twelve model members and two imports were the whole cost of the boundary. The move was mechanical — a dry run prints the couplings and the compiler catches what the script missed — and the one thing the compiler cannot check is the standing rule of this interface: the month arrives on a clock, so every panel is rebuilt under the player and must restore its own scroll position. Opening every tab on the split found three small things: the fare dial carried the player to the Policy tab the moment it was let go, because the staged-lever slider redrew the policy screen by name; the Build tab scrolled twice as fast as every other, because it is the one screen whose content sits straight in the page and a wheel floor meant for a notch was raised on every delta of a burst; and the Reports tab, City History now, moved under the pointer when a line was ticked, because the legend grows a block per line, so that one page keeps its position as pixels from the bottom.

The statement opens

Jerus: "can you make it so in the UI when you click on revenue or cogs, it expands and shows the individual items." One figure that is really ten is a figure a player can read and cannot act on. Four lines on every sector's income statement open now — Revenue by good, split city and abroad; the cost of sales by good, split city and imported; Wages by pay tier, with posts; and Sales tax remitted, charged, at the border and credited — and underneath them is a Sector.Split per good, booked from the trade rather than reconstructed from units times price, because a local sale and an export clear at different prices in the same month. The first thing it showed was that the city's own bakeries were shipping 62% of what they baked at the world's floor while the shops bought imported meat, fruit and fish by the half-million. A disclosure that discloses nothing is worse than none: a line with one good and nothing shipped does not open, because the caret would promise a breakdown and then repeat the figure above it — and the rule about how many rows there are has to count the rows, since Construction sells no goods at all and fell straight through a rule about goods to draw a caret onto one row that repeated its total. That other revenue turned out to be two businesses under one label, work recognised off the order book and this month's repairs billed to every standing building, and a city whose building work has stopped and whose repair income is still growing is a city that has finished growing. Since 0.7.19 the material escalation the owners paid is a third part of it, and since 0.7.22 the overtime on rushed sites a fourth. The parts are frozen into the statement when it is struck, like the per-good money, because a screen reading a working field late describes a month the statement above it is not about.

The same disclosure reached two more statements on 19 September. The Government tab's bridge from the budget to the treasury's cash opens its last row into the treasury's journal, with what the journal cannot explain printed under it as Not accounted for (tax, policy and GDP); and the bank's trading desk, whose opened lines had never added up to its total because the re-mark was left out, foots (the owners and the exchange). A row with nothing to disclose stays a plain row. Because the panel is rebuilt every month under the player, a line drawn closed every time snapped shut each month while the player was reading it; since 0.7.9 a screen can keep a set of the lines it has open (Statement.opens()), and the Bank tab is the one that does — every other screen's opened lines still snap shut, which is on the list.

The summary is a problem list

The summary panel used to be six fixed rows — economy, tax, labour, health, land, people. Of the sixteen problems a player can actually build their way out of it could show exactly one, health, and only as a sick rate. A city with no water, no cells and nowhere to bury its dead read exactly like a city with none of those problems.

It is two lists now. NEEDS YOU carries twenty conditions that have a lever, yellow near the line and red past it, each row a door to the screen that owns the fix, and each disappearing when it is solved; when it is empty it names the condition closest to its yellow line instead, so it is never blank. Since 0.7.20 a row whose fix is already a building on site, for anybody's order, says so and how long it will take, and falls from red to amber. HOW THE CITY IS carries seven readings with no dial — out of work, off sick, hungry, people a month, doubled up, ground used, the currency — always present, coloured when bad, and its door goes to the screen that diagnoses rather than the one that fixes. Since 0.7.20 the Dashboard's GDP, and the ratios read off it, are annualised before a year is recorded; it had summed the months there were and called them a year.

Writing it turned up a model bug that no harness had: getEnergyRatio() and getWaterRatio() both return min(supply / demand, 1), so a row keyed off them could not appear until the lights were already out. They read load — demand over supply — now, against the 75% the dashboard has called "tight" all along.

23

Time, saves & logs

There is no next month button: the month arrives on a clock, a save has to be the city it was to the cent, and everything the game says goes to a file.

Time runs on its own

The city runs on a clock at five seconds a month (SECONDS_PER_MONTH) and a speed the player picks off a snapping ladder — 0.1× · 0.25× · 0.5× · 1× · 2× · 5× · 10× · 20× · 50× — with the space bar as play/pause and the arrow keys moving the ladder; since 0.7.21 the ladder is the header clock's ‹ and › rather than a slider. The day inside the month is painted so the city looks alive between the ticks, even though nothing in the model moves until the month turns.

Three things that only matter once the month arrives unasked. A frame that takes a long time is clamped at a quarter second, so a stalled window cannot swallow thirty months while it was away — the worst a single frame can advance is 2.5 months. Redraw is capped at one in every 120ms, because at 50× a month lands every 100ms and the screen cannot usefully be redrawn faster than it can be read. And a panel is now rebuilt while the player is still looking at it, so every scrolling page has to hold its own position across the rebuild rather than snapping to the top — which took seven attempts and a trace file to get right, and is written up on its own.

The game can pause itself when something happens, and by default it does not: the setting exists, and it is off, because a clock that stops without being asked is worse than a notice that waits. Since 0.7.22 the clock stands still while any question is up — a cancel, a demolition, a buy-out, the Quit dialog — so that nothing changes under a decision being made.

The calendar

Month 1 is January 2000. Purely presentational, with deliberately no reverse function — nothing should derive a month from a date string.

Skipping time

A skip takes a snapshot before, samples every month during, and diffs a snapshot after. Episodes cannot be reconstructed from endpoints: a city that starved for fifty months and recovered looks identical at both ends to one that never had a problem. The report counts months short of power, water, land and road capacity, months nothing was built, epidemics and their worst month, and the peak unburied — then writes plain sentences ordered by how much each should worry you. Since 0.7.8 it says what lenders wrote off, amber only past a sound book's loss, and since 0.7.15 a skip no longer stops at an empty treasury: it stops only when a month throws, and says what the central bank advanced over it and what is owed at the end. On the default seed on 1 October no skip refused to run, so the playtest never had to step a month by itself instead.

The year book, as text and as CSV

Jerus: "csv if its cheaper token wise to read than xlsx, also keep the txt". Since 0.7.16 the export writes six files: year-book.txt and decade-book.txt as before, the same bytes 0.7.15 wrote bar the version on their first line, and beside each its main table and its WITHIN table, each year's worst and best month, as CSV — year-book.csv, year-book-within.csv, decade-book.csv and decade-book-within.csv, in the game's folder beside the log (GameFiles.yearBookCsv()). A CSV is plain text, so it is never dearer to read than an xlsx, which is zipped XML; Excel opens it as columns without an import step; and it needs no library. Each table is built once (YearBook.Table), and the text's block and the CSV are both written from it, so the two files cannot disagree. The format is RFC 4180: CRLF line ends, a field quoted only for a comma, a quote or a line break, the text's own column names as the header, the text's own strings in the cells — three significant figures and a point for the decimal whatever the locale — a blank left empty, and no BOM. City History's message names the folder once and then each book's three files. Its first locale test passed a writer that formatted by locale, because it wrote the CSV after restoring the locale, and was fixed. Left small for Jerus: an episodes CSV, full precision, a friendlier message when a CSV is open in Excel, and an Excel set to a comma decimal, which opens a comma CSV as one column.

Saves

Ten numbered slots plus an autosave, under %APPDATA% on Windows and the platform equivalent elsewhere — chosen because Steam Cloud syncs from known roots and a bare home folder is not one. Writes go to a temp file, back up the existing save, then atomically move; the worst case is a stray temp file and a save one autosave old. History graphs are per slot. Autosave fires every twelve months, before any skip, and on quit, and since 0.7.20 Quit asks first. The load list names each city and slot and the autosave once, Autosave · month 13, and since 0.7.21 the main menu's YOUR CITIES reads the three newest slots' headers.

The save format is at 29, and its changelog is the best history the project has. Older saves load, back to format 21; what is refused is a save from a newer build and one from before the sector template. Nineteen carried the household balance sheet, twenty the repriced unit of material — an old yard read at its old value — and twenty-one the sector template, which was a clean break. Twenty-two was business services, twenty-three manufacturing, twenty-four the farms, twenty-five the bank's own cost of funds, twenty-six the price index's water-marks and twenty-seven the sixth age band — which also made every band-indexed array save its band names beside its values, so the day a seventh band arrives no existing file is read at the wrong offsets. Twenty-eight is the player's hand on the queue (0.7.22), under one key: a format-27 build handed such a save would load it wrongly, not merely incompletely, because a stopped shell and a demolition on site hold ground and are off their stacks, and an older build would come back with the ground free, the work gone and the money spent. Twenty-nine is the decision log (0.7.23), under one key: a format-28 build would load the city, play on, and write it back without its decisions at its next autosave, and a decision is a flow nothing else in the save can give back. Both are safe the other way: a 27 save loads with no order set and nothing rushed, stopped or being demolished, and a 28 with an empty log, which is what each city had.

The thirteen foods went in without a bump, because goods have been saved by name since the sector template: a good this build does not know is dropped and says so in the log, which is what happens to an old city's warehouse of FOOD. And so did everything the batches of two editions ago added — five sectors, the freight band, the three streams, the modes and the fare, the railway's quote and its locomotives, every sector's fleet, the households' cars, their investment income, the luxury baseline and the dinners they ate — because a sector is keyed by name, a market is keyed by name, and every new figure in a household cell is appended to the end of an array whose reader checks its length. A city saved before them opened with no cars, no fleets, no kitchens, and the rolling stock its track implies, which is exactly what that city was; an eleven-sector build was proved to load a ten-sector save to the cent before the first of the five shipped. The central bank went in without a bump too, and so did everything around it: the treasury's journal for both months and its raised counter; the two health dials, the coverage each month used and a cell slot for the share of a cell's people who could pay for care; the grant's basis and amount, the loan's rate and the tuition scale on the policy array's tail; the vault in dollars and its revaluation (slots 22 and 23 of the foreign accounts), the debt's revaluation (24), the defence (25–27) and the real rate differential with its two inflations (28–30); what the bank still owes for paper sold between the presses, under two keys; the central bank's books, the arrears, the autopilot, the policy rate boxed so that a 0% dial reloads at 0%, the holdings dial and the advances ceiling, each under its own key; who holds every bond, saved with the bond; the households' paper as the thirty-first slot of a cell, with its book ratio; and a buyback's holders outside the pools. The fold and the spend factor added nothing: the first is a rule about counts, and the second is struck again on load. A city saved by 0.6.7 opened in 0.7.3 with an empty central bank, the vault it had bought if it bought one, the bank holding all of its paper and nothing in arrears — exactly what that city was — and a city saved by 0.7.2 pays one month's EI twice on its first month, once. Between them the long sick, the people outside the families, the household record and the whole crime system went in without a bump, deliberately: Gson leaves a missing key alone, and a format-21 city loads with each of them empty, which is correct for that city. A big saved city comes back with only its founding constabulary and shows the crime notice until it builds a station.

The batches from 0.7.4 to 0.7.15 went in without a bump as well, each batch's new figures under keys of their own or on the end of an array whose reader knows its older widths: the inflation target's key, the three tax bases and then a tuition scale for each of the nine kinds of school on the policy array's tail, each school kind's month on the end of the schools' array; the history's new series — two a sector, GDP's four parts, the dial, the bank's prime, deposit rate and fees, and six of its capital — with an older history's strain premium skipped on load; the land office's choice of how to pay, its listing behind a marker that says its prices are dollars, the office's dollar ground price as a fourth slot, and the land's dollars as slots 31–36 of the foreign accounts; the bank's pricing record and its late profit, its allowance, its capital record, its month's lines and a year of them, its year of balance sheets and its equity's two parts; every borrower's last quarter, the builders' salvage and a statement's stock paid for earlier; the households' account fee on the end of their statement; the eight keys of the founding record; the mortgages, saved among the business debts under their own type with the insurance's three counters and two lines on the government's month; the bond market, each cell's bonds as the thirty-second slot and bond by bond by name, the exchange's books, and the interim loans under a type of their own; the rollover's setting, ledger and record; the city's fund, the bank's preferred and its record, three slots a company on the register for the city's holdings and a city holder on every bond, and the four inputs the debt market's rate was struck from; and what the central bank has paid the households for their paper and paid at issue rolling its own. A 0.7.15 save with the holdings dial past half, opened in 0.7.14, loads at half.

And from 0.7.16 to 0.7.21 nothing needed one. 0.7.16, 0.7.18, 0.7.20 and 0.7.21 put nothing in the save: the year book's CSV is a file it writes, the best-paid fill is a function of the posts, the workers and the licences, all saved already, and the remembered build category, the receipts, the toasts and the fonts are the window's. 0.7.17 put the builders' share of their posts, its need and the fill it was struck on in their sector's extras; 0.7.19 each payer's contract on site with the material its quote allowed for and the share of the tax it gets back, the sectors' capital purchases in the month's ledger, the builders' escalation in their extras, the bank's customers and its operating cost in its month's lines with two slots on the end of its last month, and the grant's marker that its fixed amount is real on the end of the tax policy's state. The pins on City History are a preference in settings.json, not the city's. A city saved by the build the last edition described, 0.7.15 at format 27, opens in this one as the city it was, bar the construction points it had parked on stacks with nothing left to build, which are dropped on load and move no money (7,801,561 in Jerus's city): its builders offering every post until its first month strikes their crews, each contract on site read as its owner's with the escalation starting at the load, its grant on the basis it was played on, no order set on the queue and nothing rushed, stopped or coming down, and an empty decision log. From its first month this build's rules apply to it.

Logs

Every println in the game already goes to a rotating log file, because a logging system that needs a thousand edits does not get adopted. In a packaged build there is no console, so all of it previously went nowhere. Reports and graphs to the log default off, so the 2 MB log holds the crash rather than three centuries of ASCII graphs. The decision log prints nothing: it is in the save and on the chart.

24

How it's checked

Sixty-six self-hosted harnesses plus a 4,005-month playtest that audits every month, asserts money is conserved to the cent, and reloads the city repeatedly to prove a saved game and a played one agree. AllChecks runs all sixty-seven, one JVM each, in under seven minutes — 397 seconds on 1 October, in a cloud session where the one that needs JavaFX, BuildMenuCheck, is skipped: sixty-five green and one red, HealthCheck. BuildMenuCheck had failed 73 checks since 0.7.19, because its price check was older than the builders' sales tax; since 0.7.21 it checks the taxed price, worked from the price rule's own inputs rather than read back off the quote (Jerus: "Update to the taxed price"), and it runs in the cloud on its own against the Windows JavaFX jars, where at 0.7.23 it read PASS, 73 of 73. HealthCheck's red line reads the hunger of a city charging fifteen times the founding fees for care against a free one's over their last year, and since partway through 0.7.12 the dear city has been hungrier by more than the price of care — 8.4 points against a tolerance of five on 28 September, and 40.0 on 1 October (health); it shipped red on Jerus's word, "Ship with it red, flagged", and has a batch of its own on the list.
