The line that stood here before it, InfrastructureCheck's "and its shops can actually be supplied", which Jerus put down to roads — "its fine, we will balance later." — went green by accident on one build of 0.7.2 and red again on the next, and has passed on every suite since 0.7.7; the fixture is sensitive to the young city's rates, so it stays on the list. Every count and constant on this page is struck from the tree at 0.7.23; every measurement carries its date or its batch, and "the default seed on 1 October" is the plain playtest run that night, as "28 September" was 0.7.15's. RealismCheck, a probe outside the build, is the rebalance's instrument and the thing to run first if any real-world figure is questioned. And since 18 September the playtest is the gate for a refactor as well as for a mechanic: it is deterministic, two baseline runs are byte-identical, and every move out of Game and every comment-only pass was proved by a run that compared equal to the morning's, bar its wall-clock line. The four interface batches proved the same way that they left the model's default run alone: the default playtest's eight trace files — the month, the borrowing, the houses, the people, the bank, the bonds, the building and the labour — came out byte-identical to 0.7.19's at 0.7.20, 0.7.21, 0.7.22 and 0.7.23, so a reading of the default seed taken at 0.7.19 still holds at 0.7.23.

ChartCheckthe chart and the decisions (0.7.23), in seven sections, without a screen: each kind of decision logged once, at its month, with its label; the log through a save and a load, and a format-28 save with none; the bands are the year book's recessions, named for the recession that holds them, and the lane its episodes; years on every axis and months where they fit, over 492 windows and 4,000 scales, with nice steps on the value axis; pan and zoom stop at the data, keep the month under the pointer and follow the newest month; the flags, one a month with its count and close ones drawn as one; and a young city's window growing into its range and staying on it
BuildMenuCheckevery card says what its building is, every job type has a name, and the two prices on a card agree with the till: since 0.7.21 the taxed price, worked from the price rule's own inputs, which it had failed 73 times since 0.7.19; it needs JavaFX, so the cloud runs it on its own
…and twelve morecalendar, the carry trade, GDP, housing, the inbox, mining, policy, restructuring, robustness, save slots, skip reports, water
Rules the harnesses are written to

A fixture must cause the condition, not wait for it. Assert against the model's own constants, never a literal — a check pinned to 0.06 or 2.25 tests that nobody edited the harness. Assert the shape of a distribution, not only its total — an aggregate cannot see a missing category. Count how often a new mechanic actually fires in a real run — out-migration once shipped fully tested and fired zero times in 4,002 months, and foreign borrowing had to be told to happen. A quantity in one unit tested against a rule that assumed another is the shape that keeps recurring. A fixture has to cause the condition under test, not stand next to it — fifty-odd sightings now, the latest a fixture that measured an oven's revenue on one of its two products and reported it losing money. When a fix is safe because some case is exactly unchanged, assert that case at zero tolerance — a sector making one good must carry exactly 1.0 of its own cost, and that is a check rather than a comment. A harness cannot check a thing nobody does — the fare was collected from nobody for as long as no fixture had built a bus. When a harness goes red on your change, ask the same question of ten fixtures before believing it is your change — one fixture said the farms broke the currency reform; ten said the model had been carrying a coin-toss for months. A sentinel is a value for the code to compare, never a value to print, and the place it leaks is the screen, which was the one layer with no harness behind it — and the plainer rule beside it: play the game, because fifty-one green harnesses and a ten-fixture sweep did not find a nine-quintillion-dollar unit cost that clicking on Agriculture found in ninety seconds. Assert the premise the whole suite stands on — a month++ left twice at the top of the month passed every harness in the suite, and only the playtest's month numbers gave it away; CalendarCheck asserts one press is one month now. Is the field money? Then it belongs in the reform the day it is written — a rule cheaper than the twenty-five constants of the family before it. A held sector is held in both directions — a planner that could demolish a harness's fixture scrapped six spurs of an over-built railway, and an assertion whose subject is decided by a planner is an assertion about the planner. A fixture says what it lives on — when the founding treasury went from D$2.5B to D$100M, forty-two fixtures in twenty-five harnesses turned out to have been living on it, only ten went red, and three were green because their calendars had quietly run short; each is handed its treasury explicitly now (0.7.10). Compare mechanisms, not counts — a last-bit rounding difference moves an eight-seed count of bank failures by about three and the amount written off by about $60bn (0.7.8).

Decided by dust

The money-constant family — a bound written in absolute money that a currency reform walks past — is past twenty-five sightings, and since 16 September it has two relatives that are not constants at all. The sign of a residue. Four retired households owed minus 2.7e-29 dollars, out of the monthly rebuild handing a cell weight × g / g, which can come back an ulp above weight. The magnitude is not money and nothing reads it as money; one thing reads its sign — investAbroad() asks debt <= 0 to decide who may keep money abroad — so four cells flipped, their paper came home at a tenth a month, the financial account moved by thousands, and the exchange rate, which is every import and export price in the city, moved with it: a city and its reformed twin ran together at 1e-13 for 158 months and were 2–4% apart a decade later. The fix is four clamps and no numbers, because a ratio cannot go stale in a new unit. A zero-crossing test is a threshold, and a threshold on a residue is decided by luck. A money ratio that comes out as a physical quantity. The car affordability test is money over money and the answer is cars, which the road, the market and the trade balance all read; (a/100)/(b/100) is not a/b, so a reformed city wanted an ulp more of a car, bought it somewhere else, and DenominationCheck went red on sixteen assertions. Scale-invariant in arithmetic and not in floating point, and the cure is a grain: a cell buys whole cars, and the shops sell whole baskets. The playtest flags any household position below zero at zero tolerance, every month of every seed, and was seen to fail 216 times before it was trusted.

Two more sightings since. A hair of borrowing is a discrete state (0.6.8): the waterfall clamps a negative remainder, but a positive 1e-21 becomes a debt, and investAbroad() gates on debt <= 0; the first draft of the price at the door re-split a row's bill by its paying heads, and the reform twins parted a decade later over interest 2.36e-21 against 0.0 in one cell. The re-split was dropped rather than touch the waterfall. A ghost household (0.7.2): a cell whose census count had fallen to a residue — 1e-15 households, or a real 0.3 — still held savings and was still being paid, a household to some lines and not to others, and one line divided a credit by it; DenominationCheck went red at a floating-point knife-edge and the trace found it. The cure is a grain again: under half a household a cell is empty, and DenominationCheck looks 202 times over both cities and finds no cell under half a household holding anything. And one in the harness itself: its own two dust thresholds were fixed dollar amounts until 0.7.12, and scale with the currency now.

The tree, and the prose that describes it

The tree is 233 files and 193,097 lines: one flat package plus sectors/, ui/ and tools/. Game.java is the seam every system meets at, 13,550 lines and thirty-seven banner sections, and on 18 September four mechanics that already had the shape of a class left it with their text intact and their behaviour byte-for-byte unchanged — the households' car market as Motoring, the boutiques and the kitchens as LuxuryCounter, who is at risk of offending as Offending, what the city eats as CityBasket — each called from the same line of the month it always was, with every moved getter kept on Game as a delegation so that no caller changed. The currency reform stayed, because it redenominates twenty-two subsystems through Game's own fields and that is the seam doing the seam's job. The investor, the foreign borrowing and demographics are bigger and entangled, and each would be a plan of its own.

Nobody reads those files whole. tools/ reads the sources as text and writes docs/: a code map with one row per file and one page per file listing its banners and every method with its line; dials.md, every static final with its value and the sentence above it, 1,111 of them; month-order.md, the month as a numbered list of statements with where each call goes, which is what section 01 of this page is struck against; and harnesses.md, the 6,268 labelled assertions under their sections and which harnesses name which class — which is how it is known that nothing yet names Automotive or LuxuryRetail. Where finds a member or a banner by name and prints it; SaveDump looks inside a save without loading it. And every batch has a sixth step since 18 September, done by a context that did not make the change: a docs pass that makes every comment, header, index and count agree with the code again, because the agent that changed the code has stopped seeing the prose. Its first run found four stranded javadocs and two false headers; its mechanical half is tools.Stale, which found 84 orphaned javadocs on its first run and is at zero now; the half that needs judgement stays with a reader, and four comments whose numbers disagreed with their constants were decided by Jerus — "the code is correct, the comments are the outliers."

25

Open questions

Known, measured, and written down rather than discovered later. These are decisions, not defects. Forty-three stood here in the last edition. None has been answered since, fifteen have moved and are rewritten, and the eight batches from 0.7.16 to 0.7.23 opened ten more — fifty-three, reconciled against the project's own list on 1 October. Nine questions those batches raised were answered before this edition, and are under the table.

QuestionWhat is measured
The city's size is hypersensitive to a small steady dragSeven hundredths of a point added to the sick rate, with no crime at all, ends two seeds 9% smaller; at a thousandth of crime's strength another ends 16% smaller. It predates every recent batch, and it means an eight-seed median can hide or invent a 10% effect. The first thing to look at next.
A bank with no lending businessHalf answered, and the other half closed by measurement. On the default seed on 22 September the bank lends $13.2bn of $77.9bn deposited and runs at 69% of its capacity, on 28 September it ended with $131M lent of $293bn deposited, at 13%, and on 1 October with $1k lent of $260.5bn, at 6%. Since 0.7.0 its spare cash earns the policy rate at the central bank rather than the world's 2% abroad, which changes what idle money pays and not how much of it there is. Nothing borrows below what the money costs the lender now, and over sixteen seeds failures went 5.0 → 0.0 median, recapitalisation $3.8bn → $0, months run at a loss 44–58% → 3–14%. The intended next step — a real intermediary funding domestic investment out of domestic savings — was measured and dropped: the bank lends 1.87% of its deposits at home and runs at 31–49% of the capacity it already has. The city's savings are on the order of two hundred thousand times what its productive sector wants to borrow. It is demand-limited, not funding-limited, and deposit-gathering cannot change that. The open question is what a city this rich should be able to do with the money.
Cities that empty out, and nothing noticesOver sixteen seeds, nine had a population drawdown of more than half — median worst 57%, deepest 77% — and it is present in the control build too, so it is not new. Every one of those runs reports "Nothing. Every month passed the audit." The money conserves, the books foot, and a city loses three people in four with no finding raised. The audit has no measure of a city shrinking, nor of one running away the other way: on a Lean founding in 0.7.13, seed 0 ended owing $476.6T with 43.9% of its people out of work, and the playtest raised no finding.
Savings that cannot be spentRead off a real screen, slot 3, month 2305, a city of 1,003,081. The 1,361 households in out of work, EI run out are each $390 a month short, have $201,363 put by — 516 months of cover — and have defaulted. The reading is settled and the fix is not made: the draw-down path is wired, and the discharge fires anyway because discharge() tests the wage against subsistence and never looks at savings — a bug in the opposite direction, and why a city with no poor people in it manufactures bankruptcies. It belongs with the supply work. Alongside it: orphan bands three orders of magnitude apart on a 2,300-month steady state. The prisoners' $70 student loan that stood here was the balance they carried in — nothing collects a student loan except from a working family — and since 0.6.9 the freeze is the prisoner's own, no instalment and no interest.
The index's base periodThe basket is weighted from a month two years in, and the shelf-price row below says the shelf floor holds the level at about 0.78 and it cannot fall further — so a run reading near that has been measured partly against a floor rather than against its own start. A rebased version is built and not shipped: DeathRecordCheck goes red on it with the orphan count moving the other way (60→73 as shipped against 59→46 rebased), which is a directional flip and wants explaining before either number is trusted.
The landlock predicate does not clearGame.isPrivateInvestmentLandLocked() reads true at month 12 with 1,974,000 sq ft still free, and is still true at month 300. landBlockedSectors does not appear to be emptied, so the inbox notice "your businesses have nowhere to build" is very likely firing on cities with plenty of ground. The summary deliberately does not use it.
The households' trillionHalf answered, and the half that is left is a supply question. The hoard was diagnosed on 17 September — foreign coupons compounding abroad at 1.58% a year, $2,649bn of them against $584bn of every wage ever paid — and it was given somewhere to go: the coupon paid home, a wealth term in the plan, cars, boutiques and kitchens. Net worth oscillates now instead of climbing (432 → 137 → 128 → 214 months of GDP), and the default seed ends on 22 September with the households holding US$197bn abroad against $73.8bn at home, on 28 September US$356bn against $60.6bn, and on 1 October US$337bn against $51.6bn, where one seed used to hold US$1.1tn against $105bn. What is left: a quarter of every plan is never delivered and the undelivered quarter is the hoard, because the city cannot sell its households what they already want to buy; and MARGINAL_PROPENSITY has no life-cycle behind it — a fifth of discretionary income saved for ever, by everybody, at every age, is the third leak and the only one entirely untouched.
A lockupThe smallest prison is 300 cells for $360M; a city of 15,000 needs about twenty cells. No seed of that size built one, so 2,100–2,900 people were caught over 333 years and every one of them walked — and the cities are ten times the size now: 59,189 caught on the default seed on 22 September, all of them walking, with three stations covering 46% at the end. On 28 September the advisor built jails at last, four of them standing at the end with 344 people inside, and still 35,279 of the 79,592 caught over the run were not held; on 1 October it built none again, and all 69,581 caught walked. A 40-cell lockup, or cells in the headquarters, is what is left to decide.
Rent per cell should follow the doorThe housing match bills landlords by unit size and puts a pensioner in the smallest flat that fits; the household books still charge families and the retired the city's average door. So Senior living alone is the one cell in trouble in every seed — −$46 to −$294 a month for the first decade, $8,970 owed at the ceiling, locked out — and since the sixth band, Elder living alone beside it is the one to watch first when senior care is short. The people outside the families already pay their own door share; families and pensioners do not.
The shelf price still has a floor doing a price's jobRetail's cost-plus shelf price cannot fall below a founding constant. The shelf is thirteen invoices now rather than one, so the cost side is real in a way it was not — but the floor is unchanged, and it is still a constant doing a price's job and still part of why the price index cannot fall past about 0.78. It is also half of why the policy rate cannot reach the index; the other half is the row on the shelf and money below.
Full general care is worth less than it wasA fully served adult used to die at 0.15% a year. They now die at 0.232% — the halved base rate plus a sliver of illness — because the ×3 swing is gone. The halving does not make up for the good end. The lever is the halving, not the ring.
The investment planner ignores every ratioIn most sectors it costs a new building at nameplate capacity — real estate is the exception and the pattern to copy; since 0.7.18 every planner that builds posts asks whether the city could staff them, which is a gate, not a cost. It also under-builds shops, and it has never built a college, so the student machinery is only exercised in a harness — and, since 0.6.9, in a playtest that is told to build schools (-Dplaytest.schools=true).
The supply wallWhy can the shops fill only three quarters of a plan in a city with a hundred billion of unspent demand standing in front of them? Retail's binding constraint is coverage times the operating rate, and the operating rate in the default playtest is about half. On the default seed on 22 September the shops fill 77% of the queue they could serve at all, running at 77%, and the city gets 4.2% of the groceries it asks for, on 28 September 54%, running at 54%, and 2.5%, and on 1 October 56%, running at 57%, and 2.6%; whatever is not delivered is banked, by construction, because the settle has nowhere else to put it. The kitchens are a second door and took median hunger from 36% to 22%. The dearer basket — a rich household buying a dearer basket rather than more of them, since nobody eats twice — is the only consumption item left, and Jerus's call was to take it through the new sectors first.
Who is playingThe playtest's advisor built five wind farms and two cemeteries for 176,000 people over three centuries, not because it ranks constraints badly but because the loop skips six to a hundred and twenty months between looks, averaging fifty-four — deliberately, the length a person actually clicks. So every number the harness has ever reported was measured in a city in crisis from neglect. A second player, -Dplaytest.player=attentive, checks in yearly with the same advice: over eight seeds 418,948 people against 148,479, $2,125M a month against $544M, hunger 18% against 43%, sickness 7.8% against 23.1% — and a labour ceiling the neglected city never reached, at 89% fill. The default is bit-identical to before the flag, so every ensemble in the record stays comparable. And one that cannot be explained: over 600 months the neglected city ends bigger and richer than the attentive one while being sicker and hungrier, and over 4,000 the ordering reverses decisively. Something about the early game. And the stand-in player falls behind on police late in a run: that, through crime and migration, is why the default city ended a few percent smaller on 0.7.11, and in 0.7.14's runs every seed that ended with police coverage of 40% or more had 5 to 19 points more unemployment.
Bounded world demandAn unbounded export market at a fixed floor means the single best export is built for ever and nothing else ever is: 179 Fabrication Works and zero Machine Works over a run, then nineteen Locomotive Works and two hundred and forty Commercial Vehicle Plants the day those could be sold abroad. The narrow fix was to say the world does not buy vans or wagon sets; the general fix touches four sectors and would change every existing city, and it is still its own batch.
Shells, and a banned firmA sector with no plant can keep a dust of debt and a pantry it can neither use nor sell for up to 3,200 months, defaulting on its interest every month — 78–96% of all the defaults for want of cash on 0.7.12, and little money, 1,276 of 1,535 on the default seed on 28 September, and 616 of 663 on 1 October. Nothing winds it up, where a real company in that state is liquidated. And a banned firm with an empty till cannot restock, because the ban shuts the working-capital line the rules keep open for everyone else.
Output does not read its inputsA maker's output does not read the inputs it bought, so holding its inputs to what it can pay for would make goods out of nothing — on 0.7.12's runs it would have skipped $393B of inputs with the same output — and a maker's inputs are bought whole for that reason. A production recipe would be a sixth throttle beside the five ratios (Jerus: "Buy inputs whole, todo").
Where the companies' spare cash goesBy OutwardInvestment's rule, mostly abroad: after 0.7.12's eight default runs the companies held $6.7T abroad against $1.7T in their tills, and 26–32% of the bonds; on the default seed on 28 September they ended with $928bn abroad against $233bn in their tills, and on 1 October with $837bn against $209bn. And the household cells' rebalancing on the book barely fires, $0.47M offered over eight runs, because a cell short of money has usually sold in its waterfall already.
The young bank, and a plant that loses money before interestUnder every failure 0.7.8 left, one sector held 30–73% of the book, and since 0.7.12 concentration is priced rather than capped (answered below). What still fails the bank is the young one, on the $32M of capital its founding branch brings, before month 1,000, when Manufacturing or Automotive goes under; a plant retired at material price can take a sector past the line within months, and on autopilot seed 7 a $44M backstop met a $21M bank. The last change of 0.7.12 took the autopilot's failures from 4 to 9, and that was not traced. The single-industry multiplier the price uses is Basel III's 1.25, set for large financial institutions, and is kept and flagged. Branch openings between the minimum and the target were 35% of the default runs' openings on 0.7.11, and left open the old trap of recapitalising a bank by opening branches; since 0.7.19 a branch opens only for its customers and their fees, and the default city of 1 October opened none past its charter in 4,005 months, but the band itself has not been measured again. The row that stood here, a plant that loses money before interest is still lent for, now meets a price off the curve at the leverage the plant would leave the sector at (0.7.8) and a door shut past the default point (0.7.12); it has not been measured again since 0.7.2.
The default curve's one numberBusinessDebtManager.ASSET_VOLATILITY 0.25, the spread of fortunes among a sector's firms, from the Merton and KMV literature's 20–35% and not tuned to any result: at 0.20 the curve is steeper, fewer defaults under the line and more past it, and at 0.35 flatter. Jerus's to settle.
The bank's target sits at its capBank.MAX_BUFFER binds in every seed: the worst year a bank lives through was 38–109% of its weighted book on 0.7.8's runs, so its target is 16.5% throughout and covers a sixth of it. On the default seed on 28 September the worst year on record was 12.06% of the book, and on 1 October 22.24%, the target 16.5% both times.
The quarter prices the shortfall desk past the default pointThe shortfall desk lends on the month's leverage while the price reads the quarter, which was Jerus's choice, so over 0.7.8's default eight 1,435 of its loans were priced past the default point, 73% of them Luxury Retail in the high month of its restock. Pricing the shortfall desk on the month, or on the lower of the two, would stop it, and it is his call. On 28 September 16 loans were written past the default point over the run, and on 1 October 16 again.
Deposits are not on the bank's booksThe bank counts the city's savings as deposits for its capacity without holding them, the one stated simplification in its header. It is the root of the worst stress finding of 0.7.7 — held at 10%, a recovered bank paid savers its chosen 3.5% over the world's 2%, the households brought US$2.7bn home into a city of about 2,000, and prices ran to 5.64 times founding before they unwound — and the reason the Balance sheet page carries deposits as a memo line. Putting them on the books moves the bank's three statements, the audit's pools, every payment between a household and a business and the central bank's interest on reserves, and it is its own batch (Jerus: "Memo now, own batch later").
The city keeps its bankA resolved bank's stake falls only as it sells new shares (Jerus: "New issues only"), so the city keeps its bank for decades: a median 58.8% twenty-five years after a default resolution, and 91–100% in the autopilot and held setups (0.7.14); the default seed of 1 October, resolved in month 664, still held 65.0% twenty-five years on and 62.2% at the end. A sell-down like the United Kingdom's out of NatWest, by placements, a trading plan and directed buybacks from 2015 to 2025, is Jerus's if he wants one; the player can sell the rescue book by hand. Beside it: the fund never pays for a rescue, by his word, and at a 300% dial all 66 months on advances were resolutions' while the fund held about $500B; it can pass 10% of a company without buying when that company's buybacks shrink the shares under it; and TARP's $25B cap is left out, because nothing converts it into this game's money.
The landlords' test is per buildingSo a landlord whose profit is under its mortgages' principal borrows the difference from the shortfall desk — $1.46B on one seed of 0.7.11 — and the 10% city's insurance losses came from the same place. And a landlord with an empty till can wait on its 15% down payment: 250 months on 0.7.12's seed 5, with households there going without a home for 161 of them.
Size-level slack in the housing retirement ruleA distressed landlord sold 5,800 of 13,272 homes in seven months because the rule asked whether the segment had spare doors and the slack was spread over four sizes. Guarded — nobody sells a door while somebody has nowhere — not fixed. Whether a given set of households fits a given set of doors is a packing question and no comparison of totals answers it.
Every planner assumes the ground is freeThe fields plan off the crop market, the houses off the jobs, the fabricators off the steel price, the railway off the freight nobody is carrying, and none of them can see that they are bidding for the same land. The rule that stopped the farms crowding out housing is a patch on one sector, not a mechanism, and the railway and the assembly plants are the hungriest for ground the game has had. A land market the planners bid into.
Converting for land does not push the currencyThe 0.7.6 brief asked that whatever pressure the exchange puts on the rate when the treasury buys dollars apply to land too, and there is none to apply: a treasury purchase of dollars is the financing item below the line, which the pressure never reads, so buying reserves has never moved the rate and ForeignCheck's section 14 asserts an equality with nothing. Right for reserves, whose dollars stay in the country; arguably wrong for land, which the world sold the city and was paid for in dollars the city had to buy, so a city that spends a decade buying ground sees no weakening for it. The fix is small — count the dollars paid abroad for land as an outflow the pressure reads, for converting or for both — and it is Jerus's call.
A third of meals out, and two businesses that cannot failMOST_MEALS_EATEN_OUT is a third and nothing measures whether that is the right number; on the best seeds a quarter of the city eats out and the kitchens build to the cap. Restaurants finish seven seeds of eight with zero write-downs at 2.00% credit, the profile of Retail and Agriculture, the sectors that have never gone under — a business that cannot fail is not much of a decision. Luxury Retail is the opposite, going under more often than anything but Mining and Materials, its two buildings never sized against a city's luxury budget, and it still has no harness of its own: every bootstrap fault the kitchens hit had already been paid for there, and nothing names Automotive either.
A car costs nothing to keep, and its loan is a bridgeJerus wants a car to cost something to keep, which gives cut back expenses something to cut and makes a financed car a real commitment; the open question is where the money lands — rent reaches Real Estate through a good and a sale, so a motoring bill needs a new good sold by Automotive, or fuel imported from the world. And a car loan with a term, if the credit cycle is meant to be felt rather than merely recorded: today's loan is repaid out of spare cash within a month or two.
The railway's allowed return is a constantA regulated network's allowed return follows the cost of its money; here TARGET_RETURN is 1.2% a month whatever the credit desk charges, and the link runs the other way — dear money keeps the band wide. Alongside it: the railway settles at 38–43% of the lorry rate in the ensemble and has been seen quoting 99% of a lorry, which leaves shippers barely better off; and rail haulage does not reduce a sector's van need, while Construction draws its materials on demand and so reads as moving nothing.
Small orders wait behind large onesBromilow's weights give a big building the bigger crew, and Jerus's rule when he left it was that small orders waiting is accepted (0.7.17). What it looks like to a player: a new city that orders its depots beside a coal plant gives them a fiftieth of the plant's crew, and ForeignCheck's founding sat at about 1,000 people for fifteen years; in his city the lead-time gate refused Industry for 94 sector-months and Materials for 99 behind his 47 care complexes. The first thing to try on the PC. And nothing caps a site's crew at the pace a building could go at alone, Bromilow's K, so since 0.7.22 an order the player sets can hand the city's whole share to its first site.
Unemployment moves twice as muchSince the builders lay off the crews their work does not need (0.7.17), unemployment moves about twice as much from month to month: the 95th percentile of the default eight's monthly move went from 0.81 points to 1.93, and the schools city's from 0.64 to 1.53.
A city short of handsWhen the best-paid fill moves workers at once and the wage walks after them, a city short of hands pays first: Jerus's year-149 city ran a deficit of $9.87B in the first year against $5.10B, and was in surplus from the third (0.7.18). A young town that waits for staff builds late and then fast — MonetaryCheck's town, without its re-cause, opened its Boutique and Diner at month 16 rather than 5 and doubled through the window — and the ensembles' median does not show it. A default city is short of college workers all its life, at a median college fill of 0.57. The 7.5% of unskilled arrivals is Ontario's figure, to be replaced by a Canada-wide one. And Food Processing, whose own banner once measured an 80% floor as wrong for plants of four posts, was held on staffing in all 120 months of his city; whether that is the intended cost is Jerus's. The rule that a band nobody can fill takes no posts is dormant: it binds only for a band with no way in, and anything that closes arrivals to a band in play would wake it (0.7.18).
The builders' tax at its edgesThree consequences of the rules as the law has them (0.7.19). A House mostly bears its tax, being worth more with its plot than the rental rebate's $450,000. An oversupplied city's landlords sell empty buildings, because their rents are rebated and their repairs taxed: the free-tuition city of EducationCheck sold 53 of its 120 Low-Rise; Jerus: "Leave as the law has it". And near the line the Bank tab can say another branch would pay beside a figure under its cost, because the rule counts the charter's exemption and the figures do not.
The city held at 25%The stress city held at a 25% dial is smaller since 0.7.17, with households left with nowhere in every seed, and two of its collapsed seeds reload with differences in the sixth to eighth significant figure that are not traced. Since 0.7.19 it collapses, 428 people at the median against 8,168, because it had lived on the deposit cap's strain and the window, both removed on purpose. Jerus: "Let it fail"; it stays a stress case whose findings are watched, not a city required to recover. Its drift in the paper and the bonds predates 0.7.19 and is not fixed.
A demolition can payThe 0.7.8 rule sells a demolished building's material back at the day's market price, so the fixture's school brought $12.2M of material against a $1.05M demolition (0.7.22), where real demolition recovers far less than new material costs: lower it, or keep it as the 0.7.8 rule wanted? Beside it, buying out the builders' own depots pays a large business loss and takes their capacity with it; FAR's settlement costs and Ontario's injurious affection are left out by choice, having no figure in the model; and the player's own demolitions are drawn in the red the game keeps for failures. Jerus's calls.
What the short lines lostText in three layers moved the long paragraphs behind an (i), and the mockups' popover wording dropped some clauses: Construction's "the only sector in the city with a liability of that shape", the tax dial's "every sector's and every band's move rides whichever base is its own", and the Government's "a bridge that hides its own gap is not a bridge", kept where there are movements (0.7.21). Whether any comes back is Jerus's call; this page could carry them. The founding panel's name field shows no focus ring, only the caret.
A young city's growth swingsThe header's GDP tile reads real growth over twelve months from month 24, and a young city's swings hard — minus 66% at month 30, plus 10% at month 70 — because its first year's output is mostly its own orders (0.7.21). The tile shows the model's figure; its tooltip could say why.
The decision log's edgesThe log has no cap and is saved whole, and the playtest's advisor adds a line for each tax or paper call; the advisor's own steps of the policy rate are not in it, because it moves the rate by a setter the log does not watch; full screen covers the toasts and the inbox, by a call made while Jerus slept; and a wrapped legend can push the overview strip under the window's edge in full screen (0.7.23).
Four small things about the CSVLeft for Jerus by 0.7.16: an episodes CSV, not built, because the list is short and its worst figure is in a different unit for each kind of episode; full precision, not taken, so that the CSV and the text always agree; a friendlier line when a CSV is open in Excel and cannot be written; and an Excel set to a comma decimal, which opens a comma CSV as one column. English-Canadian Windows is fine.
What no harness looks atThe sector pages are the only screen whose text a harness reads. The city panel, the People screen, the Policy pages and City History are as right as somebody's last look at them; the Infrastructure tab shipped without anyone looking at it and its layout pass is owed — a twelfth tab crowding the rail on a short window, five-column tables wider than the statement column; and the four opening statement lines build nodes directly rather than lines a check can read. The screens cannot run in the cloud, so they are checked by eye on the PC. What has been seen there: 0.7.10's founding screen, which Jerus checked on 24 September; every tab once in a new city, and Build, People, History and the inbox in his city of a million, on the play-through of 0.7.19 on 30 September, which found the bugs 0.7.20 fixed and saw the tax dial's staging, the land office's cards and Buy the next N, the Needs you rows, the order line, the hub pages, the debt bar and the old History chart's hover values working; and 0.7.20's own fixes, checked the same night, bar three things the remote session could not reach — Esc, the header's price tooltip and a toast. The play-through opened the tabs without walking each batch's list, so those lists still stand, bar what has been drawn anew since: the strip, which the header replaced in 0.7.21, and the Reports page's chart, which 0.7.23 replaced. Still to look at: the Money page and the monetary page with the target's, the holdings' and the ceiling's ladders, the sector list's sparklines and the tax pages' own bases (0.7.4); Enter and Backspace on a build page and City History's pins (0.7.5); every ladder, the school rows, the GDP layers and the land office's two ways to pay (0.7.6); prime and the savers' share where the bank's premium used to show — on the Summary, the Finances rate page, the sector page and the Policy page (0.7.7); the Bank tab, rebuilt and grown since (0.7.7 to 0.7.9, 0.7.11, 0.7.13, 0.7.14, 0.7.19); the Real Estate screen's mortgages and the Government tab's premiums and claims (0.7.11); the bond market, the Sector page's owners' book, a household's bonds and the interim financing (0.7.12); the land office in km², its funding page and the rollover's block (0.7.13); the city's fund, the rescue setting, the preferred offer and the Insane card (0.7.14); the target's ladder to 20%, the holdings ladder to 100%, the rollover's central bank lines and the skip report's advances (0.7.15); the year book's message and its CSVs in Excel (0.7.16); Construction's crews kept on, a sector's laid-off posts, Real Estate's months of work and a city that orders its depots beside a coal plant (0.7.17); every planner's staffing line and the People screen's one-market note (0.7.18); the build card's price row, the Construction page's escalation, the Bank tab's branches by their customers, the Schools page on the fixed grant and the Luxury page's buyers at its price (0.7.19); Esc with a tooltip showing (0.7.20); and all of 0.7.21 to 0.7.23 — the palette and the fonts, the rail, the header's six tiles at 1,920 and 1,366 pixels and a tile's click, the main menu, the founding panel and the (i) popovers (0.7.21); the construction page's three tabs, its dialogs and the cold-start menus (0.7.22); and City History's new chart, its lanes, its cards and full screen, and the years under the other charts (0.7.23). BankCheck asserts the Bank tab's arithmetic and ChartCheck the chart's, not their drawing, and every screen but the Bank tab snaps an opened line shut on the month's redraw. Education's cost and the transit payroll are outside G, which healthcare's and the police's are not.
Two small calls from 0.7.4Whether the inflation target should be staged and applied with the rate, rather than taking effect at once as the ceiling does; and whether the bank should have its own row on the profit tax page, since it is taxed at retail's rate and nothing on the screen says so.
The term premium is Jerus's to settleDebtManager.TERM_PREMIUM_10Y … 50Y at 0.50, 0.90, 1.15, 1.35 and 1.50 points were set to have a curve at all, and the shape — linear from a year to ten, linear between the entries, flat past fifty — is the implementer's reading of the brief. The world's curve reads the same table, so the numbers price dollar paper too.
The "spread is gone" sale can hardly ever fireThe households' yield is read off today's curve, which sits on the dial, and the deposit rate is, since 0.7.7, the share of the dial the bank chooses, so the paper nearly always out-yields deposits and the sale does not happen in a real run; HoldersCheck exercises it by handing it a zero yield, a fixture standing next to its condition. And 0.7.2 saw it fire anyway: in BankCheck's settle month the deposit rate rose past the paper's yield, 6.825% against 6.726%, and the desk bought $1,080k back. Whether the rule should read the coupon locked in at issue is Jerus's.
The currency's new numbers are untunedForeignAccounts.RATE_PULL 4 and CapitalFlows.MAX_SPREAD .25 are both marked in the source as Jerus's numbers to settle. Four was chosen because six gave the loop a gain of 1.44 and sent one seed to its guard; it buys two thirds of the support a credible real rate used to buy. And the rate term acts from month one, before the trade pressure is allowed to, which predates the batch.
The exchange rate goes NaN in a city with no tradeA founding left without the advisor goes NaN at month 26, on 0.7.2 and 0.7.3 alike: pressure(), absorption() and realRateDifferential() read NaN first, and the currency's guards pass it through, because a clamp hands a NaN straight on. Found by the 0.7.3 batch and not traced to the root.
Should the defence answer only a crisis?The founders' billion went on structural deficits, not crises (0.7.2): every default seed sold more than half of it and six all but 1%, while the playtest's advisor bought it back with the treasury's cash — one seed sold US$1.29B of a US$1B vault, and held at a 50% dial US$625B. On the default seed on 22 September half of it was gone by month 726. Since 0.7.10 the founders leave US$25M, and on 28 September half of that was gone by month 753, on 1 October by month 40. A defence that waited for a run would need a rule for what a run is; the hot money's panic, a fragile position and a shock, is the nearest the model has.
Interest on reserves compounds without limit at a held extreme rateHeld at 30% or 50%, M0 reaches 108 to 3×1014 $B while prices stay near one: spare cash earning the rate in new money that becomes spare cash, and the households' savings, earning it too, reach 107 to 1014 years of income. And the central bank's equity ended below zero on every default seed measured through 0.7.3, mostly the loss carried from paying interest on reserves: minus $1.1B to minus $3.3B on 0.7.2's eight, minus $2.39B on the default seed on 22 September. On 28 September, at a dial whose median over the run was 0.44%, that seed's ended at $32.1M — the vault it counts as its asset, less $1.48M of loss carried — and on 1 October, at a median of 0.46%, at $13.9M, less $4.94M.
Nothing limits the city's own paper at homeThe price of the city's paper stops at the dial plus ten points and the term premium; the bank buys whatever the households leave, funded at a window with no cap; debt service is a promise, paid past the advances ceiling; and only abroad does a window shut. So a city can compound its paper without limit: Lean seed 0, rolling twenty-year paper for cash, ended owing $476.6T (0.7.13); held at 25% the city borrows from its bank to buy the bank's preferred, $962T of it with the advances peaking at $20.4 quadrillion, which Jerus kept ("Keep as built"), and five of eight Insane seeds spiral on the same missing limit (0.7.14); and the holdings dial at 100% makes the central bank the buyer when nobody else is without limiting what the city sells (0.7.15). A market limit — a buyer's refusal, or a debt-service rule at home — is Jerus's to design.
The shelf is priced by coverage, not by moneyThe structural reason the rate cannot reach the index. Retail's shelf sits at its floor or marks up against the shops' coverage — what it can serve, not what is wanted — so a tenth less want moves no price, and food is four fifths of the index; the counter and the table, where discretionary money goes, are not in it at all. A shelf priced by scarcity against money is the next design, and MonetaryCheck's point of transmission is still mostly the currency's until it exists.
The dear-care city is hungrierHealthCheck's red line (0.7.12): read over a year, the city charging fifteen times the founding fees was 8.4 points hungrier than the free one on 28 September, against a tolerance of five; 77 points on 0.7.17's tree and 39 on 0.7.18's, and 40.0 on 1 October, 51.1% against 11.1%. Which change of 0.7.17 widened it is not traced. Of its hungry as 0.7.12 traced them, a quarter were priced out of care and a fifth billed care their household row had already paid for — the documented shortcut in HouseholdBalance — and nearly half had money for food after their fixed bills and still planned less, which is untraced and the first thing to trace. It has a batch of its own.
OnboardingThe game never states the goal or teaches its causal order — land, housing, people, jobs, industry. Every confusing thing a new player hits is that order being violated. Still the only thing that gates shipping.
Answered since the last edition

When the first inflation reading should come — about month 50 in a Standard city, because the basket is fixed after 24 months of shopping and the rate needs a year of readings after that. Jerus: "Keep the countdown": the header reads rate in ~N mo until then, and no model figure moved (0.7.20). The price card's "against what it cost at founding", whose 1.00 is the month the basket was fixed: "Leave it". The other sector pages' money form: "With 0.7.21's text cut", and every sector page prints the short form since 0.7.21. BuildMenuCheck's 73 failures, since 0.7.19, because its price check predated the builders' sales tax: "Update to the taxed price"; it checks the taxed price from the rule's own inputs, passes 73 of 73 and runs in the cloud gate (0.7.21). Whether the city held at 25% must live, after 0.7.19 removed the deposit cap's strain and the window it lived on: "Let it fail" (the bank). The bank's branch built 29 posts without the staffing test (0.7.18): it asks it since 0.7.19, like every other planner. The five longest paragraphs on screen are a line and an (i) since 0.7.22. Settings and Load on a cold start drew over the empty start-up world's header and panels in 0.7.21, and are over the menu's backdrop since 0.7.22. City History's lines in red, amber and green waited for the chart's rebuild, and since 0.7.23 no chart draws a series in a verdict colour.

Answered in the editions before

Who gets 45% — the constant and the deposit quote's cap are gone: since 0.7.7 the bank chooses what it passes on, 0.35 of the dial when flush and toward 0.90 at the window, never more than its margin after costs or the savers' share of it. Who absorbs a failed bank — the city, since 0.7.14: every common share passes to it and the old owners get nothing, and it pays the hole and the capital to reopen as a promise, its central bank advancing what the treasury lacks. Nothing is declared as coming from outside any more, and the audit's old line for it reads nothing; whether that line goes is Jerus's. A concentration limit on the bank — answered by a price, not a limit (0.7.12): Basel's IRB capital, with a correlation that rises with the book's concentration, allocated to each sector and into its rate, took the default setup's failures from 115 to 1. Syndication past Basel's large-exposure limit was tried on the way and deleted (0.7.8); what is left is the young bank, above. The bubble — the dealer whose quote climbed with the demand it could not fill is gone (0.7.12). A price is the last trade on a book where nobody is obliged to sell, and on the default seed on 28 September the median last trade stood at 0.99 of fair value and the ninetieth percentile at 1.42; whether a dealer should bid at a quote it does not believe in went with the dealer.

The treasury rolled its whole debt in one bill — a single six-month emergency note it repaid with money it did not have and wrote again, for ever. The note is retired: since 0.7.0 the central bank advances the gap at the policy rate, up to a ceiling the player sets, and past it the promises are paid and the rest owed. Term loans are issued at five benchmark maturities on a curve. The rest of that row — long bonds dominated past fifteen years, and rounded to $100M — was not re-measured; the rounding is at least stated on the borrow page now. The overdraft had no floor — a founding village given a university went to NaN by month 1,632 — and the advances ceiling is the floor. The city borrowed below its own central bank's rate, and the constant that did it is deleted. The bank never paid for the city's paper, and it does now. Wages were indexed a third above the price level they chase — closed without a fix, because it does not reproduce: LabourCheck holds the wage index to the published price index through 240 months of steady inflation, a reform and a reload, and on all eight seeds every one of 144 checkpoints sits at the level the two-year lag implies, wages over the index between 0.84 and 1.12. The tuition table was calibrated against the wages before the rebalance, and the price of a place is the player's dial now: at ×3 the trap the header describes is back, at ×1 it is quiet, and the balance call is at the player's hand.

Is the city too rich against its food? — answered, and the answer was not the food. Measured against Canada the wholesale price was sane and the shops' markup was sane, but the poorest cell held about three times what an average Canadian does per head, and Jerus deferred the question until households carried transport and luxury costs, which they now do. The households looked rich against a basket with two things in it because they were rich, for a reason nothing on the page had measured: their savings abroad compounded at the world's 2% with the coupon rolled where it was earned, so net worth climbed to five thousand months of GDP and every household was past satiation on a shelf that was the only thing it could buy. The wealth term, the coupon paid home, cars, boutiques and kitchens gave the money somewhere to go, and the honest hunger measure then showed the shops delivering a fraction of what was asked — the food share was never the question; the supply wall is. The transit loss — $5.4M a month at the default fare, carried as a balance problem — was a ride charged once a month; forty journeys a month and it is gone. The savings that cannot be spent have their reading: the discharge tests the wage and never the savings. The fare was collected from nobody, and is a fee on every rider's statement now. Nothing about transport was on a screen — it is a tab with four pages.

Materials were priced nine times too low — $18,000 a unit now, every template re-derived, and it found an import bill that had never been paid. Retail's shelf price was the last unpriced half — done; hunger sat at 0–1% and the shops delivered 94–96% of what they planned, on a hunger measure that was later found to be reading the shops' own denominator, so those two figures were never the city's (see the supply wall above). Unemployment — 12.8–15.5% at the end of eight seeds, against 41.5% when it was the largest open problem.

The two-hundred-year plateau — answered by giving the city customers who do not live in it. A city whose every buyer is one of its own people can only grow as fast as it grows; with business services and manufacturing selling abroad, the median run went from 16,834 people with six seeds in sixteen ever taking off, to 127,651 with sixteen of sixteen, and the price level from 0.799 to 0.975. Nothing borrows below cost — one rule closed the bond floor, the business desk and the carry trade at once. The mills made food out of nothing — they buy crops now, and the plant's return fell from 6.2% of build cost a month to about 3.9%.

A laid-off parent orphaned their children — answered. The children of an adult who leaves work now go with them, at the city's own rate, and live on whatever that household lives on. Worst orphan month on the probe city 2,093.7 → 12.4; child deaths over one 4,000-month seed 55,546 → 47,503. One good called Food fed everybody — the shelf is thirteen foods, chosen by income, and the good called Food has been retired behind them. A line that made two things was charged for both twice — the sector cost methods divided a whole line's payroll, power and inputs by one good's output, which made an oven with two products produce nothing at all, and had been quietly over-costing business services and manufacturing since the day each shipped. Joint costs are split by relative sales value now, and a sector making one good is bit-for-bit what it was.

Judge nothing by a single playtest

Four thousand months of a chaotic trajectory diverge on the first differing decision. The same change measured +18% on one land seed and −28% on another, and a perturbation of one part in a million moves the long-run size of the city by 10%. Vary the seed, and measure the symptom rather than the end state.
